Twelve years had passed since I had watched the last slant of twilight slide across the old cemetery at Richmond and had seen my great-uncle laid away from the commerce of men in that singular mausoleum of his own devising. There were evenings—thick with August heat and the rasp of cicadas—when the very air above those stones seemed to hold its breath, as though the city itself listened for a sound it could no longer name.

In those years I had turned his instructions over and over like a cook worrying a recipe that will not yield its secret: the insistence upon an open casket, the heavy, old-fashioned mechanism of bolts and levers built so that the vault’s tremendous door could be fastened and unfastened from within, and from within only. It was an arrangement at once domestic and uncanny—like setting a supper table for a guest who has already been mourned, yet leaving the latch unbarred because the household remembers older customs than grief allows.

And always, beneath my puzzling, lay the manuscript he had left me: the handwriting of a man who spoke of Mars—Barsoom—as if it were a neighbor’s farm beyond the river, and of miracles as if they were chores. He was a man who remembered no childhood, who could not even offer the blurriest guess at his own age; always young in limb and face, and yet he had once dandled my grandfather’s great-grandfather upon his knee. He had spent ten years upon that dying planet beneath two moons, had fought for the green men and against them, for the red men and against them, and had won Dejah Thoris of Helium for his wife. For nearly ten years he had been a prince in the house of Tardos Mors, Jeddak of Helium—titles that sounded, on Earth, like words from a language that our mouths could shape but our minds could not truly taste.

When his body was found later upon the bluff above his cottage on the Hudson, the world had accepted the simple, blunt vocabulary of death; but my own thoughts would not stay obedient. Had he gone again to the dead sea bottoms? Had he returned to Barsoom in time—had he thrown open the frowning portals of that mighty atmosphere plant and saved the countless millions who were choking in thin air on that far-gone day before he was flung back across forty-eight million miles of void to Earth? Had he found his black-haired Princess in the royal gardens, and the slender son he had dreamed of standing there among the flowers, waiting with the grave patience of Barsoom? Or had he come too late, and so walked again into a living death upon a dead world? Or—simplest and hardest—was he merely dead, as the stones said, never to return to either his mother Earth or his beloved Mars?

So I drifted in those speculations on a sultry August evening when old Ben—my servant, steady-handed and quiet-footed—entered with a telegram. The house had its usual sounds: the faint tick of a clock, the distant roll of a wagon over cobbles, the low hush of summer wind in leaves. Even these small noises seemed suddenly precious to me, as though I were learning to listen the way a man listens when he fears silence.

I tore open the yellow paper and read:

“Meet me to-morrow hotel Raleigh Richmond.
JOHN CARTER”

For a moment the words had the same effect as the first sharp scent of vinegar when a lid is lifted from a jar you thought sealed forever—an undeniable proof that something within has been alive all along.

Early the next morning I took the first train for Richmond. The journey felt both swift and endless: the clatter of wheels like an insistent drumbeat, the shrill cry of the whistle cutting through the countryside, each station a brief breath. I found myself noting, without meaning to, how the modern world had grown louder in the twelve years since his tomb had closed—more iron, more steam, more harsh advertisement of motion. The noise seemed to press upon the mind, flattening thought, as if it were the language of a place that had forgotten the softer dialects of wind and water.

Within two hours I was being ushered into a room at the Hotel Raleigh.

He rose as I entered.

The same straight, clean-limbed fighting-man of thirty stood before me, as though time had been something he had declined to taste. His keen gray eyes were undimmed. Only the lines of iron character and determination—those deep-set marks that seemed less like age than like the grain in seasoned wood—remained as I remembered them from nearly thirty-five years before. He smiled, and it was the old-time cordial smile of welcome that made the room, for an instant, feel like a kitchen with a fire on and bread in the oven.

“Well, nephew,” he said, “do you feel as though you were seeing a ghost, or suffering from the effects of too many of Uncle Ben’s juleps?”

“Juleps, I reckon,” I answered, because my tongue reached for the nearest homely word to steady itself; “for I certainly feel mighty good. But maybe it’s just the sight of you again that affects me. You have been back to Mars? Tell me. And Dejah Thoris? You found her well and awaiting you?”

His gaze shifted, not away from me, but as though it traveled briefly through distance—through thin, cold spaces that our Earth language names too simply. When he spoke again, the certainty in his voice was like a knife laid flat upon a board: controlled, familiar, intended for work.

“Yes, I have been to Barsoom again,” he said, “and—but it’s a long story, too long to tell in the limited time I have before I must return. I have learned the secret, nephew, and I may traverse the trackless void at my will, coming and going between the countless planets as I list; but my heart is always in Barsoom, and while it is there in the keeping of my Martian Princess, I doubt that I shall ever again leave the dying world that is my life.”

He said it without ornament, yet the words carried a weight our speech can indicate but not translate. In them I heard, oddly, the notion of home not as a place on a map but as a cadence—the particular way a land sounds at night, the way its silence fits the ear. Perhaps that is why his longing struck me so sharply: I had begun, in those years, to suspect that a man can be exiled by sound as surely as by border.

“I have come now,” he continued, “because my affection for you prompted me to see you once more before you pass over for ever into that other life that I shall never know, and which though I have died thrice and shall die again to-night, as you know death, I am as unable to fathom as are you.

“Even the wise and mysterious therns of Barsoom—that ancient cult which for countless ages has been credited with holding the secret of life and death in their impregnable fastnesses upon the hither slopes of the Mountains of Otz—are as ignorant as we. I have proved it, though I near lost my life in the doing of it; but you shall read it all in the notes I have been making during the last three months that I have been back upon Earth.”

As he spoke he laid his hand upon a swelling portfolio that rested on the table at his elbow. The leather looked newly handled; the corners were softened by use, as if the thing had traveled in saddle-bags and been opened by lamplight more often than by day. It struck me then that his wonders were not presented as parlor tricks; he had recorded them like a careful steward recording stores—measured, exact, with a soldier’s discipline and a farmer’s respect for what must be remembered if the household is to survive.

“I know that you are interested,” he said, “and that you believe, and I know that the world, too, is interested, though they will not believe for many years; yes, for many ages, since they cannot understand. Earth men have not yet progressed to a point where they can comprehend the things that I have written in those notes.

“Give them what you wish of it, what you think will not harm them, but do not feel aggrieved if they laugh at you.”

There was in that warning an understanding of power—not the kind that draws blood, but the kind that decides whose words become “truth” and whose are dismissed as quaint dialect, old wives’ talk, savagery. I thought of how quickly a dominant lexicon can strip meaning from a thing by renaming it, and how easily men, made deaf by the din of their own inventions, mock what they cannot hear.

We spoke a little longer—too little, and yet enough to leave me with the taste of separation already on my tongue. The day passed with the bright, indifferent noises of the city outside the windows: carts, footsteps, shouted greetings, the bell of a streetcar. Under it all I seemed to hear another sound—thin, far, persistent—like the whisper of a wind that had crossed a dead sea.

That night I walked down to the cemetery with him.

The air was heavy and warm, and the insects sang as if they were the only creatures left who remembered the old music of the world. The city’s clamor fell away behind us, muffled by distance and stone. Here the soundscape was older: leaves shifting, a low owl call, the soft grind of our shoes on gravel. It felt as though we had stepped into a pocket of quiet where even time moved with a different gait.

At the door of his vault he turned and took my hand in his hard, familiar grip.

“Good-bye, nephew,” he said. “I may never see you again, for I doubt that I can ever bring myself to leave my wife and boy while they live, and the span of life upon Barsoom is often more than a thousand years.”

He released my hand and entered the vault.

The great door swung slowly to, with the patient inevitability of a barn door closing at dusk. The ponderous bolts grated into place—metal on metal, a sound as final as a period. The lock clicked, small but absolute.

I stood there listening until even the echo of that click had been swallowed by the night.

I have never seen Captain John Carter, of Virginia, since.

But here is the story of his return to Mars on that other occasion, as I have gleaned it from the great mass of notes which he left for me upon the table of his room in the hotel at Richmond. There is much which I have left out; much which I have not dared to tell; but you will find the story of his second search for Dejah Thoris, Princess of Helium, even more remarkable than was his first manuscript which I gave to an unbelieving world a short time since, and through which we followed the fighting Virginian across dead sea bottoms under the moons of Mars.

E. R. B.

I stood on the bluff above my cottage while March tightened its cold fingers around the Hudson, and the river below lay long and grey and hushed, like broth gone thin and forgotten on the back of a stove. The night had its own kitchen-sounds: ice ticking along the shore, a far train worrying the darkness with its iron whine, the wind combing dead reeds into a dry susurrus. Between those honest noises—water, wood, breath—there intruded the city’s distant clamor, a dull metallic grinding that made the whole air feel bruised. I had learned in ten years of waiting that such sound is not merely heard; it settles in the bones and seasons the mind, for good or ill. Tonight it only sharpened my hunger.

Not for food—though the body always asks its due—but for Mars.

I lifted my arms toward that ruddy point of light, the war-god’s eye, and prayed as I had prayed a thousand times: not with eloquent words, but with the plain insistence of a man begging the universe to return what it had once set upon his table and then snatched away. Ten years I had stood here, in one language and then another, trying to name what I missed. There are losses the dominant tongue can only flatten into a single convenient word—love, exile, longing—when the truth is a whole pantry of scents and textures that refuse translation: the warm spice of Martian air, the copper tang of its dust, the particular way a voice sounds under two moons.

Tonight the old pull answered.

A qualm swept through me—sudden, sour, rising from the belly as if some unseen hand had jerked the world sideways. My knees failed. I pitched forward, and for an instant the edge of the bluff was a knife beneath my face.

The nausea snapped into a crystal clarity that brought with it a memory I had tried, uselessly, to bury: that ghostly Arizona cave; the dark that had a weight; the thin, horrible rustle of something that did not belong among men. My muscles refused me again. I lay pinned, awake inside my own skin, hearing—here, absurdly, above the placid Hudson—the echo of those old moans.

Then I made the same superhuman effort, not with limbs but with will, as though I could tear myself free by sheer refusal to submit. There came the sharp click, like a taut wire parting, and I stood naked and free beside the staring shell that had been John Carter a heartbeat before.

I did not linger over that empty thing. My eyes went back to Mars. My hands rose. I waited.

The answer came like thought itself—swift, soundless, absolute. Cold and black wrapped me, an unthinkable chill that had no wind and no mercy, and then the darkness broke and sunlight poured through a tiny opening in a living dome.

I opened my eyes on red turf, cropped close as if by careful shears. The grass—if grass it was—held a sharp, mineral-green scent that pricked the nose like crushed stems and clean iron. Above me towered trunks so immense that the mind balked at their scale. Their surfaces shone as if polished by a million patient hands: ebony-black here, china-white there, and beyond, stems that were azure, scarlet, yellow, purple—colors with the intensity of spices ground fresh, not dulled by time. Blossoms clustered thickly, and the air was heavy with their perfume: sweet, resinous, almost edible, like nectar warmed on stone.

Birds—winged things—flickered among the branches, bright as tossed fruit. They were voiceless. Their silence unsettled me more than any cry would have. A forest should speak: the click of beaks, the argumentative chatter, the rhythm of life. Here, the only sound was the soft stir of leaves far overhead and my own breathing. Even the lack of song felt like a kind of theft, as though some ancient dialect of nature had died here and left only gesture behind.

Through the trees I saw something that made my heart stumble: an open sea, blue and shimmering under the brazen sun.

For an instant terror seized me that I had been flung to some alien world, a cruel miscast of fate. What guide had I through that trackless void? What certainty that the god I begged had heard me correctly? The mind scrabbled for familiar signs the way a cook scrabbles for the right herb in a dark pantry.

I rose—and immediately made the same ridiculous blunder I had made the first time I had awakened on Mars. The lesser gravity and thin air offered so little resistance that the simple act of standing launched me several feet upward. I came down hard, face-first, in the brilliant turf, the red blades springing back under me like resilient chaff.

The smell of the crushed vegetation rose quick and sharp, a green bitterness with a faint sweetness, and it steadied me. Mars or not, this was no Earth. I laughed aloud at my own forgetfulness, and when I rose again I did it with care, mastering once more the art of measuring effort to this lighter world.

I walked downslope toward the glint of water. The land had the look of deliberate husbandry. The sward was kept like an old English lawn; the trees were pruned to a uniform height of perhaps fifteen feet above the ground before their great crowns began, so that the forest became a high-ceiled chamber, a cultivated hall. Whoever tended this place understood the discipline of growth—how to cut back a living thing to make it flourish in the shape one wants. It was civilization’s signature, as unmistakable as bread baked daily or a hearth kept clean.

And yet the silence persisted, a careful silence, like a kitchen where knives are sharp and voices restrained.

As I neared the forest’s edge the scent changed. Salt threaded the air, and beneath it came the cool wet smell of a great river. Then the trees opened and I stepped from shadow into light and saw the meadow spread wide before the sea.

To my left the ocean ran to the horizon. Before me its far shore was only a faint line. To my right a mighty river flowed between scarlet banks, broad and majestic, sliding toward the sea with the calm confidence of something that had done this for ages. Upstream, perpendicular bluffs rose, and it seemed as though the river issued from their base like poured metal.

It should have been a scene to feed the eyes and soothe the mind.

Instead, my attention was torn away by movement: a score of figures in the meadow near the riverbank, shambling and feeding. At a distance they were manlike; closer they were a blasphemy stitched together from familiar parts.

The largest stood ten or twelve feet high when erect, with torsos and legs proportioned like a man’s. But their arms were short and moved in sinuous undulations, like trunk or serpent, boneless in appearance. They ran those odd hands over the turf as they went, as though feeling for something hidden beneath the close-cropped growth.

I slipped behind the bole of an enormous tree and watched.

One wandered nearer, and I saw it plainly. Its body was hairless and a ghoulish blue, except for a broad white band around a single protruding eye. That eye was all dead white—no pupil, no iris, no hint of mind behind it, just a blank staring disk. In the center of its face yawned a ragged circular hole, inflamed and wet, like a fresh bullet wound before the blood comes. Below it, the face went smooth and blank to the chin. No mouth.

The head—everything but that empty face—was capped with a tangled mass of jet-black hair eight or ten inches long. Each “hair” was as thick as an angleworm. As the creature’s scalp muscles shifted, the mass writhed, each strand seeming to move with its own intent, crawling and coiling around the fearsome visage. The sight turned the stomach. The imagination insisted on giving each strand an ear, an attention, a hunger.

Its feet were human in shape but monstrous in size, flat, broad, three feet from heel to toe. Behind it dragged a massive tail six feet long, round at the base, tapering to a flat blade that trailed at a right angle to the ground.

Then I understood the motion of its hands. It fed.

With razorlike talons it cropped the tender red vegetation and, in a gesture both intimate and obscene, sucked it into two mouths set in the palms of its hands. The arms were not merely arms but throats, pulsing as the stuff was drawn inward. The act had the methodical steadiness of grazing animals, yet it felt like a parody of human eating—hands made into hunger, the face denied even the dignity of a mouth.

And from each armpit dangled a tiny replica of the adult creature, each perhaps six inches long, suspended by a stem that grew from the crown of its head to the parent’s body. Some adults bore them, some did not. The small ones varied from tight buds no larger than an inch to perfectly formed little horrors ten or twelve inches in length. Others of similar size fed freely among the herd, and the whole group graded upward in size to the great adults.

I had faced green warriors and great white apes; I had seen death deal its brutal, honest work. This was something else—life arranged in a manner that mocked the categories by which men remember the world.

Still, fear is a practical thing. They did not look built for fighting. I was naked and unarmed, but curiosity—rash, familiar—rose in me. I found myself on the verge of stepping out to see how they would react to a man.

The decision died in my throat.

A shrieking wail split the air from the direction of the bluffs. It was not a natural cry. It had the harsh insistence of a signal, a sound made to be obeyed. The herd froze as one, every white eye turning toward the cliffs. At the same instant, every wormlike “hair” upon their heads rose stiffly upright, like a thousand antennae listening.

In that motion lay the explanation: those writhing strands were ears—countless ears—the last remnant of some strange evolution from an original Tree of Life. The thought came with a chill. A creature that hears through a thousand living filaments lives in a different acoustic world than any man. It is tuned to whispers. It feeds on sound the way it feeds on grass.

All eyes turned toward one of their number, larger than the rest—clearly the leader. A purring sound issued from the mouth in the palm of one hand. Then it sprang, and the herd followed, bounding toward the bluffs with leaps of twenty or thirty feet, like grotesque kangaroos.

They were fleeing—or going to kill. The wail had summoned them.

I followed.

It was madness for a naked man to chase monsters across an open meadow, but the urgency of that cry, and the way it had commanded them, hooked me. In this lighter gravity my own leaps were prodigious; Earth muscles, given Martian conditions, turn a man into a projectile. I crossed the meadow in long bounds, the wind of my passing stirring the cropped red blades.

Near the cliffs the ground was littered with great boulders fallen from the crags above. Using these as cover, I came close before the scene broke upon me.

The plant men—there was no other name—had surrounded a small party of green Martians: five or six of them, men and women. The sight of them snapped certainty into place. This was Barsoom. The sea and forest might be strange, but the green warriors were unmistakable: towering males with gleaming tusks thrusting up from massive lower jaws; eyes set laterally so they could look forward and back without turning the head; antennae-like ears rising from the forehead; the extra pair of arms between shoulder and hip. Their glossy green hides and metal ornaments marked different hordes.

Different hordes. That puzzled me even as dread rose, because the green Martians war eternally among themselves. Only once had I seen them gathered across tribal lines—when Tars Tarkas had marshaled the great host to march upon Zodanga for Dejah Thoris. Yet here they stood together, back to back, as if necessity had forged a brief, brittle alliance.

They held longswords and daggers. No firearms. Against these plant men, that absence was a death sentence.

The leader of the plant men charged.

It came within a dozen feet, then sprang up and over them, as though to pass directly above their heads. Its tail rose high to one side, and as it flew over, it swept that blade down in one terrible arc.

The blow crushed a green warrior’s skull like an eggshell.

The herd began to circle the remaining Martians with bewildering speed, their leaps long and erratic, their purring screeches designed to confuse and terrify. Two more plant men leaped simultaneously from either side, tails swinging.

Two more green Martians fell.

Now only one warrior and two females remained, and the plant men tightened their ring. The lone warrior’s stance was magnificent, the old courage of his race burning bright even when all else fails. When the next two charged, he was ready. He swung his great longsword up and met one hurtling bulk with a clean cut that clove it from chin to groin.

But the other’s tail swept down and crushed both females where they stood.

The last green warrior saw the end and did not wait to be penned. When he perceived the entire herd charging as one body, he rushed to meet them, swinging that sword in the ferocious figure-eight I had seen only the greatest warriors achieve. He cut a path through them and broke for the forest, seeking cover where their aerial blows would be harder to deliver.

They overtook him before he was halfway.

He backed against a boulder, panting, sword up, while the herd hissed and screeched and bounded about him, balked only for an instant by the stone at his back.

Admiration swelled in me until it became action. I have never been able to watch courage die unaided. Acting on impulse, with a plan formed in the heartbeat it took to commit, I sprang from my concealment.

In half a dozen leaps I reached the corpses of the fallen green Martians. The smell of blood—real blood this time, metallic and warm—hit the air. I snatched up a mighty longsword from a dead warrior’s hand. The weight of it was familiar, honest. It fit my grip like a remembered tool, as if my palm had been made for it in another life.

Then I bounded toward the plant men.

The old battle-joy rose in me, that red mist that narrows the world to edge and target, and I smiled the smile that has always betrayed me in the midst of danger.

They did not see me at first. Their single eyes were fixed on their prey; their thousand ears were tuned to his breath and the scrape of his feet and the nervous rustle of the meadow grass. My approach was soundless in the light gravity.

I was among them before they understood. Four of them fell in quick succession, my blade biting through blue flesh with a wet, resistant give. Instead of blood, a green sticky syrup spurted and smeared, like the viscous ooze from a crushed caterpillar, and the air took on a faint, bruised-plant odor—sap and bitterness.

For an instant the herd recoiled.

In that instant the green warrior sprang to my side, seizing the chance as only a seasoned fighter can. Together we fought with our backs near the boulder, denying them the space to leap cleanly overhead.

Their tails lashed like scythes. Talons raked. The purring screeching churned the air, and above it, again, rose that shrill wail from high on the cliff—once, twice, again and again—calling more.

We were splashed head to foot with their syrupy lifefluid. It clung to skin and hair, tacky and cold, and when it dried it tugged like spilled sugar. A tail’s blade grazed my arm; the pain was sharp, clean, like a knife drawn across flesh. Another creature sprang, and I felt its great weight slam onto my back.

Talons sank into me.

Then came the most horrifying sensation of all: moist lips—if one could call them that—fastening to the wounds while the claws still held me, sucking at my lifeblood. Not from a face, but from a palm-mouth, drinking with the efficiency of a man siphoning broth through a tube. The intimacy of that feeding, the theft of warmth, made my vision flare white with fury.

I fought to keep my throat safe. In front of me one plant man lunged, reaching for my neck, while two more on either side whipped their tails in murderous rhythm. The green warrior beside me was hard-pressed, ringed and slashing, holding his ground with brute skill and relentless endurance.

I felt the unequal strain—two men against too many—and knew we could not keep it long.

Then the green warrior tore free, saw my plight, and with one great sweep of his blade raked the creature from my back. It fell twitching, syrup jetting from its severed channels. Freed, I dispatched the others nearest me, and we closed together again near the boulder, almost back to back, making a wall of steel.

We were winning ground now, because on the earth their advantage lessened. They could not soar above us for the killing tail-sweep without the clear space to leap, and each time they rushed in we met them with edge and point.

The shrill wail rose again.

This time I glanced up.

High above, on a little natural balcony in the cliff face, stood a man-shaped figure shrieking the signal. He waved one hand toward the river’s mouth as though beckoning, and with the other he pointed and gesticulated down at us, directing doom with the careless authority of a conductor leading a harsh, repetitive anthem.

I followed his gaze.

Across the meadow, from the forest, from the far flat land beyond the river, lines of leaping plant men converged—scores, then more, a hundred separate streams of bounding blue bodies. With them came new shapes, stranger still: swift-running monsters that moved now upright, now on all fours, their gait switching like a machine changing gears.

The meadow that had looked so carefully tended—this domestic, civilized carpet of red growth—was suddenly a battlefield about to be drowned in bodies.

“It will be a great death,” I said to my companion, because there are moments when plain words are all that remain. I nodded toward the oncoming tide. “Look!”

He cast one quick glance, and then—of all things—he smiled.

“We may at least die fighting and as great warriors should, John Carter,” he replied.

At the sound of my name in his guttural Martian tongue, astonishment cut through even the battle-haze. I turned, and through the slime and the sunlight and the stench of crushed sap I recognized the face beside me: the broad, fierce features; the intelligent eyes; the bearing that no rank can counterfeit.

Tars Tarkas, Jeddak of Thark—my friend, the mightiest of the green men of Barsoom—stood at my side.

Tars Tarkas and I had no breath to trade our stories among the toppled bodies at the boulder’s foot. The valley itself seemed to have been roused and poured toward us: from every direction a torrent of nightmares came running, summoned by the thin, piercing cry of that lone figure far up the cliff face.

“Come,” the Thark snapped, voice like flint struck on stone. “To the cliffs. There is our only hope—some cave, some ledge. Two may hold a narrow place forever against an unarmed mob.”

We fled across the scarlet sward. I kept my pace to his—his great stride was powerful, but his bulk cost him speed, and I would not leave him to be pulled down alone. The turf underfoot had the spring of a cultivated lawn, clipped too evenly to be natural, as though some patient, hungry gardener had been grazing it to the same height for centuries.

Three hundred yards. A single cooking-length in a man’s mind, but an eternity in a hunt.

The shriek from above kept slicing the air—high and bright as a knife on glass—and it drew the horde as surely as the smell of blood draws flies to a butcher’s trough. Beneath that call the valley filled with other sounds: thudding feet, the wet slap of bodies, the hard-click of claws. And underneath everything, like a hearth that would not go out, a purr—low, contented, obscene—rolling from the throats of the plant men.

Tars Tarkas glanced once at the distance still between us and the rock wall and read the odds as he would read a battlefield. “Go,” he said. “Find the shelter. Save the minutes.”

I threw all my Earth muscles into it and reached the cliff base in immense, bounding leaps. Up close, the wall rose sheer from the level sward, as if the world had been sliced clean with a blade. There was no talus, no slope of broken stone, no easy scramble—only scattered boulders half-sunk in the turf like bones in a shallow grave.

My first quick scan turned my stomach cold. Nowhere did I see the mercy of a crack, a seam, a handhold—nothing but the smooth, forbidding face, and far above it the strange herald still shrieking. To my right, the cliff’s foot vanished into the forest, whose luxuriant crowns climbed a thousand feet up the rock like waves frozen mid-surge. To my left, the escarpment ran unbroken toward mountains that penned the valley in a vast ring. Ahead, a river seemed to burst straight out of the cliff base, but it offered no path—only another trap.

The wall was five thousand feet high, dull yellow in shade, streaked with dusky red and green, with occasional flashes of white quartz. It was beautiful the way a sealed pantry is beautiful to a starving man: flawless, useless, and maddening.

Then the sun slid past the cliff’s zenith.

Light struck the face and the whole wall flared alive—gold and ruby and emerald, white gleams like chipped diamond. The rock did not merely shine; it glittered as if the cliff had been glazed with metal and studded with gems. It was a display fit for gods and thieves, but in that blaze I saw what I had missed in shadow: several black spots, high along the line where forest met cliff—cave mouths, dark as oven doors, tucked behind branches.

A path existed, but it ran through the trees.

I could climb; the skill was in my hands the way kneading is in a baker’s palms. Tars Tarkas, with his enormous weight and the Martian’s general lack of climbing practice, would find it near impossible. On Barsoom, the land rarely demanded a man climb straight up; the old, gentle ascents had let even warriors remain strangers to true vertical labor.

But there was nothing else. The horde was already close enough that their breathing began to thicken the air behind us.

We plunged into the forest’s shadow. The change was immediate: the crisp openness of the valley replaced by damp green dimness, the smell of resin and bruised leaf, and a chorus of living sounds—branch creaks, insect rasp, distant bird-calls—an acoustic shelter compared to the valley’s industrial roar of pursuit. Even here, though, the predators’ noise invaded, harsh and commerce-like, flattening the subtler music of the woods into something merely background.

We had almost reached a great tree that brushed the cliff face when one of the pursuers sprang in behind us—a giant plant man, claws outstretched, its multiple mouths working wetly in anticipation. It was ahead of the others by a hundred yards.

“Up, Tars Tarkas!” I shouted. “Climb—this one is mine.”

He seized the trunk and began to haul himself upward with grim, reluctant effort. I turned, sword raised, meaning to end the plant thing quickly—like cutting down a thick stalk before it seeds.

But the creature was not the mindless growth I’d taken it for. It halted at my feint; my blade carved only air. Its great tail swept the sward with the force of a battering ram and knocked me clean off my feet.

In a heartbeat it was on me. Cold, sucking mouths sought my throat and breast—lips like leeches, but stronger, greedier. I caught two writhing tentacles, one in each hand, and locked my arms. The thing was heavy and muscled, but there is a kind of strength that comes from a lifetime of knowing what it is to strain, to lift, to hold—strength as practical as a cook’s wrist after years of kneading and chopping. I choked it back from my neck and fought for leverage in the grass.

For an instant I thought I could win if we were alone.

Then I saw over its shoulder the wave that had been rushing to swallow us.

White apes.

Even on Mars, where every creature seems designed to test a man’s courage, these were the ones that reached nearest to fear—because they wore a rough parody of humanity. Fifteen feet tall, walking erect, with an extra pair of arms set between upper and lower limbs like some obscene attempt at improving upon the human frame. Their eyes were close-set, their ears high, their snouts and teeth like gorillas’. Bristling hair crowned their heads like a savage headdress.

They came in a storm of sound: snarls, screams, the hard drumming of feet, and that hideous purring from the plant men—so wrong in a thing that meant to drink you, as if a predator could make a meal of you and call it contentment.

They struck.

Talons and fangs found flesh. Sucking mouths fastened where they could, seeking arteries as deftly as a butcher seeks the right vein to bleed a carcass. Pain came hot and sudden, then a cold draining as the plant mouths clung.

I fought up, somehow, bodies dragging at me. I shortened my grip on the long-sword until it became a dagger and stabbed with brutal economy—no flourishes, only the necessary cuts that open a heart and end a charge. For a breath I stood free, half-blinded by sweat and the metallic smell of my own blood.

In that same breath Tars Tarkas dropped from the lower branches he had so painfully gained and landed beside me. Again we stood back to back as we had a hundred times—two blades answering a chaos of bodies.

The apes rushed; we beat them back. Plant men lashed us with tails like flails, sprang above us with greyhound speed, tried to wrap and suck and drag. Every assault met steel. My blade flashed and bit; Tars Tarkas’ sword moved with the sure, unsentimental precision of a man who had lived his whole life with death as his daily ration.

But no skill, no legend, can hold forever against a world’s worth of hungry muscle.

Step by step we were forced back until our shoulders hit the massive trunk of the tree we had chosen to climb. Around it we retreated, pressed by bodies, until we had been driven half around its base. Tars Tarkas led, and suddenly he gave a short, fierce sound—not fear, not pain, but triumph.

“Here is shelter for one at least, John Carter,” he said.

At the roots I saw a round opening, three feet across, yawning into darkness like a cellar door.

“In with you,” I cried.

He shook his head. “Too small. For you there is chance. For me—none. If we stay without, we both die. If you enter, you may live to avenge me.”

The Thark—whose people so often preferred death to flight—spoke of life as if it had become newly valuable. I did not understand the hunger in him, but I understood the thing driving me: Dejah Thoris. Love is a kind of native tongue; it makes commands of the body that no other language can translate.

“Then we die together,” I said. “I will not go first. You go while I hold them. When you are in, I can slip after.”

We spoke in jagged phrases between cuts and thrusts. At last he yielded, grim humor on his hard face.

“It was ever your way,” he said, “to think last of your own life. And ever your way to command others—even the greatest of jeddaks.”

He, Jeddak of Thark, turned to obey a man half his size.

“If you fail,” he said, “the cruel and heartless Thark—whom you taught the meaning of friendship—will come out to die beside you.”

“As you will,” I answered. “Now—head first. Quickly.”

He hesitated at the shame of turning his back. Pride is a dialect green men speak fluently, and retreat is a word that does not sit easily in their mouths.

“Haste,” I urged. “Or we both go down for nothing.”

He dropped and forced himself at the opening, shoulders grinding, while the howling mass sensed escape and hurled itself at me with doubled fury.

My sword became a blur. Green sap—sticky, acrid—slicked the steel from plant men I opened; red blood sprayed from white apes whose hearts I found with ruthless thrusts. The noise was a crushing thing now: screeches and growls, and that purring—soft as a satisfied diner’s hum, but uttered by mouths that drank flesh.

They piled on. Even with the ground heaped with their dead, they came.

At last they dragged me down. Cold mouths sealed against torn skin; life seemed to drain with a sickening suction.

Then powerful hands clamped my ankles and yanked me backward. In a second I was sliding into the tree’s hollow, hauled by Tars Tarkas. A plant man clung to my chest, tenacious as a burr. For a moment it was a tug-of-war between its hunger and the Thark’s strength, until I wedged my sword point under it and drove upward, piercing whatever served it for vitals. It shuddered and went slack.

Inside, I lay panting on the flat, hard floor—torn, bleeding, tasting iron and sap. The hollow was wide—fifty feet across—and the ground bore the polished wear of long habitation, as if generations of creatures had used this place the way families use a kitchen: by returning to it again and again until their bodies leave a history in the surface.

Tars Tarkas crouched at the opening, sword ready, blocking it with his bulk as best he could. For an hour the mob raged outside—howling, shrieking, battering the trunk. They tried the entrance a few times but found it too small to force in numbers; soon they settled into sound—terrorizing screams, ape growls, and that purring that seemed designed not merely to threaten the body but to sour the mind.

Eventually most of them drifted away, leaving perhaps a score to watch. A siege, then, and starvation. Even if we slipped out after dark, where would we go in this unknown valley—hemmed by cliffs and monsters and a forest that could hide a thousand mouths?

As the noise dwindled and my eyes adjusted to the dimness, I explored. Looking up, I saw far above a faint glow—an opening in the stem. Hope, thin as the first scent of bread from an oven when you have been starving.

At the far side I found a rough ladder leading to a series of horizontal wooden bars spanning the narrowing shaft. They were set three feet apart, forming a perfect climb, vanishing into darkness above.

I told Tars Tarkas. He nodded once. “Go,” he said. “See what lies above. I will guard.”

I climbed.

The bars rose endlessly. The light grew stronger. Five hundred feet up, I reached an opening about the size of the one below. It opened onto a broad limb, its surface worn smooth by long use—an avenue, a road in the air. I did not step out. Sound carries strangely in high places; a single scrape can betray you. Instead I returned swiftly.

We climbed together then, Tars Tarkas leading. As I ascended, I pulled free the lower bars behind us, dislodging them and passing them up. He carried them a hundred feet higher and wedged them in place, while I continued stripping away the rungs beneath. Soon the tree’s interior was denuded of all means of ascent for a hundred feet from the base—no pursuer could follow easily. It was a cook’s instinct applied to war: remove the tools, deny the next hand the knife.

When we reached the upper opening, Tars Tarkas stepped aside. “You,” he said. “Your weight is less. Your limbs are quicker.”

I eased out onto the great limb. It rose at a gentle angle toward the cliff. Following it, I found it ended above a narrow ledge at the mouth of a small cave. The branch’s tip was slimmer; it bent under me until it swayed level with the ledge, only a couple of feet away. Below lay the scarlet valley carpeted like spilled spice; above, the golden cliff face blazed, jeweled and merciless.

This cave was not one I’d seen from below; those were higher, perhaps a thousand feet. But a shelter is a shelter, and a door is a door, no matter what name you give it in what language.

I returned for Tars Tarkas. Together we wormed our way along the wavering branch, clinging like men on a hanging bridge. At the end, our combined weight depressed the limb so far that the cave mouth rose out of reach.

We argued quickly and decided: Tars Tarkas would go back along the branch, leaving his longest leather harness strap with me. When the limb rose under my lighter weight, I would enter the cave, then lower the strap and haul him up.

He retreated, stiff with distaste at the necessity of yielding ground. The moment his weight left the tip, the branch rose. I balanced, reached, and swung myself onto the ledge, then into the cave mouth. When he returned, I dropped the strap. He caught it, and with brute strength and my leverage I hauled him up until we stood together on that dizzy little balcony.

From there the valley spread like a painted tablecloth: forest and crimson sward bordering a silent sea, all framed by the monstrous, gleaming cliffs. In the far distance, for a moment, we thought we saw a gilded minaret shining among the treetops—an artifact of civilization, a promise of voices and hearths and meals eaten without fear. But the image wavered and vanished into our doubt, and we dismissed it as a mirage born of desire.

Below, white apes tore into the remnants of Tars Tarkas’ fallen companions, feeding with the noisy obliviousness of beasts that have never learned reverence for the dead. Plant men grazed in widening circles, trimming the red sward as neatly as any herd trims a pasture, their feeding a grotesque echo of domestic routine—harvest without gratitude, sustenance without memory.

Attack from the tree now seemed unlikely. We turned to the cave, believing it a continuation of the hidden path—leading away, gods alone knew where, but away from that valley’s ferocity.

Inside, we found a tunnel cut clean from the solid cliff. It was well proportioned, twenty feet high, five feet wide, roof arched like an old stone oven. We had no light. We groped forward, slow and careful, Tars Tarkas’ hand on one wall, mine on the other, our free hands clasped so we would not be separated if the passage branched. In darkness, touch becomes its own language, and even that language fails when fear starts translating every brush of stone into a threat.

We went on until our hands met an obstruction. Not the rock itself—this felt like hard wood, fitted across the tunnel like a partition. I swept my fingers over it until I found what every Martian dwelling knows: the button that stands in for an Earthman’s knob. I pressed it gently.

The door gave.

A dimly lighted chamber opened beyond, seemingly empty. I swung the door wider and stepped through, the Thark following.

We stood silent, listening. The chamber’s air smelled old—dry, sealed, as if it had been stored away from weather and time like a rare spice kept in a jar no one dares to open.

Then a slight noise behind us.

I turned in time to see the door close with a sharp click—clean, final—like a lid snapping over a pot.

I sprang back and clawed at it, suddenly certain that the quiet here was not peace but preparation. My fingers found no button on this side. The portal was smooth and unyielding.

And then, from unseen lips, laughter rang through the chamber—cruel, mocking, and edged with the satisfaction of a host who has trapped his guests in a house whose language they do not speak.

For a handful of breaths after that awful laughter died away, Tars Tarkas and I held the silence as though it were a weapon—tight-gripped, ready to be turned. Nothing else stirred. The air lay still in the rock-hewn chamber, tasting of old dust and the faint, sharp tang that radium light always seems to leave upon the tongue, like metal kissed too long.

At last the great Thark gave one of those low, rumbling laughs his people loose in the face of terror—not hysteria, but a hard-edged relish, as though horror were a spice and they had been bred to crave it. I had seen them laugh so, green giants rolling in mirth while the Great Games wrung screams from women and children. It was the same sound now, though quieter, as if even he measured his breath in this place.

I forced a smile, because there are moments when a steady mouth is a better shield than a trembling jaw.

“What do you make of it?” I asked. “Where in the deuce are we?”

He stared as though I had said the sky was underground.

“Where are we?” he repeated. “Do you tell me, John Carter, that you know not where you be?”

“That I am upon Barsoom is all I can guess,” I said. “But for you—and the great white apes—I should scarcely even guess that. The sights of this day are as strange to me as though I had walked out of my own grave and found another planet in Mars’s place. No, Tars Tarkas, I know not where we be.”

He drew a deep breath, and in it I heard the dry rasp of the chamber’s silence answering him.

“Where have you been,” he demanded, “since you opened the mighty portals of the atmosphere plant—years ago—after the keeper had died and the engines stopped and all Barsoom was dying, that had not already died, of asphyxiation? Your body even was never found, though a whole world sought it. Though the Jeddak of Helium and his granddaughter—your princess—offered such rewards that even princes hunted for you.

“There was but one conclusion when all failed: that you had taken the long, last pilgrimage down the mysterious River Iss, to await in the Valley Dor upon the shores of the Lost Sea of Korus the beautiful Dejah Thoris.

“Why you had gone none could guess, for your princess still lived—”

“Thank God,” I broke in, and the words came up out of me like water from a struck spring. “I did not dare ask you. I feared I might have been too late. She was very low when I left her in the royal gardens of Tardos Mors that night—so low I scarcely hoped even then to reach the atmosphere plant before her dear spirit fled from me forever. And she lives yet?”

“She lives, John Carter.”

The relief ran through me so keenly it was almost pain. For a heartbeat the radium-lit gold of the walls seemed warmer, as though the chamber itself had softened.

“You have not told me where we are,” I said, clinging to the one practical thread left to me.

“We are where I expected to find you,” he answered, and his voice turned heavy, like a blade held point-down. “And another. Long ago you heard the story of the woman who taught me the thing green Martians are reared to hate—the woman who taught me to love. You know the tortures and the death her love won at the hands of Tal Hajus.

“She, I thought, awaited me by the Lost Sea of Korus.

“You know it was left for a man from another world—you—to teach this Thark what friendship is; and you, I thought, also roamed the care-free Valley Dor.

“Thus were the two I most longed for at the end of the pilgrimage I must take some day. And so, as the time elapsed which Dejah Thoris had hoped might bring you once more to her side—since she has always tried to believe you had but temporarily returned to your own planet—I at last gave way to my yearning. A month since I started upon the journey the end of which you have this day witnessed. Do you understand now where you be, John Carter?”

“And that was the River Iss,” I said slowly, “emptying into the Lost Sea of Korus in the Valley Dor?”

“This is the valley of love and peace and rest to which every Barsoomian since time immemorial has longed to pilgrimage at the end of a life of hate and strife and bloodshed,” he replied. Then, with a cold, iron twist that tasted like ash: “This, John Carter, is Heaven.”

The bitterness in it was not loud. It did not need to be. It was the bitterness of a man whose cradle-songs had promised sweetness and whose mouth had met only poison.

I laid my hand upon his shoulder, the tough hide of his harness warm under my palm.

“I am sorry,” I said, because there was nothing else that did not sound like mockery.

“Think,” he said, “of the countless billions who have taken the voluntary pilgrimage down that cruel river since the beginning of time, only to fall into the ferocious clutches of the creatures that assailed us to-day.

“There is an ancient legend that once a red man returned from the banks of Korus—returned from Dor—back through the River Iss. The legend says he told a blasphemy: that horrid brutes lived in a valley of wondrous loveliness, brutes that pounced upon each Barsoomian as he ended his pilgrimage and devoured him upon the banks where he had looked to find love and peace. But the ancients killed the blasphemer, as tradition ordains any shall be killed who return from the bosom of the River of Mystery.

“But now we know it was no blasphemy. The man told only what he saw. And what does it profit us? Even should we escape, we would be treated as blasphemers. We are between the wild thoat of certainty and the mad zitidar of fact—we can escape neither.”

“As Earth men say,” I replied, and could not help the thin smile the phrase pulled from me, “we are between the devil and the deep sea, Tars Tarkas.”

“There is naught to do,” he said, “but take things as they come. At least we shall have the satisfaction of knowing that whoever slays us will count far more of their own dead than they gain in return. White ape or plant man, green Barsoomian or red man—whosoever takes the last toll from us will know it is costly in lives to wipe out John Carter, Prince of the House of Tardos Mors, and Tars Tarkas, Jeddak of Thark, at the same time.”

The grim boast had a sturdy comfort in it; I laughed, and he joined me—one of those rare, clean laughs of his that sounded almost human.

“But about yourself,” he said at length, the merriment dropping away like a cloak. “If you have not been here all these years—where have you been? How is it that I find you here to-day?”

“I have been back to Earth,” I answered. “For ten long Earth years I have prayed and hoped for the day that would carry me once more to this grim old planet of yours—for which, with all its cruel customs, I feel a bond of sympathy and love even greater than for the world that gave me birth.

“For ten years I have endured a living death of doubt as to whether Dejah Thoris lived. And now, the first time my doubt is relieved, fate hurls me into the one tiny spot of all Barsoom from which there is apparently no escape—or if there be escape, at a price that would snuff out forever the last flickering hope of seeing my princess again in this life. And you have seen to-day with what pitiful futility man yearns toward a material hereafter.

“Only a bare half-hour before I saw you battling with the plant men I stood in moonlight upon the banks of a broad river that taps the eastern shore of Earth’s most blessed land. I have answered you. Do you believe?”

“I believe,” said Tars Tarkas, “though I cannot understand.”

While we spoke, my eyes had been ranging over the chamber. It ran perhaps two hundred feet in length and half as broad. In the center of the wall opposite our entrance stood what appeared to be another doorway.

The room had been hewn from cliff material that dulled to a muted gold under the dim, steady glow of a single radium illuminator set in the roof’s center. Here and there, polished patches of ruby, emerald, and diamond caught and threw back the light like hard, cold candies in a dull loaf—bright flashes embedded in something otherwise lifeless. The floor was of a different substance, worn by long use until it had the smoothness and slick gleam of glass underfoot.

No other openings showed. Since the door behind us was locked, I went to the one ahead.

As I stretched my hand toward the controlling button, that cruel, mocking laugh rang out again—so close this time that my body drew back before my mind could order it, and my fingers tightened on my sword hilt.

Then from the far corner of the chamber a hollow voice began to chant, each phrase falling with the same dull insistence as a pestle striking mortar:

“There is no hope, there is no hope; the dead return not, the dead return not; nor is there any resurrection. Hope not, for there is no hope.”

We snapped our gazes toward the sound. No figure waited there. The emptiness itself seemed to speak. A cold ripple ran down my spine; the short hairs at my neck lifted as a hound’s will when it scents what it cannot see.

I crossed quickly to the spot, but the voice ceased before I reached the wall, and then from the opposite end of the chamber a second voice burst out, shrill and piercing, like a knife drawn across glass:

“Fools! Fools! Thinkest thou to defeat the eternal laws of life and death? Wouldst cheat the mysterious Issus, Goddess of Death, of her just dues? Did not her mighty messenger, the ancient Iss, bear you upon her leaden bosom at your own behest to the Valley Dor?

“Thinkest thou, O fools, that Issus wilt give up her own? Thinkest thou to escape from whence in all the countless ages but a single soul has fled?

“Go back the way thou camest, to the merciful maws of the children of the Tree of Life or the gleaming fangs of the great white apes, for there lies speedy surcease from suffering; but insist in your rash purpose to thread the mazes of the Golden Cliffs of the Mountains of Otz, past the ramparts of the impregnable fortresses of the Holy Therns, and upon your way Death in its most frightful form will overtake you—a death so horrible that even the Holy Therns themselves, who conceived both Life and Death, avert their eyes from its fiendishness and close their ears against the hideous shrieks of its victims.

“Go back, O fools, the way thou camest.”

And again the laughter—now here, now there—skittered through the chamber like something alive.

“Most uncanny,” I said.

“What shall we do?” Tars Tarkas asked. “We cannot fight empty air. I would almost sooner return and face foes into whose flesh I may feel my blade bite—know I am selling my carcass dearly—before I go down to that eternal oblivion which is evidently the fairest eternity mortal man may hope for.”

“If, as you say, we cannot fight empty air,” I replied, “neither can empty air fight us. I, who have faced and conquered thousands of warriors and tempered blades, shall not be turned back by wind; nor no more shall you, Thark.”

“But unseen voices may come from unseen creatures who wield invisible blades,” he insisted.

“Rot,” I snapped. “Those voices come from beings as real as you or I. In their veins flows blood that may be let. The fact they remain invisible is proof enough they are mortal—and not over-courageous mortals at that. Think you John Carter will fly at the first shriek of a coward who dares not come out and face a good blade?”

I spoke loudly on purpose, so our tormentors might hear. I was weary of being played upon like a stringed instrument—twitch here, tremble there, dance to a disembodied laugh. And too, it had occurred to me that the whole performance was meant to drive us back to the valley of death we had escaped, where the plant men and apes might finish us more conveniently.

A long silence followed.

Then a soft, stealthy sound behind me—more felt than heard, like the faintest scrape of a claw on stone—made me whirl.

A banth was creeping upon me, sinuous and low.

The banth is a great beast of prey from the low hills around the dead seas of ancient Mars. Like most Barsoomian beasts it is nearly hairless save for a bristly mane about its thick neck. Its long lithe body runs on ten powerful legs. Its enormous jaws bear several rows of needle fangs, and the mouth splits back almost to its tiny ears. Its eyes—bulging, green, coldly bright—give the final touch of nightmare.

It lashed its tail against its yellow sides; and when it saw itself discovered it loosed that roar which freezes lesser creatures for the instant it springs.

It sprang.

But the voice that would have paralyzed prey had no hold on me. It met steel. My long-sword took it full, and a heartbeat later I drew the blade free from its still heart.

Turning, I saw Tars Tarkas facing another such monster. He dispatched it with one heavy, sure stroke, and before the carcass finished shuddering, instinct—some guardian something that has served me more than once—pulled my head around again.

Another beast was already leaping across the chamber.

From then on, for the better part of an hour, one hideous creature after another was loosed upon us—banths and other savage denizens of the Martian wilds—springing as though from empty air.

Tars Tarkas grew almost content. Here at last was an enemy he could feel, a throat to cut, a bone to cleave. For my part, the diversion was a marked improvement over ghost-voices and laughter. These things bled. Their rage and pain were honest in sound. Their death stank as real death does—hot blood and torn meat—filling the golden chamber with the harsh animal musk of slaughter.

And yet the manner of their coming was too neatly staged. I noticed that they appeared only when our backs were turned. We never saw one truly materialize. I never once allowed myself to believe in any supernatural birth from nothing. There had to be a concealed entrance—well contrived, well fitted to the golden stone.

Among the ornaments of Tars Tarkas’ leather harness—his only clothing besides capes and robes for the cold—hung a small mirror midway down his back, about the size of a lady’s hand glass.

Once, as he stood looking down at a newly fallen brute, my gaze caught that mirror’s shine. And in it I saw something that made my breath hitch and my voice drop to a thread.

“Move not, Tars Tarkas! Move not a muscle!”

He did not ask why. He simply froze like a carved idol while I stared into the mirror as though it were a window.

In its bright surface I saw a section of wall behind me shift—a precise turn upon pivots. With it, a section of floor in front of it turned as well, moving together so cleanly that no seam had shown in the dim light. It was as though one set a visiting-card upon its edge on a silver dollar: the card the wall, the coin the floor, both turning in one smooth motion.

Halfway through the turn, a great beast was revealed sitting upon its haunches on the moving floor—waiting like a weapon in a hand. When the section stopped, the beast faced toward me, now on our side of the partition.

Simple—once you saw it.

But what struck deeper than the mechanism was what the half-open partition revealed beyond: a larger chamber, well lighted, where several men and women were chained to the wall. In front of them, close to the pivoting device, stood a wicked-faced man—not red like the red men, nor green like the green men, but white like myself, with a great mass of flowing yellow hair.

Behind him, chained prisoners—red Martians. With them, chained beasts: the very sort that had been turned upon us, and others as savage.

When I turned back to meet the next foe, my heart was lighter than it had been since the laughter began.

“Watch the wall at your end,” I whispered to Tars Tarkas when we were close enough. “It is through secret doorways that the brutes are loosed.”

I spoke low so the watchers would not learn we knew; yet I was certain they did watch. No further attacks came as long as we stood facing opposite ends of the chamber. It was clear enough: there were peepholes or slits somewhere, and they dared not swing the door while their trick would be seen in the open.

A plan came to me.

Backing close to Tars Tarkas, I breathed it into his ear while keeping my eyes fixed on my end of the room. He grunted assent—quick, decisive.

He began backing toward the wall I faced while I advanced before him. We moved slowly, like men inching toward a fire they mean to seize, not to flee. When we were within ten feet of the secret doorway, I halted him.

“Stay motionless until my signal,” I murmured, and then—hard though it was—I turned my back upon the wall I knew could open, as though I were weary and careless.

My eyes snapped to the mirror on his back.

A moment. Two.

Then the golden surface began to move—rapidly this time, confident.

Before the turn was complete I gave the signal.

In the same instant I sprang for the receding half; Tars Tarkas wheeled and leaped for the opening made by the in-swinging section. One bound carried me clean through into the adjoining room.

I came up face to face with the yellow-haired man.

He was about my height, well muscled, shaped in every outward detail like an Earth man. At his side hung a long-sword, a short-sword, a dagger—and one of the destructive radium revolvers common on Mars.

Though I held only my long-sword, and by Barsoomian law he should have met me with the same or lesser weapon, his ethics were as warped as his grin. He whipped out the revolver before my feet had fully found the floor.

I met it with an upstroke; my blade slapped the weapon from his grasp before it could speak.

Then he drew his long-sword, and we were evenly armed.

We went at it.

He was a marvelous swordsman—trained and practiced. I had not gripped a sword for ten years before that morning. At first my arm felt the old motions like a forgotten language, familiar in the bones but rusty on the tongue. Yet the body remembers what the mind mislays. Within minutes I had found the rhythm again—the measure of his reach, the timing of his shifts. And then I saw it dawn on him: he had met his match.

His face went livid with rage as my guard held. Blood began to bead and run from a dozen small cuts on his cheek, his brow, his forearm—little red proofs that steel had found him.

“Who are you, white man?” he hissed through clenched teeth. “That you are no Barsoomian from the outer world is evident from your colour. And you are not of us.”

The last came almost as a question.

“What if I were from the Temple of Issus?” I threw it out on a wild guess, the words tasting like bait on my tongue.

“Fate forfend!” he breathed, and his face blanched beneath the blood.

I stored that reaction away like a cook noting which spice makes a man flinch—useful later, perhaps, when I might need fear as a seasoning in another’s will.

But the present matter required no guessing. It required only the simple, final thrust.

Within seconds I got my sword between his ribs. He stiffened, his eyes widening in disbelief, and then he sagged to the floor in a heavy, inert heap.

All through our duel the chained prisoners had watched in tense silence. No sound in the room but steel’s clang, the soft shuffle of our bare feet, and the few tight words we had forced out between breaths.

Now, as the body slumped down, a cry broke from one of the chained women.

“Turn! Turn! Behind you!” she shrieked.

I wheeled at the first note and found myself facing a second man of the same white race. He had crept out of a dark corridor and was almost upon me, sword raised.

Tars Tarkas was nowhere in sight. The secret panel through which I had come was closed.

How I wished the great green warrior stood at my shoulder then. I had fought almost continuously for hours; I had been wrung through adventure as though fate meant to see what strength could be drained from a man. I had eaten nothing for nearly twenty-four hours, and slept not at all. Weariness clung to me like a soaked cloak.

For the first time in years I felt the faint, ugly question: Can I do this?

There was no choice. My salvation lay only in speed and ferocity—ending him before the long strain could finish me.

I rushed him.

But he would not be rushed into death. He backed and parried, parried and sidestepped, cool as a man stirring a pot he knows will boil in time. If possible he was even more adroit than the first. He led me—step by step—until my limbs began to feel distant from my will, until the room’s edges softened, until the radium light smeared in my sight.

I grew weaker, and he knew it. He played me, drawing me around until I stood before the corpse of his fellow.

Then he rushed suddenly—forcing me back.

My heel struck the dead body. The momentum of my own wearied weight pitched me backward across it. My head hit the hard pavement with a crack that rang in my ears.

And to that pain alone I owe my life, for it snapped my brain clear as cold water in the face. Temper flared up through exhaustion—hot, bright, and clean—and as I flung a hand down to drive myself up, my fingers closed upon something cold and metallic on the floor.

The first thing that hauled me back from the soft blackness was not light, nor pain, but sound—an ugly stew of it, thick as overcooked porridge. Feet scuffed and dragged beyond the blank golden wall at my side; chains whispered and answered one another; metal harness rang with a dull, tired clink; and underneath it all came the animal music of the place—banths coughing in their throats, a low snarl that vibrated through stone, and a man’s breath forced out in hard, wet pulls as though the air itself were something to be chewed.

For a heartbeat I could not place where I lay. Then the chamber resolved around me: the stench of old blood dried into metal; the sour reek of beasts kept too long without clean water; the copper taste that always rose in my own mouth after fighting. Along the opposite wall the prisoners and the brutes were chained in a line like goods hung in a market that sold only despair. Eyes followed me—some dull with resignation, some bright with hatred, some with the sharp, starving hope that a change in the wind can bring.

One face among them held my attention, not because it pleaded, but because it watched as a skilled hand watches a fire. She was young, a red Martian woman of that clean-limbed, noble type whose beauty seems almost indecent in such a place—skin like light copper warmed at the forge, eyes steady, mouth set as if she had learned to ration her words the way one rations spices when the pantry is bare. She wore no ornaments by which to read her rank, only the plainness of captivity; yet there was a composure about her that did not fit chains.

The sounds beyond the wall sharpened and I knew them at last. Tars Tarkas. Not the measured tread of the Jeddak of Tharks issuing orders, but the brutal cadence of a warrior alone, making his own body a barricade.

I threw myself at the secret door, shoulder and hip driving into the smooth gold. It might as well have been the cliff itself. I clawed along the seam, searching for the trick of it with fingertips that still trembled from the earlier struggle, but the wall gave me nothing—no latch, no handle, no mercy. I had just lifted my longsword, rage ready to ruin good steel against senseless metal, when the chained girl spoke.

“Save thy sword, O mighty warrior,” she called, her voice low and certain, like an elder in a farm-kitchen warning a youth not to waste flour on a failed batch. “Thou shalt need it where it will bite. That panel yields to a finger when one knows its secret.”

“You know it?” I demanded.

“Yes. Release me, and I will open to thee. The keys are upon the first dead of thy foemen.” She hesitated only long enough to glance toward the other side of the wall, where the struggle rasped on. “But why return to face the banth again—or whatever ruin they have loosed? None are meant to go back.”

“Because my friend fights there alone.”

I found the ring of keys on the carcass of the dead custodian—cold metal slick with blood—and carried it to her. There were many keys, each shaped to some particular cruelty. Without fumbling she chose one, the right one, and the great lock at her waist sprang with a heavy click. The sound was as loud in that room as a ladle struck against an empty pot: freedom announced.

She moved to the blank wall as if she had done it a thousand times in her mind. From the ring she selected a slender, needle-like key and slid it into an almost invisible puncture in the metal. A soft internal catch sighed, and the panel swung on its pivot.

The floor beneath my feet moved with it—an ingenious treachery. Before I could even shift my weight, the revolving section carried me through the wall and set me down in the adjoining chamber.

Tars Tarkas stood there, backed into an angle, holding the corner with his body because the room had become the enemy. Six banths crouched in a half circle before him—huge, corded things, heads low, shoulders ridged and smeared with their own blood and his. The beasts’ wariness was written in the slices and bruises across their muzzles. It was also written on him: his glossy green hide was torn as though someone had tried to carve him like meat from the bone. Talons and teeth had opened his leg, his arm, his breast. Blood—dark and copious—had run and dried and run again, the way a cooking pot bubbles over and crusts at the rim and then boils anew.

He should not have been standing. The wall did the work his failing strength could not. Yet his one hand held his sword as though the steel were an extension of will, not muscle. He looked up and, impossibly, a grim smile touched his mouth—whether relief at seeing me or amusement at my own battered state I could not tell.

I sprang forward, ready to throw myself into the circle, when a hand—light as a brush of flour from a baker’s wrist—touched my shoulder. I turned. The girl had followed.

“Wait,” she whispered. “Leave them to me.”

She stepped past me—unarmed, unarmored—into the animal stench of the banths as calmly as someone stepping into a dairy yard at dawn. The beasts’ snarls swelled; their claws flexed on the floor with a sound like knives on a board. She spoke a single Martian word, soft but edged with command.

The six great heads snapped toward her. I saw, in my mind’s eye, her flesh torn, her voice cut off mid-syllable. Instead, the monsters slunk to her feet like punished hounds, shoulders rolling low, ears flat, tails dragging. She spoke again, too low for me to catch, and began to walk toward the far side of the chamber. The banths rose and padded after her in obedient file, their heavy breathing now subdued, the rhythm of their paws on metal oddly domestic, like animals led from pen to trough.

One by one she sent them through another secret panel. The last banth vanished; she turned back and smiled at us—brief, almost weary—and then slipped through herself, leaving the chamber abruptly empty of everything but blood, breath, and astonishment.

For a moment neither Tars Tarkas nor I spoke. The absence of snarling was so sudden it rang in the ears like a silence after a factory’s engines cut out. In that hush the only living sound was the Thark’s harsh breathing and the distant, muffled clatter of the underground.

At last he said, “I heard fighting beyond the partition. I did not fear for thee, John Carter, until I heard the report of a revolver. There lives no man on Barsoom who may face thee with naked steel and live; but a shot—” He shook his head slowly. “Thou hast no firearms. Tell me.”

I told him quickly—of the therns, the trap, the girl’s warning, and the bullet that had been meant for me. While I spoke, his eyes stayed on my face with that relentless attentiveness his kind gives to a scent on the wind.

When I finished, we went together to the panel I had entered by, the one opposite the passage through which she had led the banths away. We searched for its secret with bruised fingers, pressing, tapping, testing seams. It did not yield. We crossed the chamber to the other golden door and tried it as well. Nothing. The place was a jar sealed without a lid.

Prisoners were kept chained here, which meant the therns had no fear of them reaching the outside. Somewhere there must be passages—means to move beasts, slaves, food, weapons, the whole machinery of terror. Yet the metal refused us. Again and again we turned between the two panels like men walking between two ovens, feeling the heat but unable to find the handle.

Then, as though mocking our ignorance, one panel turned of its own accord, silent as a thought. The girl stood there once more.

“Who are you,” she asked, “and what your mission, that you have the temerity to attempt escape from the Valley Dor and the death you have chosen?”

“I have chosen no death,” I said. “I am not of Barsoom, nor have I taken the voluntary pilgrimage upon the River Iss. My friend here is Jeddak of all the Tharks. Whether he longs to return or no, I am taking him from the living lie that lured him to this place.”

I straightened, and in that foul chamber spoke the titles that tied me to another world, another life. “I am John Carter, Prince of the House of Tardos Mors, Jeddak of Helium. Perhaps a rumor of me has reached even here.”

She smiled, but without warmth. “Yes. Naught that passes in the world we have left is unknown here. I have heard of you—many years ago. The therns wondered whither you had flown, since you took neither pilgrimage nor could be found upon Barsoom.”

“Then tell me who you are,” I said, “and why you are prisoner—yet hold power over the beasts as one who knows their breeding and their bridles.”

“Slave I am,” she replied. “Fifteen years a slave in this terrible place. Now they tire of me—and fear what I know—so I am condemned to die the death.” She shuddered, and the sound was like cloth drawn across skin.

“What death?”

“The Holy Therns eat human flesh,” she said simply, as if naming a recipe learned unwillingly. “But only that which has died beneath the sucking lips of a plant man—flesh from which the defiling blood of life has been drawn. To this end I have been condemned. It should have been within hours, had your coming not interrupted them.”

I felt a coldness behind my ribs. “Then those I killed—were they Holy Therns?”

“Oh, no. Lesser therns. Of the same cruel race.” She nodded toward the unseen reaches of the mountain. “The Holy Therns abide upon the outer slopes, facing the broad world from which they harvest victims and spoils. Labyrinthine passages connect these caves with their palaces, and through them pass lesser therns, slaves, prisoners, beasts—the inhabitants of this sunless world.”

She spoke on, and as she did her voice took on the steady cadence of one reciting a history that the conquerors keep alive while the conquered forget their own words. “There are men and women born within these passages who have never seen the light of day—nor ever shall. They are kept to do the bidding of the therns; to furnish their sport and sustenance.

“Now and again a pilgrim drifting from the Iss escapes the plant men and the great white apes that guard the Temple of Issus and falls into thern hands; or, as was my misfortune, is coveted by a Holy Thern who watches from the balcony above the river where it issues from the mountains through the cliffs of gold to empty into the Lost Sea of Korus.”

The names—Iss, Korus—fell from her tongue with the weight of old belief, syllables polished by repetition until their edges cut. I wondered how many dialects had died down here, how many native songs had been replaced by the therns’ words for fear and obedience. Even the sounds of this place—chains, claws, metal—seemed designed to drown out memory.

“All who reach the Valley Dor are, by custom, rightful prey of plant men and apes,” she continued, “while arms and ornaments become portion of the therns. But if one escapes the valley’s denizens even for hours, the therns may claim him. And a Holy Thern on watch, should he see a victim he covets, often tramples upon the rights of the valley brutes and takes his prize by foul means if he cannot gain it by fair.

“It is said that occasionally some deluded victim of Barsoomian superstition so far escapes the enemies that beset him as to reach the walls of the Temple of Issus. But what fate awaits within—none know. None who pass those gilded walls return.”

“The Temple is to the therns what the Valley Dor is imagined by the outer world to be to them,” she finished, and for an instant contempt flashed across her face like a knife catching light. “Their ultimate haven—delights of the flesh for a race of mental giants and moral pygmies.”

“A heaven within a heaven,” I said. “Let us hope it measures to them what they measure to others.”

She murmured, “Who knows? The therns are spoken of by all Barsoom with awe, as of gods.”

“They are mortal,” she said. “They die as we do, save that their allotted span is one thousand years. Those who live it may take their way, by custom, through the long tunnel that leads to Issus. Those who die before are supposed to spend the balance of their time in the image of a plant man. For this reason plant men are held sacred by the therns, since they believe each hideous creature was formerly a thern.”

“And if the plant man dies?” I asked.

“Then the soul passes into a great white ape—if the thousand years are not yet complete. If the ape dies before the exact hour that ends the thousand years, the soul is lost for ever and passes into the carcass of the slimy silians whose wriggling thousands seethe the silent sea beneath the moons when the sun is gone and strange shapes walk through the Valley Dor.”

Tars Tarkas, despite blood loss and exhaustion, gave a low laugh. “We sent several Holy Therns to the silians to-day, then.”

“And so thy death will be the more terrible when it comes,” she said, as though the therns’ theology were a ledger where each act accrued interest. “And come it will—you cannot escape.”

“One has escaped, centuries ago,” I reminded her. “What has been done may be done again.”

“It is useless even to try,” she answered, hopelessness slipping into her words like ash into bread dough. Then she looked at me, and something like pride fought its way through. “But try, if you must.”

“Try we shall,” I said, “and you shall go with us, if you wish.”
