She drew herself up. “To be put to death by mine own people—and render my memory a disgrace to my family and nation? A Prince of the House of Tardos Mors should know better than to suggest such a thing.”

I felt Tars Tarkas’s gaze on me—heavy, expectant. My answer would be more than counsel to her; it would be the shape of our own fate. If I bowed to superstition, we would remain and be consumed by this mockery of paradise.

“We have the right to escape if we can,” I said. “Our moral sense will not be offended if we succeed, for we know the fable of love and peace in Dor is a wicked deception. The valley is not sacred. The Holy Therns are not holy. They are cruel mortals who know no more of the real life to come than we.

“Not only is it our right—it is our duty. Even if our peoples revile and torture us when we return. Only thus may we carry the truth to those without. And though mortals cling to superstition like a child to a familiar word even when the meaning is empty, we should be cowards to shirk what lies before us. With the testimony of several, perhaps the truth may be believed—or at least doubted enough that an expedition will be sent to investigate this hideous mockery.”

Silence settled. In it I could hear, faintly, the distant living machinery of the underground—doors moving, steps, the far-off echo of a chain drawn through a ring—sounds that, over years, must have rewritten the minds of those trapped here. Sound shapes thought; noise can become a master as cruel as any thern.

At last the girl spoke. “Never had I considered it thus,” she said softly. “Indeed would I give my life a thousand times if I could save one soul from the awful life I have led. Yes. You are right. I will go with you as far as we can go—though I doubt we shall escape.”

I turned to Tars Tarkas.

“To the gates of Issus or to the bottom of Korus,” he said, voice steady. “To the snows north or the snows south, Tars Tarkas follows where John Carter leads. I have spoken.”

“Come, then,” I said. “We cannot be further from escape than we are now—in the heart of this mountain and within four walls built for death.”

“Come,” the girl answered, and there was a grim humor in it. “But do not flatter thyself there is no worse place within the therns’ territory.”

She swung the panel open and led us back into the chamber where the chained prisoners waited. There were ten red Martians in all, men and women, their faces hollowed by captivity yet still carrying the stamp of their homes and tribes—features like remembered recipes, distinct and local, stubborn against the therns’ uniform cruelty. When we explained—briefly, for time was a knife at our throats—they chose to join us. I saw their misgivings: the old superstition still tugged at them, not because it was true, but because it was the language their world had used to name death for centuries.

The girl—Thuvia, we learned—moved from prisoner to prisoner, selecting keys with the same surety she had shown at the door. Locks sprang, chains dropped. The sound of each shackle hitting the floor was a small percussion of rebellion.

Tars Tarkas and I stripped the two dead therns of their weapons: swords, daggers, and two of the red Martian revolvers—those compact, deadly things that snap like a whip crack and leave only silence. We distributed what we could. Two of the women took the firearms; Thuvia was one of them. The weight of the revolver in her hand looked less like a weapon than like a long-denied utensil finally returned to its rightful owner.

With Thuvia guiding, we moved out swiftly but warily into the maze—winding corridors, steep inclines, great chambers hewn from the metal cliff itself. The air changed from passage to passage: sometimes close and warm with beast heat, sometimes cold enough to prickle the skin; always carrying the mineral tang of the gold walls and the stale breath of too many bodies. Now and again we pressed into shadowed recesses as footsteps approached, letting patrols pass—lesser therns and slaves moving with the blind obedience of those who have forgotten there is any other rhythm to life.

Our first destination, Thuvia told us, was a storeroom where arms and ammunition could be found. From there she would lead us toward the summit of the cliffs, and then—if we had both wit and steel enough—through the very heart of the Holy Therns’ stronghold to the world outside.

“And even then, O Prince,” she whispered to me as we went, “the arm of the Holy Thern is long. It reaches every nation. Secret temples lie hidden in the heart of every community. If we escape, word of us will run before us, and death will wait—lest we pollute the air with our blasphemies.”

We had been moving perhaps an hour when we entered a great chamber and found a man there—alone, waiting as if he had stepped from the wall itself. He was thern, but more than the common sort: besides leather trappings and jeweled ornaments, a great circlet of gold encircled his brow, set in its center with an immense stone.

The sight of it struck me like the smell of a long-remembered food—instant, vivid, dragging memory with it. I had seen such a gem once before, nearly twenty years ago, upon the breast of a little old man at the atmosphere plant. Two such jewels were known to exist, worn as insignia by the two old men entrusted with the engines that pump artificial air across Mars. That plant—and its secret—had given me the power to save a world from suffocation.

This stone matched the other in size—an inch across, perhaps—and in the way it broke light into nine rays: the seven familiar colors, and two beyond Earth’s seeing, colors that seemed to be felt more than looked at, like a tone too deep for the ear yet heavy in the chest.

The thern’s eyes narrowed to slits. “Stop!” he cried. “What means this, Thuvia?”

For answer she lifted her revolver and fired point-blank. The crack of it slammed through the chamber, harsh and final, the kind of sound that leaves the ears ringing and the heart leaping. The thern sank without a sound, dead before his body finished accepting what had happened.

“Beast,” Thuvia hissed, the word spat as if it burned her tongue. “After all these years—I am at last revenged.”

She turned toward me, explanation rising, but her eyes widened as they fixed on my face. She stepped closer with a sudden, fierce energy.

“O Prince,” she cried, “Fate is kind to us. The way is still difficult, but through this vile thing upon the floor we may yet win to the outer world. Notest thou not the resemblance between this Holy Thern and thyself?”

I looked down. The man was my stature. His features—eyes, nose, mouth—echoed mine unsettlingly, like a reflection seen in troubled water. Only his hair differed: a mass of flowing yellow locks, while mine is black and close-cropped.

“What of it?” I asked. “Would you have me, with my black hair, pose as a yellow-haired priest of this infernal cult?”

Thuvia smiled—a quick flash of practicality. She knelt beside the corpse, removed the golden circlet, and then, to my utter astonishment, lifted the entire scalp bodily from the dead man’s head. It came away like a cap, the yellow hair attached—a wig made of skin and tradition.

She rose and set it on my head, then crowned me with the circlet and its impossible gem.

“Now don his harness, Prince,” she said. “You may pass where you will in the realms of the therns. Sator Throg was a Holy Thern of the Tenth Cycle—mighty among his kind.”

As I bent to strip the dead man’s gear, I saw his skull beneath—smooth and bare as an egg.

“They are all thus from birth,” Thuvia said, seeing my surprise. “The race from which they sprang were crowned with luxuriant golden hair, but for many ages the present race has been entirely bald. The wig has become part of their apparel—and so important that it is deepest disgrace for a thern to appear in public without it.”

In another moment I stood garbed as a Holy Thern, wearing his harness and the circlet with the priceless jewel, the dead man’s false hair settling over my own like an adopted language—powerful, sanctioned, and rotten at the root. Two of the freed prisoners lifted Sator Throg’s body and bore it with us, as though we carried our own lie on our shoulders.

We continued toward the storeroom. Thuvia’s keys opened it without struggle, and within we found what we needed: arms, ammunition, the cold tools by which men convince other men to obey. We outfitted ourselves quickly, hands moving with the brisk economy of those who know they may not have time to correct mistakes.

Only then did my body demand its due. Exhaustion came down like a shutter. The air of the room seemed suddenly thick, and my limbs felt filled with sand.

I threw myself upon the floor, bidding Tars Tarkas to do likewise, and set two of the freed prisoners to keep careful watch. The last sound I remember, before sleep took me, was the soft, vigilant shifting of feet at the doorway—an attempt at quiet in a world that had forgotten what true quiet sounded like. In an instant I was asleep.

I cannot tell how long I lay on the storeroom floor—stone hard, smelling of old grain-dust and dried roots, of oil that had once been rubbed into weapons and never quite faded from the pores of the walls. Sleep came in ragged mouthfuls, like stolen bites snatched in a famine, and with it the dull ache of my wounds. Then sound tore through the darkness: an abrupt, ugly clamor—cries, the sharp metallic bark of rifles, and the long after-ring of the shots chasing themselves down the subterranean corridors until the echoes stacked and multiplied like clattering pans in a cavernous kitchen.

I sprang up as the fusillade ceased.

In the great doorway opposite stood a dozen lesser therns with rifles lowered, their white faces pinched between triumph and alarm, as though the very noise they had made accused them. Around me lay the bodies of our companions—strewn like butchered game after a careless slaughter—still, twisted, already cooling on the bare stone. Only Thuvia of Ptarth and Tars Tarkas had lived through the first raking fire, as I had, because we had slept flat upon the floor where the bullets passed high.

The therns stared at me, and I felt, as plainly as the weight of the diadem upon my brow, that I must become Sator Throg again in an instant or die as myself.

“What means this?” I roared, putting rage into my voice the way a cook throws salt into thin stew to give it body. “Is Sator Throg to be murdered by his own vassals?”

The nearest of them—one with a narrow chin and eyes too quick—half fell to his knees. “Have mercy, O Master of the Tenth Cycle!” he cried, and the others edged backward as though the threshold itself might swallow them and hide them from my wrath.

Thuvia, close at my elbow, breathed, “Ask them their mission here.”

“What do you here?” I demanded. “Speak!”

“Two from the outer world are at large within the dominions of the therns,” the fellow answered. “We sought them at the command of the Father of Therns. One was white with black hair; the other a huge green warrior,” and he let his gaze flick to Tars Tarkas with a suspicion that was almost hunger.

Thuvia stepped forward with the calm of one who has fed lions from her hand. “Here, then, is one of them,” she said, indicating the Thark. “And if you will look upon this dead man by the door perhaps you will recognize the other. It was left for Sator Throg and his poor slaves to accomplish what the guard could not—we have killed one and captured the other; for this had Sator Throg given us our liberty. And now in your stupidity you have come and killed all but myself, and like to have killed the mighty Sator Throg himself.”

The therns shifted, abashed, the way underlings do when they realize they have burned the very meal they were meant to serve.

“Had they not better throw these bodies to the plant men and then return to their quarters, O Mighty One?” Thuvia asked me, as though she were suggesting the clearing of refuse before it soured the air.

“Yes,” I said. “Do as Thuvia bids you.”

They moved among the dead, lifting them with the brisk efficiency of men accustomed to carrying meat from a kill. When one stooped over the body of Sator Throg himself, he hesitated; his fingers tightened, and I saw the tiny change in his face as his eyes measured that bald, shining dome, the set of the mouth, the angle of jaw that did not match the title I wore. He stole a glance at me—sideways, like a thief checking a doorway—then back down at the corpse. He said nothing, but as he rose, a faint curl touched his lips.

Again, as he bore Sator Throg away, he turned his head just enough to take me in from the corner of his eye. The last glimpse I had of his profile as he passed from sight was of a smile—quick, cunning, satisfied, like a man who has found a spice another has forgotten and knows he can sell it dear.

Then they were gone, and the storeroom felt emptier than before. The air held the raw, coppery scent of blood, and beneath it the stale dryness of stored provisions that had never been meant for mouths like ours. Tars Tarkas stood very still, massive as a pillar. Thuvia’s hand trembled once and then steadied at her side.

The therns’ marksmanship had erased our companions’ slim chance of reaching the world above. As soon as the last footsteps died down the corridor, Thuvia urged us onward.

“She noted him,” I saw; she had watched the bearer of Sator Throg as one listens for the first wrong note in a familiar song.

“It bodes no good for us, O Prince,” she said softly. “Even though he dared not accuse you in error, there be those above with power sufficient to demand closer scrutiny. And that would indeed prove fatal.”

I shrugged, though the motion pulled at my wounds. Pain traveled through me with every breath, and weakness from blood loss made the world tilt a fraction, like a tray held by a tired hand. No salves, no green Martian ointments here—none of the resinous, sharp-smelling balms that could knit torn flesh in an hour and leave a man ravenous for real food again. In this place there was only stone, old air, and the faint mineral tang of radium lamps.

Hopelessness came over me—thick as the darkness between Mars’ suns and moons. Yet the long yellow hair of the Holy Thern, blown by a stray draft, brushed my cheek, and with it came the reminder that disguise was still a tool in my hand, like a knife not yet dulled.

“If we act quickly,” I said, “we may still slip out before the alarm is general. What will that fellow do first, Thuvia? How long before they return for us?”

“He will go directly to the Father of Therns—old Matai Shang,” she replied. “He may have to wait for an audience, but since he is high among the lesser therns—as a thorian among them—it will not be long. If Matai Shang believes him, another hour will see the galleries and chambers, the courts and gardens, filled with searchers.”

“Then what we do must be done within an hour. What is the shortest way out of this celestial Hades?”

“Straight to the top of the cliffs, Prince,” she said, “and then through the gardens to the inner courts. From there our way lies within the temples and across them to the outer court. Then the ramparts.” Her voice tightened. “O Prince, it is hopeless. Ten thousand warriors could not hew a way to liberty from out this awful place.

“Since the beginning of time, little by little, stone by stone, have the therns been ever adding to the defences of their stronghold. A continuous line of impregnable fortifications circles the outer slopes of the Mountains of Otz. Within the temples behind the ramparts a million fighting-men are ever ready. The courts and gardens are filled with slaves, women, and children. None could go a stone’s throw without detection.”

“If there is no other way,” I said, “why dwell upon the difficulties? We must face them.”

Tars Tarkas’s deep voice rumbled. “Can we not better make the attempt after dark? There would seem to be no chance by day.”

“There would be a little better chance by night,” Thuvia answered, “but even then the ramparts are well guarded—possibly better than by day. There are fewer abroad in the courts and gardens, though.”

“What is the hour?” I asked.

“It was midnight when you released me from my chains,” she said. “Two hours later we reached the storeroom. There you slept for fourteen hours. It must now be nearly sundown again. Come—we will go to a nearby window in the cliff and make sure.”

She led us through winding corridors where the air changed from dry storage-staleness to the colder, cleaner scent of stone that has never known sunlight. At a sudden turn we came upon an opening that overlooked the Valley Dor.

The sun was setting to our right—a huge red orb sinking behind the western range of Otz. The light struck the eastern banks of Korus and turned them to a painted feast of color: crimson sward like spilled wine, forests glowing as though each leaf had been glazed. Yet the beauty carried the wrong music—too much silence, broken only by distant cries and the faint, sickly chorus of feeding.

A little below us a Holy Thern stood watch upon his balcony. His scarlet robe was drawn tight against the cold that comes like a blade on Mars the moment heat begins to drain away. Here the thin air holds little warmth; it is scorching under the sun and bitter when the sun is gone, with no gentle twilight to soften the change. The light does not linger; it vanishes as if someone snuffs a lamp, and the world is plunged into black.

Beneath the trees we saw herds of plant men feeding—adults standing tall upon toes and mighty tails, talons pruning every reachable leaf and twig with a methodical, rasping efficiency. Then I understood at last why the forest had seemed trimmed and kept, like a park tended by careful hands. It was tended—by mouths and claws.

Our eyes moved to the rolling Iss issuing from the base of the cliffs below. Presently a canoe slid out from the mountain, laden with lost souls. A dozen red Martians—highly civilized, richly accoutered even in ruin—crowded its narrow belly. They stepped onto the sward as though arriving at a festival they did not yet understand.

The herald below saw them as we did. He leaned out over his low rail and gave voice to a shrill, weird wail—a sound that tore at the nerves, a call that did not belong to any wholesome soundscape of wind through reed or water over stone. It was a signal meant to summon predators, and as it rang out it seemed to pollute the valley itself, turning the hush into a listening trap.

For an instant the plant men stood with ears stiff, then they poured from the grove toward the riverbank in great ungainly leaps. The party tried to defend itself—briefly, desperately—steel flashing once, twice, then swallowed beneath a mass of repulsive shapes. Silence followed, but it was not peace; it was only the sudden muffling of voices under bodies. Scores of sucking mouths fastened to flesh, and the sound of it carried faintly even up to us: wet, busy, obscene, like a kitchen where the work is all wrong.

I turned away, bile rising. Thuvia’s face was pale in the dying light.

“Their part is soon over,” she said. “The great white apes get the flesh when the plant men have drained the arteries. Look—they are coming now.”

Across the valley a dozen great white monsters ran toward the riverbank. Then the sun slipped beneath the horizon and darkness—so abrupt it could almost be touched—fell upon us. One moment the valley was a table spread in lurid color; the next it was a black bowl waiting.

Thuvia wasted no time. She led us back into the corridors, toward the passage that winds up through the cliffs to the surface thousands of feet above. The radium bulbs set far apart along the roof gave a cold, dim light—no warm flicker, no comforting crackle, only a thin glow like the aftertaste of metal.

Twice great banths wandered loose through the galleries and blocked our progress, their manes bristling, their breath a rank heat in the cool air. Each time Thuvia spoke a low word of command, and the snarling beasts slunk aside, sullen as dogs scolded away from a larder.

“If you can dissolve all our obstacles as easily as you master these fierce brutes,” I said, forcing a smile, “I can see no difficulties in our way. How do you do it?”

She laughed once—quickly—and then shuddered, as though the sound of her own laughter startled her in that place.

“I do not quite know,” she said. “When first I came here I angered Sator Throg because I repulsed him. He ordered me thrown into one of the great pits in the inner gardens. It was filled with banths. In my own country I had been accustomed to command. Something in my voice—something in my manner—cowed the beasts as they sprang to attack me. Instead of tearing me to pieces, as Sator Throg desired, they fawned at my feet.

“So greatly were Sator Throg and his friends amused that they kept me to train and handle the terrible creatures. I know them all by name. There are many wandering through these lower regions. They are scavengers. Many prisoners die here in their chains. The banths solve the problem of sanitation—in this respect.

“In the gardens and temples above they are kept in pits. The therns fear them. It is because of the banths that they seldom venture below ground except as their duties call them.”

Her words struck a spark in my mind. “Why not take a number of banths and set them loose before us above ground?” I asked. “If they run ahead, they may scatter guards and slaves alike.”

Thuvia’s eyes flashed. She laughed again, this time with something like grim delight. “It would distract attention from us, I am sure.”

As we climbed, she began calling in a low singsong voice—half purr, half chant—like a lullaby sung in a dialect the beasts alone remembered. The sound traveled strangely in those corridors; it did not echo harshly like gunfire, but seemed to settle into the stone and then rise again from somewhere behind, as though the tunnels themselves had learned the melody long ago. It reminded me that languages can survive in hidden places, even when men forget them—yet here the surviving tongue was one of command and hunger.

Soft padded feet sounded close behind us. I turned and saw a pair of green eyes shining from the dark. From a diverging tunnel a tawny shape crept toward us, sinew and stealth. Low growls and snarls answered Thuvia’s call from every side. One by one the banths joined, and to each she spoke a word—name, order, reassurance—until the beasts moved with us like a pack trained to heel.

Soon we were entirely surrounded by some fifty of them. Two walked close on either side of Thuvia like guards escorting a queen. Others brushed against my naked limbs, their fur slick and warm, their breath thick with the sour-sweet stink of meat. Now and then I caught the hungry side-glance they gave Tars Tarkas and me, as though our bodies were walking provisions.

It was a strange procession: the almost noiseless pad of bare feet and paws; the golden walls splashed with precious stones that caught and fractured the radium glow; the dim, clinical light overhead; the low constant accompaniment of growl and breath; the mighty green warrior towering above all; myself crowned with the diadem of a Holy Thern; and leading us, Thuvia—slender, steady, her voice the only thing holding back fifty throats.

Presently we approached a great chamber more brightly lighted. Thuvia halted us. Quietly she stole forward, glanced within, then motioned us after her.

The room was filled with specimens of the underworld’s cruel harvest: a heterogeneous collection of hybrids—the offspring of prisoners from the outer world, red and green and thern mingled. Constant confinement below ground had worked odd changes upon their skins; they resembled corpses more than living beings. Many were deformed, others maimed, and the majority, Thuvia whispered, were sightless. They lay sprawled about the floor, sometimes overlapping, sometimes in heaps, as though life had been poured out and left to congeal.

The banths’ nostrils flared. Their hunger thickened the air.

Picking our way carefully, we threaded a winding path across the chamber. The beasts sniffed at the helpless bodies as though at meat laid out too conveniently. The sight struck me with the grotesque force of old illustrations I had once seen of Dante’s Inferno—yet here there was no artistry to distance the horror, no poetic veil, only the physical fact of suffering reduced to a mute pile. Whatever languages these wretches had once spoken—whatever hearth-songs and market-calls and names for bread and broth—had been swallowed by this underground silence. Their mouths were open sometimes, but no word lived there.

Several times we passed entrances to other chambers similarly peopled, and twice again we were compelled to cross directly through them. In others were chained prisoners and beasts, iron biting into flesh, the faint metallic scent of rust and old sweat rising like a bitter steam.

“Why is it we see no therns?” I asked, my voice low now, as though loud speech might curdle the air.

“They seldom traverse the underworld at night,” Thuvia replied, “for then the great banths prowl the dim corridors seeking their prey. The therns fear the denizens of this cruel world they have fostered beneath their feet. The prisoners even sometimes turn upon them and rend them. The thern can never tell from what dark shadow an assassin may spring upon his back.

“By day it is different. Then the corridors and chambers are filled with guards; slaves from the temples above come by hundreds to the granaries and storerooms. All is life then. You did not see it because I led you not in the beaten tracks, but through roundabout passages seldom used. Yet it is possible we may meet a thern even yet. They do occasionally find it necessary to come here after the sun has set. Because of this I have moved with such caution.”

At last we reached the upper galleries without detection. The air changed—fresher, carrying the faint damp-green promise of growing things. Thuvia halted us at the foot of a short, steep ascent.

“Above us,” she said, “is a doorway which opens on to the inner gardens. I have brought you thus far. From here on for four miles to the outer ramparts our way will be beset by countless dangers. Guards patrol the courts, the temples, the gardens. Every inch of the ramparts themselves is beneath the eye of a sentry.”

I could not reconcile such vigilance with the superstition that cloaked this place. Who would dare come here to attack them? What enemy could pierce such mystery and stone?

I voiced the question as we climbed. Thuvia put her hand to the door.

“They fear the black pirates of Barsoom, O Prince,” she said, “from whom may our first ancestors preserve us.”

She swung the door open.

The smell of growing things greeted my nostrils—soil, leaf, night-blooming vines, the cool mineral sweetness of water. Cold air flowed across my cheek, clean after the underground staleness. Under the lurid light of the nearer moon the gardens lay spread—paths, shadows, temple roofs like pale cliffs.

The great banths sniffed the unfamiliar odors, their bodies tensing as if the new scents were seasoning to their hunger. Then, with a sudden rush, they broke past us and swarmed out across the gardens, low growls rolling ahead of them like distant thunder.

And at once—like a sour note struck in a sacred chant—a great cry arose from the roofs of the temples: alarm, warning, a sound taken up from point to point, running east and west from temple to court to rampart until it dwindled into dim echoes in the distance.

Tars Tarkas’s long-sword leaped from its scabbard with a whisper of steel.

Thuvia shrank, shuddering, to my side.

“What is it?” I asked.

Thuvia did not answer with words. She lifted her hand and pointed, the gesture as sharp as a knife-flash.

Above the white terraces and the clustered gardens, shadows skimmed the night like swallows over a watercourse. At first they were only darker blots against the sky; then came the brief, bitter strobing of fire—light bursting, vanishing—followed by the hard percussion of muskets. The sound rolled over the temples and clipped itself against the ramparts, and between each report the thin Martian air carried a higher, keening note: the hiss of something fast moving, the wing-slice of small fliers banking hard.

“The black pirates of Barsoom, O Prince,” Thuvia said.

They came in widening circles, dropping lower with each sweep, as though tasting the defenses, feeling where the therns’ line was soft. Their craft were mostly small—two or three men to a deck—built for darting in, biting, and darting out again. A few larger hulls held high above, steady as vultures, and from their keel batteries bombs fell with a dull, belly-deep thud that seemed to press the breath from one’s ribs.

The therns poured out from the temple mouths into the open courts and the garden walks. Their presence in the moonlit space drew the pirates like the scent of fresh cut fruit draws the small night-things of Earth—only this hunger was for blood and for screaming. A score of black fliers arrowed toward our portion of the grounds from every direction. The thern soldiery fired through the rifle-shields strapped to their weapons, their muzzle-flashes brief as the spark when a flint strikes steel. The answering volleys from above came in savage ripples, as if the air itself were being beaten with sticks.

Then, at some signal I could not see, the pirates near us rushed the ground together. Their craft struck, skidded, right into the midst of the thern line, and before the runners had even stilled the black men were already leaping down. They hit the therns with the fury of released storm.

Never had I watched fighting of that temper. I had believed the green hordes the last word in ferocity; yet these men fought as though battle were not duty but feast, and the garden their table. Under Mars’ two bright moons the scene lay naked and clear—the white-skinned, golden-haired therns locked hand to hand with ebony warriors whose bodies looked carved from polished night.

They fought among beauty without seeing it. A knot of them churned over a bed of pimalia until the blossoms were pulp and fragrance; a curved blade found a thern heart and left him folded at the foot of a statue cut from living ruby, moonlight pooling in its facets like wine; a dozen therns pressed one pirate back against an emerald bench inlaid with diamond patterning—delicate work meant to be studied in stillness, now smeared with grit and dark splashes that took the sparkle away.

A little apart stood Thuvia, Tars Tarkas, and I, as the tide of violence swung near and away again. When it eddied close enough, I could read faces and hands. The black pirates had lived in rumour for me—tales traded in Helium’s mess halls and whispered in the red man’s bazaars: that they descended from the lesser moon; that they struck at long intervals; that they took arms, ammunition, and girls; that their rites ended in sacrifice and the eating of their captives. Legends, always told with a shiver, the way old cooks will speak of famine years.

Now the legends wore flesh.

They were large men—six feet and more—with clean-cut, striking features. Their eyes sat wide and bright; the iris was so black it seemed to drink light, while the whites were clear as fresh-peeled fruit. Their frames were like our own in all but colour: skin of deep, lustrous ebony, which, strange as it may sound from one reared beneath a Southern sun, made them appear not less but more beautiful. Yet whatever the gods had given their bodies, their hearts appeared to hoard for themselves. There was a relish in their killing, a malign savor, as though each thrust were a note in some brutal song they had learned long ago.

All about the garden, pirate craft lay where they had landed—unguarded, abandoned, as though the men had no fear of losing them. Now and again a black warrior would burst from a temple doorway with a young woman in his arms. He would run straight for his flier and leap aboard, while comrades converged, blades out, to cover his escape. The therns would throw themselves after him, desperate to reclaim the girl; and in an instant the struggle would knot into a howling whirl, steel on steel, bodies stumbling and striking as in a mill race. Yet again and again the pirates prevailed, and the girl—miraculously untouched—would be borne away into the black air, her pale limbs and streaming hair a brief, helpless flare against the night.

The sound of similar fighting carried along the ribbon of thern territory—down the outer slopes of the Mountains of Otz—until it faded into distance, swallowed by the planet’s thinness. Thuvia watched the far flashes, her face pale but steady.

“Do you understand now, O Prince,” she asked, “why a million warriors guard the domains of the Holy Therns by day and by night? What you witness is but a repetition of what I have seen enacted a score of times during the fifteen years I have been a prisoner here. From time immemorial the black pirates have preyed upon the Holy Therns.

“Yet they never press their expeditions to the point where extermination threatens. It is as though they keep the therns as playthings—satisfying their lust for fighting, and collecting toll in arms and ammunition and in prisoners.”

I looked again at the deserted fliers scattered among the trampled beds and shattered shrubs.

“Why don’t the therns destroy the craft?” I demanded. “That would end this—or at least make the blacks less bold. They leave their vessels as though they were in their own hangars.”

“The therns do not dare,” Thuvia replied. “They tried it once, ages ago. The next night, and for a whole moon thereafter, a thousand great black battleships circled the Mountains of Otz and poured tons of projectiles upon the temples, gardens, and courts until every thern not killed was driven into the subterranean galleries. They live only by the sufferance of the black men. They were near extermination then; they will not risk it again.”

As her words died, another sound rose—a different rhythm, not human. It was a low, harsh coughing roar that made the hairs at the back of my neck lift. The banths we had loosed in the garden had at first hung back, cowed by gunfire and the sheer bedlam of shouted commands and clashing metal. But continuous noise can sour even the most patient beast; and blood—fresh, warm, plentiful—carries a message no language needs.

A great striped form shot from a clump of low shrubbery into the thick of the melee. The banth screamed, not in fear but in rapture of killing, as its talons found living flesh. As though that cry were a bell rung for a hunting pack, the rest came in a rush—huge bodies, shoulders rolling, jaws wide.

Panic snapped through both armies at once. Thern and pirate turned together against the common terror, for the banths chose neither side. They bore down men by the weight of their charge alone, bowling warriors aside like stalks in a storm. Paws rose and fell; fangs flashed; the air filled with the wet tearing sounds no poet should ever try to shape into words.

For a heartbeat the horror fascinated me—battle within battle, men who had moments before hacked one another now scrambling back to back in the trampled pimalia. Then the practical thought cut in: while the therns were occupied with teeth and claws, if ever we were to escape, it must be now.

I turned, searching for a path through the confusion to the ramparts. But as my eyes swept the garden, the simplest road to liberty lay everywhere around me—those unguarded fliers.

Why had I not seen it sooner? I knew the mechanism of every known make of Barsoomian air craft. For nine years I had served Helium’s navy; I had flown one-man scouts and commanded battleships whose bulk made earthly dreadnoughts seem toys. To think, with me, is to act.

I gripped Thuvia’s arm and whispered to Tars Tarkas to follow. Keeping low, we glided toward a small flier lying farther from the press of men and beasts. In an instant we were on its tiny deck, huddled close beneath the low rail. My hand found the starting lever by touch, as a baker’s hand finds familiar tools in the dark before dawn. I pressed my thumb upon the button that controls the repulsion ray—that exquisite Martian discovery that makes the air itself a road.

The craft rocked faintly. It did not rise.

Again I pressed—harder. Nothing.

Then a warning broke upon us: shrill cries and the slap of running feet. I glanced up and saw a dozen black pirates tearing toward us out of the churned confusion, faces lit by moon and malice. We had been seen.

I hammered the button. Still the flier clung to the ground as though nailed there.

And then understanding struck me with the cold clarity of a knife: we had chosen a two-man craft. Its ray tanks were charged for two ordinary men. The Thark’s great weight—Tars Tarkas’s massive frame—was an anchor stronger than any chain.

The pirates were almost upon us. There was no time for debate, no room for regret.

I pressed the button all the way in and locked it. I shoved the lever to high speed. Then I slipped from the deck to the ground, long-sword drawn, and stepped into the oncoming rush.

Behind me Thuvia cried out—one sharp, torn sound—and then, rising above the clamor as the flier at last answered the repulsion ray, I heard her voice, thin with height and anguish:

“My Prince, O my Prince; I would rather remain and die with—”

The rest drowned beneath the pirates’ shrieks as they fell upon me.

Yet I knew my ruse had worked. For the moment, at least, Thuvia and Tars Tarkas were beyond their reach, lifted away on the little craft I had forced into flight. The knowledge steadied my hand even as steel met steel.

For an instant the weight of numbers threatened to crush me. But again—as so often upon this savage planet—I found that my earthly strength, under Barsoom’s lesser gravity, made my arm a thing the foe could not easily measure. My blade became a whirling line of brightness, a net of death in the moonlight. The blacks pressed in with their shorter swords, then recoiled as men do when they have touched fire and learned respect.

Even so, I knew the truth: more would come. Greater numbers would wear me down, slip around my guard, take me from behind. In the end I must fall. A chill passed through me at the thought of dying here, nameless in this garden of false holiness, with no word ever reaching Dejah Thoris.

Then the old blood in me—Virginian, fighting, stubborn—rose and drove the dread out like smoke from a room. A fierce joy surged up, the battle-smile that has unsettled a thousand foes set itself upon my lips, and I went at them with a fury that those who lived would remember.

Still, even while striking, I kept my mind working, hunting an exit as a trapped man hunts a crack of light.

It came from the darkness behind me.

I had just disarmed a huge black fellow after a desperate struggle, and for a breath the others hung back, shoulders heaving, eyes glittering. One spoke in a tone that held hate, and beneath it, unwilling admiration.

“Thern,” he said, “you fight like a Dator. But for your detestable yellow hair and your white skin, you would be an honour to the First Born of Barsoom.”

“I am no thern,” I began, seizing the opening—thinking to throw them the truth and perhaps turn their blades with mine against the therns, if only for long enough to win freedom.

The words died as something heavy struck me between the shoulders with a resounding whack that nearly drove me to my knees.

I spun, expecting a new assailant. Instead, something swept past my shoulder and smashed one of the pirates full in the face, dropping him senseless onto the grass.

Above us, not fifty feet overhead, a fair-sized air vessel drifted—perhaps a ten-man cruiser. The object that had struck us was its trailing anchor, swinging as the ship rose slowly, the iron arms cutting the air like a pendulum.

In that instant the anchor became a ladder offered by fate.

The vessel was lifting, and the anchor had swung beyond the circle of black men and was climbing out of their reach. With a bound that left them staring, I sprang clean over them. Another leap—higher, farther—carried me to the rising iron. My fingers closed on it as it jerked upward.

I hung by one hand, dragged through the upper branches of the garden growth, leaves slapping my face, while below me the pirates howled and shook their swords. The ship veered west, then swung gracefully south. In another moment the Golden Cliffs fell away behind and I was carried out over the Valley Dor. Far beneath, six thousand feet down, the Lost Sea of Korus shimmered like hammered silver in the moons’ light, serene and indifferent to slaughter.

I hauled myself into a sitting position across the anchor’s arms and looked up along the chain into shadow. Perhaps the vessel was deserted. Perhaps it belonged to some friendly people who had wandered too near the battle and now fled. Its retreat gave that hope a thin but living flavor, like broth scraped from a nearly empty pot.

I needed certainty. Carefully—slowly, with every muscle measured—I began to climb the chain toward the deck.

One hand reached the rail. My fingers found it.

And then a fierce black face thrust over the side, and eyes filled with triumphant hate locked onto mine.

For an instant the black pirate and I held ourselves like two statues carved out of the same hard night—his face above the rail, mine below it, the thin anchor-chain biting my palm and forearm as though it meant to saw me from the world. The air was salt-cold and metallic with the ship’s oil, and the nearer moon had already slipped away; only the farther one washed the cruiser’s deck in pale light, turning every rivet into a pinprick of frost.

His handsome lips curled. Over the deck’s edge slid an ebony hand, unhurried, as if he were reaching for a spice-jar he knew by heart. The cold hollow eye of a revolver found the center of my forehead.

My free hand shot up, not for the weapon, but for his throat—warm, living, and corded as a rope. His hiss—“Die, cursed thern”—caught and broke in his windpipe under my fingers. His trigger tightened; the hammer fell; the chamber answered with a futile click.

Before he could roll to another round I dragged him so far over the edge that he had to drop the revolver and clutch the rail with both hands. My grip on his throat throttled any outcry. We fought in savage silence: he scrabbling to tear free, I hauling him toward the black drop beneath us. His face darkened to a livid bruise; his eyes bulged, pleading not for mercy but for air.

With a final effort he threw himself back upon the deck, and in that same fraction of time let go the rail to claw at my fingers. That was the moment I had been waiting for. I surged downward with all my weight. He swept clear of the deck, falling so close to my body that his plunge nearly tore me from my single hold on the chain and sent us both tumbling to the Lost Sea.

I did not loosen my grip. A single shriek from him, flung upward, would have been enough to rouse the deck. So I held on—choking, ever choking—while his frantic struggles dragged me lower along the chain, the iron links clinking softly like a miser counting coins. His contortions turned spasmodic, then smaller, then ceased. I released him only when he was utterly quiet. He vanished into the shadows below, swallowed without a splash I could hear.

I climbed again until my eyes cleared the rail.

The farther moon’s clear effulgence lay across the deck, bringing into sharp relief six or eight black men sprawled in sleep, their harness straps loose, their swords and pistols tossed near their hands the way a tired cook leaves knives too close to the board. The deck smelled of sweat and powder residue and old ship’s grease—human odor braided with machinery—so strong that even the cold could not thin it.

Huddled tight to the base of a rapid-fire gun sat a young white girl, securely bound. Her eyes were wide with the kind of fear that anticipates pain before it arrives. They fixed on me as my head rose above the edge.

Then relief flooded them—instant, unutterable—as though they had fallen upon the mystic jewel that sparkled in the center of the headpiece I had stolen. She did not speak; instead, with a tiny motion of her gaze, she warned me of the sleepers around her.

Noiselessly I gained the deck, letting my boots find the planks between scattered weapons. The girl nodded for me to come. When I bent close she whispered, urgent and practical as a housekeeper in a fire.

“Release me. I can aid you—and you will need all the aid available when they awaken.”

“Some of them will awake in Korus,” I replied, and I smiled as grimly as the night itself.

She understood at once. The cruelty of her answering smile struck me like a slap—cruelty on a beautiful face is an abomination, like blood spilled into milk. I cut her bonds quickly.

“Give me a revolver,” she breathed. “I can use that upon those your sword does not silence in time.”

I placed one in her hand. The metal looked too heavy and too dark against her skin, as if a soot-stained pot had been set on a lace cloth. Then I turned toward the ugly work waiting for us.

There was no room here for fine compunctions, no time for the delicate courtesies that these demons would neither value nor return. Stealthily I approached the nearest sleeper. When he woke he was already far on his journey to the bosom of Korus. His piercing shriek, born as consciousness returned, came faintly up from the depths as he fell—thin as steam escaping a kettle in the distance.

The second woke as my hand touched him. I succeeded in hurling him overboard, but his wild cry of alarm jerked the rest to their feet.

Five of them—five blades flashing up at once in the moonlight.

The girl’s revolver spoke in sharp staccato. One sank back to the deck and rose no more.

The others rushed me with drawn swords. She dared not fire again with me in the mêlée, but I saw her move—silent, cat-like—seeking an angle on their flank as one might circle a crowded hearth to reach the salt.

Then they were upon me.

The deck was too cramped for footwork; it was stand fast and pay in steel. For a few minutes it was the hottest fighting I had ever known. At first I took more than I gave—cuts that burned cold, a jar up the arms as blades met and skidded. The crashing raised a din that seemed obscene in the vast quiet of the night, as if some great factory had been dropped into a sleeping valley. Sparks leapt at each impact. Then I got beneath one fellow’s guard and saw him crumple to the planks.

The others redoubled their efforts. Steel on steel, a terrible clanging—noise without music, the kind that fouls the mind and makes even courage taste like ashes. Somewhere in it came the dull, sickening sound of bone parting under my blade, as unmistakable as cleaver meeting joint.

Three faced me now, and the girl was nearly in position to cut them down with lead.

What happened next came so fast I can scarce order it even now. The three rushed me together, plainly meaning to drive me back the few steps that would send me over the rail into the void. At the same instant the girl fired, and my sword arm made two swift moves.

One man dropped, a bullet through his brain. Another’s sword flew clattering across the deck and toppled over the edge as I disarmed him. The third went down with my blade buried to the hilt in his breast, three feet of it protruding from his back; and when he fell he wrenched the sword from my grasp.

Disarmed, I faced the last of them—bare-handed now, for his own sword lay somewhere thousands of feet below, lost in the Lost Sea. The new conditions pleased him. He smiled, teeth gleaming, and rushed at me with empty hands, trusting in the great muscles rolling under his glossy skin. He did not even trouble to draw the dagger at his harness.

I let him come almost upon me. Then I ducked under his arms and sidestepped right. Pivoting on my left toe, I drove a terrific right to his jaw. He dropped like a felled ox, the sound of his skull on the deck a final punctuation.

Behind me rang a low, silvery laugh—light as a spoon tapping crystal.

“You are no thern,” said the sweet voice of my companion, “for all your golden locks or the harness of Sator Throg. Never lived there upon all Barsoom before one who could fight as you have fought this night. Who are you?”

“I am John Carter, Prince of the House of Tardos Mors, Jeddak of Helium,” I said.

“And whom,” I added, “has the honor of serving been accorded me?”

She hesitated—only a breath—and then asked, as if the question mattered more than my name.

“You are no thern. Are you an enemy of the therns?”

“I have been in the territory of the therns for a day and a half,” I replied. “During that entire time my life has been in constant danger. I have been harassed and persecuted. Armed men and fierce beasts have been set upon me. I had no quarrel with the therns before, but can you wonder that I feel no great love for them now? I have spoken.”

She studied me for several minutes, gaze drawn-out and searching, as though she were weighing my words against some private lexicon where mercy and weakness were spelled the same. At last she seemed satisfied.

“I am Phaidor, daughter of Matai Shang, Holy Hekkador of the Holy Therns, Father of Therns, Master of Life and Death upon Barsoom, Brother of Issus, Prince of Life Eternal.”

As she spoke I saw that the black I had felled with my fist was stirring back toward consciousness. I sprang to him, stripped his harness away with quick hands, and bound his wrists behind him, then his ankles, and tied him to a heavy gun carriage. His skin was warm under the straps; he smelled of sweat and leather and the sour tang of fear held in check.

“Why not the simpler way?” Phaidor asked.

“I do not understand. What ‘simpler way’?” I said.

With a slight shrug of her lovely shoulders she made a gesture—hands casting an imaginary body over the side as carelessly as one might toss refuse from a kitchen pail.

“I am no murderer,” I told her. “I kill in self-defence only.”

She looked at me narrowly, divine brows puckering, and shook her head as though my words were in a dialect she refused to learn. She could not comprehend. My own Dejah Thoris had once found such restraint foolish and dangerous, but with her there had been tenderness behind the practicality—a sorrow for stern necessity. With Phaidor, I sensed something colder: not regret for danger spared, but disappointment at a pleasure withheld.

The bound man watched us now, fully awake. He was handsome—clean-limbed, powerful, an intelligent face with features so finely chiseled that even Adonis might have envied him. His eyes were alive with calculation.

The vessel, unguided, had been moving slowly across the valley. It was time to take the helm.

Only in the roughest way could I guess our location. I knew the Valley Dor lay far south of the equator—constellations told me so—but I lacked the charts and delicate instruments with which, in the Heliumite Navy, I had once reckoned position. Still, one conclusion was plain: northward would bring us quickest toward the more settled portions of the planet.

Beneath my hand the cruiser swung about with graceful obedience. I pressed the control of the repulsive rays and we soared up into space. With the speed lever pulled to the last notch, we raced north as we climbed ever farther above that terrible valley of death.

Below, as we passed at a dizzy height over the narrow domains of the therns, flashes of powder winked like angry fireflies, mute witness to battle along that cruel frontier. No sound came up to us. In the rarefied air, sound-waves died before they could reach our ears. The silence at that height was not peace; it was absence—like a world where every familiar voice has been erased and only the harsh instruments remain.

Cold seized us. Breathing grew difficult, each inhale thin and grudging. Phaidor and the bound black pirate stared at me; the girl’s gaze clung to my hands on the levers.

At length the girl spoke, calm as if announcing that a pot was about to boil over.

“Unconsciousness comes quickly at this altitude. Unless you are inviting death for us all you had best drop—and that quickly.”

There was no fear in her tone. Merely certainty.

I dropped the vessel to a lower level—and not a moment too soon. The girl swooned. The black, too, went slack and senseless. I held on, I think, only by sheer will; responsibility itself can be a kind of air.

We swung along low above the foothills of the Otz. Warmth returned, and air thickened enough to fill starved lungs. Soon the black opened his eyes, and a moment later the girl’s lids fluttered.

“It was a close call,” she said.

“It has taught me two things,” I replied.

“What?” she asked.

“That even Phaidor, daughter of the Master of Life and Death, is mortal,” I said, smiling.

“There is immortality only in Issus,” Phaidor answered. “And Issus is for the race of therns alone. Thus am I immortal.”

A fleeting grin passed across the black man’s features at her words—wicked, knowing. I did not then understand it. Later I was to learn, and she too, in a manner too horrible for any boast to survive.

“If the other thing you have just learned,” she continued, “has led to as erroneous deductions as the first you are little richer in knowledge than you were before.”

“The other,” I said, “is that our dusky friend does not hail from the nearer moon. He was like to have died at a few thousand feet above Barsoom. Had we continued the five thousand miles that lie between Thuria and the planet he would have been but the frozen memory of a man.”

Phaidor looked at him in astonishment.

“If you are not of Thuria, then where?” she demanded.

He shrugged and looked away, silent.

The girl stamped her little foot, imperious as a priestess at a feast table when a servant dares delay.

“The daughter of Matai Shang is not accustomed to having her queries remain unanswered,” she said. “One of the lesser breed should feel honored that a member of the holy race that was born to inherit life eternal should deign even to notice him.”

Again the black smiled.

“Xodar, Dator of the First Born of Barsoom, is accustomed to give commands, not to receive them,” he replied. Then, turning his eyes to me, “What are your intentions concerning me?”

“I intend taking you both back to Helium,” I said. “No harm will come to you. You will find the red men of Helium a kindly and magnanimous race, but if they listen to me there will be no more voluntary pilgrimages down the river Iss, and the impossible belief that they have cherished for ages will be shattered into a thousand pieces.”

“Are you of Helium?” he asked.

“I am a Prince of the House of Tardos Mors, Jeddak of Helium,” I replied, “but I am not of Barsoom. I am of another world.”

Xodar regarded me intently. “I can well believe that you are not of Barsoom,” he said at length. “None of this world could have bested eight of the First Born single-handed. But how is it that you wear the golden hair and the jewelled circlet of a Holy Thern?” He emphasized holy with a touch of irony that tasted like smoke.

“I had forgotten them,” I said. “They are the spoils of conquest,” and with a sweep of my hand I removed the disguise.

When his eyes fell on my close-cropped black hair they widened in surprise. He had expected the bald pate of a thern.

“You are indeed of another world,” he murmured, awe threading his voice. “With the skin of a thern, the black hair of a First Born and the muscles of a dozen Dators it was no disgrace even for Xodar to acknowledge your supremacy. A thing he could never do were you a Barsoomian.”

“You are travelling several laps ahead of me,” I said. “I glean that your name is Xodar. But whom, pray, are the First Born? What is a Dator? And why, if you were conquered by a Barsoomian, could you not acknowledge it?”

“The First Born of Barsoom,” he explained, “are the race of black men of which I am a Dator—Prince, as the lesser Barsoomians would say. My race is the oldest on the planet. We trace our lineage, unbroken, direct to the Tree of Life which flourished in the center of the Valley Dor twenty-three million years ago.

“For countless ages the fruit of this tree underwent gradual change, passing by degrees from true plant life to a combination of plant and animal. In the first stages the fruit possessed only the power of independent muscular action while the stem remained attached to the parent plant; later a brain developed in the fruit, so that hanging there by their long stems they thought and moved as individuals.

“Then, with the development of perceptions came comparison; judgments were reached and compared, and thus reason and the power to reason were born upon Barsoom.

“Ages passed. Many forms of life came and went upon the Tree of Life, but still all were attached to the parent plant by stems of varying lengths. At length the fruit tree consisted in tiny plant men—such as we now see reproduced in huge dimensions in the Valley Dor—still hanging to limbs and branches by the stems that grew from the tops of their heads.

“The buds from which the plant men blossomed resembled large nuts about a foot in diameter, divided by double partition walls into four sections. In one section grew the plant man; in another a sixteen-legged worm; in the third the progenitor of the white ape; and in the fourth the primeval black man of Barsoom.

“When the bud burst the plant man remained dangling at the end of his stem, but the three other sections fell to the ground, where the efforts of their imprisoned occupants to escape sent them hopping about in all directions.

“Thus, as time went on, all Barsoom was covered with these imprisoned creatures—hopping and skipping about the broad planet, falling into rivers, lakes, and seas to be spread farther still. Countless billions died before the first black man broke through his prison walls into the light of day. Prompted by curiosity, he broke open other shells, and the peopling of Barsoom commenced.

“The pure strain of the blood of this first black man has remained untainted by admixture with other creatures in the race of which I am a member; but from the sixteen-legged worm, the first ape, and renegade black man has sprung every other form of animal life upon Barsoom.

“The therns”—and he smiled maliciously—“are but the result of ages of evolution from the pure white ape of antiquity. They are a lower order still. There is but one race of true and immortal humans on Barsoom. It is the race of black men.

“The Tree of Life is dead, but before it died the plant men learned to detach themselves and roam the face of Barsoom with the other children of the First Parent. Now their bisexuality permits them to reproduce themselves after the manner of true plants, but otherwise they have progressed but little. Their actions are largely instinct. The brain of a plant man is but a trifle larger than the end of your smallest finger. They live upon vegetation and the blood of animals, and their brain is just large enough to direct them toward food and translate the sensations carried to it from eyes and ears. They have no sense of self-preservation and so are without fear. That is why they are such terrible antagonists in combat.”

As he spoke at such length—bound at my feet, yet discoursing like a lecturer in some ancient hall—I wondered at his purpose. It seemed too convenient, too leisurely for a captive.

Then I caught it: the faintest wandering of his gaze past me, the barest fraction of a second. He lay slightly forward of where I stood at the levers and thus faced the stern. At the end of his description his eyes fixed momentarily on something behind me, and a swift gleam of triumph lit them like a match in the dark.

We had slowed earlier; the Valley Dor was far astern, and I had let myself feel, for a breath, comparatively safe. Now I turned, sudden apprehension tightening my chest more than thin air ever had.

Close astern, forging silent and unlighted through the night, loomed the bulk of a great battleship.

Now I knew why the black pirate had held me with his tale as one holds a knife over a flame—steady, patient, waiting for the exact moment when the metal softens. For miles he had been listening with more than ears: feeling, through hull and rigging, the faint pressure-change of a greater craft above us. Had I not caught that single telltale glance of his into the upper dark, the battleship would have been directly overhead the next heartbeat, and the boarding party—already dangling in their harness from the leviathan’s keel like seedpods on a winter reed—would have dropped to our deck and snuffed my rising hope as cleanly as a cook smothers a flare with a lid.

There is a music to aerial war. It is not the cheap brassy blare of drums, but the thin, exact whine of propellers, the timbre of strained cables, the way a ship’s skin answers the wind with a different pitch when she is being stalked. Industrial noise, unthinking and constant, deadens the mind; this noise was the opposite—purposeful, edged, each note a warning. I had been too long at it to be bewildered.

In the same instant I threw my levers, reversed the engines, and dropped our little vessel a sheer hundred feet. The air snapped cold against my face. Above, through the lattice of rigging, I saw the black shapes of the boarders swaying under the battleship’s belly as she raced over us, close enough that the churn of her huge propellers beat the air into a savage, metallic roar that pressed against my teeth.

I rose again at a sharp angle and shoved my speed lever to its last notch. Like a bolt loosed from a crossbow, my craft shot her steel prow straight at the whirring propellers of the giant. If I could so much as kiss those blades, the beast would be crippled for hours—long enough, perhaps, for a man to slip a noose.

At the same instant the sun tore itself up over the horizon, as if some unseen hand had pulled back a cover from a platter, and the light fell hard upon a hundred grim, black faces leaning over the battleship’s stern to watch us die. Their shout came down like a wave of hot breath. Orders were barked—sharp, staccato syllables—but too late.

We struck.

The crash was a brutal, satisfying thing: iron on iron, the scream of shearing metal, the sudden stutter in the battleship’s song as blades shattered and the whole great body shivered. I reversed instantly, but our prow had bitten and wedged in the wound we’d made. For one second—only one—we hung there, nailed to the enemy like a skewer through meat.

That one second was plenty. Black devils poured down and across, a flood of bodies and blades. There was no fight. There was no space to fight. We were drowned by numbers.

I felt steel at my throat—and then a command, cool as a ladle in a scalding pot, checked them.

“Secure them,” said Xodar. “Do not injure them.”

Several had already cut him loose. He came to me himself, smiling as though he were doing a courteous duty, and took my weapons. He saw to my binding, and in his eyes I read the confidence of a man tying a parcel: a tidy knot, a job done. At least he thought it secure. It would have been—had I been a Martian. The puny strands around my wrists were like trussing string. When the time came, I could break them as one snaps cotton between wet fingers.

They bound the girl as well, and fastened us together with a rope, close enough that each breath and shift of weight must be shared.

Our craft was hauled alongside the disabled battleship, and we were transferred to her deck. A thousand black men manned that great engine of destruction. Their bodies were beautiful in the way a well-made blade is beautiful—clean lines, hard muscle—yet their eyes held an ugliness that chivalry would have softened in the red men of Barsoom. The deck swarmed as far as discipline allowed, all of them straining for a look at the captives.

The girl’s beauty drew out their worst appetites. Their comments were coarse, their laughter like the clatter of pans in a kitchen where no one respects the meal. I stood there in thern harness and metal—gear that still carried the faint dry reek of the dead man who had worn it—and my close-cropped black hair and thern complexion became their sport. When Xodar told his fellow nobles of my fighting ability and strange origin, questions flew at me, fast and hungry. The fact that I wore a thern’s harness taken from a thern slain by one of my party set me a notch higher in their estimation; hatred makes quick alliances.

Without exception the blacks were handsome. Their officers were dazzling—wondrous magnificence, harnesses crusted with gold, platinum, silver, and stones until the leather beneath might as well not exist. The commanding officer wore a mass of diamonds that swallowed his chest in cold fire; against the ebony of his skin they burned with an almost indecent brilliance. The scene was intoxicating in its barbaric splendour: polished skeel wood underfoot; cabins of gloriously grained sorapus, inlaid with precious metals and jewels in intricate patterns; burnished gold handrails; guns shining like newly honed knives.

Then they took us below.

Still bound, we were thrown into a small compartment with a single port-hole. The door barred behind us. The ship drifted lazily south, and through the port-hole I could see the world sliding by in pale bands. Above and beyond the metal walls we could hear men working on the broken propellers—hammering, grating, the harsh, repetitive din of repair. It was the kind of noise that, if it became your daily weather, would make you cruel without noticing.

For a time neither of us spoke. My thoughts went to Tars Tarkas and to Thuvia. Even if they had slipped pursuit, they must eventually meet either red men or green—and as fugitives from the Valley Dor they could expect little besides swift death. How I wished I had gone with them. It seemed to me I could not fail to make the intelligent red men see the wicked deception that had been kneaded into their lives like poison into bread dough, until even those who tasted bitterness called it holy. Tardos Mors would believe me; of that I was certain. Dejah Thoris would believe me; I did not even doubt it. And there were warriors—red and green—who would face the doom of all their superstitions for my sake, as Tars Tarkas would. Where I led, they would follow.

My danger lay in this: if I ever escaped the black pirates, it might only be into the hands of unfriendly red or green men—and then it would be short shrift. Yet that danger seemed almost a luxury to consider; my chances of escaping the blacks were remote as rain in a dead canal.

The rope between the girl and me allowed only a few feet of separation. When we had entered the compartment we sat on a low bench beneath the port-hole—the only furniture. The bench was sorapus, smooth and close-grained, with the faint resin-sweet smell of cut wood trapped beneath old oil. Floor, ceiling, and walls were of carborundum aluminum, light and impenetrable, the cold skin of warships.

I had been staring out through the port-hole when I felt her gaze. I turned.

Phaidor was looking at me with an expression I had not seen on her face before. In that dim room, with the ship’s noises throbbing around us, she was very beautiful. At once her white lids dropped, veiling her eyes, and a delicate flush touched her cheek. Embarrassed at being caught staring at a “lesser creature,” I thought.

“Do you find the study of the lower orders interesting?” I asked, and laughed.

She looked up again and gave a nervous, relieved little laugh of her own. “Oh, very,” she said, “especially when they have such excellent profiles.”

It was my turn to flush—though I did not let it show. I suspected mockery. Yet I admired a brave heart that could find humour on the road to death, and so I laughed with her.

“Do you know where we are going?” she asked.

“To solve the mystery of the eternal hereafter, I imagine,” I replied.

“I am going to a worse fate than that,” she said, and shuddered—a small movement, but it shook something loose in the air between us.

“What do you mean?”

“I can only guess,” she said. “No thern damsel—of all the millions stolen by black pirates during the ages they have raided our domains—has ever returned to tell what she found among them. That they never take a man prisoner lends strength to the belief that the fate of the girls is worse than death.”

“Is it not a just retribution?” I could not help but ask.

“What do you mean?”

“Do not the therns themselves do likewise with the poor creatures who take the voluntary pilgrimage down the River of Mystery? Was not Thuvia for fifteen years a plaything and a slave? Is it less than just that you should suffer as you have caused others to suffer?”

“You do not understand,” she replied, and there was genuine impatience in her voice, the impatience of someone who has never had to justify the recipe she inherited. “We therns are a holy race. It is an honour to a lesser creature to be a slave among us. Did we not occasionally save a few of the lower orders that stupidly float down an unknown river to an unknown end, all would become prey of the plant men and the apes.”

“But do you not encourage the superstition in the outer world?” I pressed. “That is the wickedest of your deeds. Can you tell me why you foster this cruel deception?”

“All life on Barsoom,” she said, “is created solely for the support of the race of therns. How else could we live, did the outer world not furnish our labour and our food? Think you a thern would demean himself by labour?”

“It is true then,” I asked, sickening, “that you eat human flesh?”

She looked at me with pitying commiseration, as one might look at a child who does not know the difference between grain and chaff. “Truly we eat the flesh of the lower orders. Do not you also?”

“The flesh of beasts, yes,” I said, “but not the flesh of man.”

“As man may eat of the flesh of beasts,” she replied calmly, “so may gods eat of the flesh of man. The Holy Therns are the gods of Barsoom.”

Disgust tightened my throat. I did not need to speak; my face must have answered for me.

“You are an unbeliever now,” she continued gently, and the gentleness made it worse, for it carried the power of an unexamined language—words that have ruled so long they do not know they are weapons. “But should we be fortunate enough to escape the black pirates and come again to the court of Matai Shang, I think we shall find an argument to convince you of the error of your ways. And—” She hesitated. “Perhaps we shall find a way to keep you as—as—one of us.”

Again her eyes dropped; again that faint colour came. I did not understand. Not then. Dejah Thoris used to say that in some matters I was a simpleton, and perhaps she was right.

“I fear I would ill requite your father’s hospitality,” I answered, “since the first thing I should do, were I a thern, would be to set an armed guard at the mouth of the River Iss to escort the poor deluded voyagers back to the outer world. Also I should devote my life to exterminating the hideous plant men and their horrible companions, the great white apes.”

She looked truly horror-struck. “No, no,” she cried. “You must not say such terribly sacrilegious things—you must not even think them. Should they ever guess you entertained such thoughts, should we chance to regain the temples of the therns, they would mete out a frightful death to you. Not even my—my—” She flushed and stumbled over the word again, as though her own tongue betrayed her. “Not even I could save you.”

I said no more. Evidently it was useless. She was more steeped in superstition than the Martians of the outer world. They worshipped a beautiful hope: love and peace and happiness beyond death. The therns—who called themselves gods—reverenced the plant men and apes as abodes of their departed dead. Even their prayers tasted of old blood.

Then the door opened and Xodar entered.

He smiled pleasantly at me; when he smiled his expression was kindly—anything but cruel or vindictive. “Since you cannot escape under any circumstances,” he said, “I cannot see the necessity for keeping you confined below. I will cut your bonds and you may come on deck. You will witness something very interesting, and as you never shall return to the outer world it will do no harm to permit you to see it. You will see what none other than the First Born and their slaves know—the subterranean entrance to the Holy Land, to the real heaven of Barsoom.

“It will be an excellent lesson for this daughter of the therns,” he added, “for she shall see the Temple of Issus, and Issus, perchance, shall embrace her.”

Phaidor’s head went high. “What blasphemy is this, dog of a pirate?” she cried. “Issus would wipe out your entire breed an’ you ever came within sight of her temple.”

“You have much to learn, thern,” replied Xodar, and an ugly smile cut across his kindness like a crack in glazed pottery. “Nor do I envy you the manner in which you will learn it.”

On deck, to my surprise, the vessel passed over a great field of snow and ice. As far as the eye could reach, nothing else—white, wind-scoured, hard as salt left too long in a pan. There could be only one answer: we were above the south polar ice cap. Only at the poles of Mars does ice or snow cling to the planet.

The world below held no sign of life. We were too far south even for the fur-bearing beasts the Martians love to hunt. Yet the silence out there was not true silence: it was the hush of open cold, broken by our own engines, by the creak of harness, by the constant grind of men at work repairing the wounds we had made. The ship’s noise laid itself over the ice like a dirty cloth, and I found myself hating it—not for fear, but for what it did to the mind: made everything feel owned.

Xodar stood beside me at the rail.

“What course?” I asked.

“A little west of south,” he replied. “You will see the Otz Valley directly. We shall skirt it for a few hundred miles.”

“The Otz Valley!” I exclaimed. “But, man, is not there where lie the domains of the therns from which I but just escaped?”

“Yes,” said Xodar. “You crossed this ice field last night in the long chase you led us. The Otz Valley lies in a mighty depression at the south pole. It is sunk thousands of feet below the surrounding country, like a great round bowl. A hundred miles from its northern boundary rise the Otz Mountains, which circle the inner Valley of Dor, in the exact centre of which lies the Lost Sea of Korus. On the shore of that sea stands the Golden Temple of Issus in the Land of the First Born. It is there we are bound.”

As I watched the ice end abruptly in a sheer wall thousands of feet high, my understanding hardened. Below the wall stretched a level valley broken by low hills and small forests; tiny rivers ran there, born from meltwater at the barrier’s base. To cross that frozen, wind-swept waste alone and on foot would be impossible.

“Only by air boat could the journey be made,” I said aloud.

“It was thus that one did escape the therns in bygone times,” said Xodar, with a touch of pride, “but none has ever escaped the First Born.”

We passed above a deep, canyon-like rift stretching from the ice wall across the valley as far as the eye could follow.

“That is the bed of the River Iss,” said Xodar. “It runs far beneath the ice field and below the level of the Valley Otz, but its canyon is open here.”

Presently I pointed out what looked like a village.

“It is a village of lost souls,” he answered, laughing. “This strip between the ice barrier and the mountains is neutral ground. Some turn off from their voluntary pilgrimage down the Iss and, scaling the canyon walls, stop in the valley. Also a slave now and then escapes from the therns and makes his way hither.

“The therns do not attempt recapture. There is no escape from this outer valley, and they fear our patrolling cruisers too much to venture from their own domains.

“The poor creatures here are not molested by us since they have nothing we desire, nor are they strong enough to give us an interesting fight—so we leave them alone. There are several villages. They increase little, since they are always warring among themselves.”

The words struck like a sour taste: a place where the language of hope had died and been replaced with the harsh, small vocabulary of survival. “Lost souls”—and by whose lexicon were they lost? The dominant tongue always names the world to suit itself; it sells cruelty as inevitability, like merchants selling painted prayer.

Now we swung a little north of west, leaving the valley. Shortly I saw over our starboard bow what appeared to be a black mountain rising from the desolate waste of ice, low and flat-topped, a truncated cone. Xodar went off to attend to duty. Phaidor and I stood alone at the rail. She had not spoken once since we came on deck.

“Is what he has been telling me true?” I asked her quietly.

“In part, yes,” she replied. “That about the outer valley is true, but what he says of the location of the Temple of Issus in the centre of his country is false. If it is not false—” She caught herself, breath tightening. “Oh, it cannot be true. It cannot be true. For if it were true, then for countless ages have my people gone to torture and ignominious death at the hands of their cruel enemies instead of to the beautiful Life Eternal we have been taught to believe Issus holds for us.”

“As the lesser Barsoomians of the outer world have been lured by you to the terrible Valley Dor,” I said, “so may it be the therns themselves have been lured by the First Born to an equally horrid fate.”

“It would be a stern retribution,” I added, “but a just one.”

“I cannot believe it,” she whispered.

“We shall see,” I said, and our words fell away, for the black mountain ahead seemed to pull every thought toward it like gravity.

As we neared the dark cone, the vessel’s speed was diminished until we barely moved. We topped the crest, and below us yawned the mouth of a huge circular well, bottom lost in inky blackness. The diameter was a thousand feet. The walls were smooth, black basalt.

For a moment the battleship hovered directly above the centre of that gaping void, poised like a spoon over a pot whose contents you do not dare to taste. Then she began to settle into the chasm. Darkness rose to meet us. Lights snapped on, casting dim halos, and down we went—down and down—until I felt we were falling into the very bowels of Barsoom.

For quite half an hour we descended, the ship’s engines droning, the sound trapped and multiplied by stone. Then the shaft ended abruptly in the dome of a mighty subterranean world. Below us rolled the billows of a buried sea. A phosphorescent radiance lit everything with a sickly, beautiful glow like the sheen on fish-skin. Thousands of ships dotted the water. Little islands rose here and there, supporting strange, colourless vegetation, pale as blanched roots grown without sun.

Slowly, with majestic grace, the battleship dropped to the surface and rested on the water. The great air propellers had been drawn in and housed during the descent; in their place smaller but more powerful water propellers ran out. When they began to revolve, the ship took up her journey once more, riding this new element as buoyantly as she had the air.
