Phaidor and I stood dumbfounded. Neither of us had heard—nor dreamed—such a world existed beneath Barsoom’s crust. Nearly every vessel was war craft. There were a few lighters and barges, but none of the great merchantmen that ply the upper air between outer cities—no traffic of ordinary life, no domestic errands, only the machinery of power.

“Here is the harbour of the navy of the First Born,” said a voice behind us. We turned. Xodar watched us with an amused smile.

“This sea,” he continued, “is larger than Korus. It receives the waters of the lesser sea above. To keep it from filling past a certain level we have four great pumping stations that force the oversupply back into the reservoirs far north from which the red men draw the water that irrigates their farm lands.”

A new light burst in my mind. Those “miraculous” columns of water spurting from solid rock in the reservoir walls of the red men—the learned had never fathomed their source. In time they had ceased to ask, as people cease to ask the origin of a word they’ve spoken all their lives. Here, beneath them, the answer laboured in the dark, unseen and uncredited. The old world above drank and called it blessing; the hidden world below controlled the flow and called it dominion.

We passed several islands holding circular buildings, roofless, pierced midway with small, heavily barred windows. They looked like prisons, made more so by armed guards squatting outside or patrolling the beaches. Most islets were small, but presently we sighted a larger one ahead. This proved our destination. The great ship made fast against a steep shore.

Xodar motioned us to follow. With half a dozen officers and men, we left the battleship and crossed toward a large oval structure a couple hundred yards inland.

“You shall soon see Issus,” said Xodar to Phaidor. “The few prisoners we take are presented to her. Occasionally she selects slaves from among them to replenish the ranks of her handmaidens. None serves Issus above a single year.”

He said it plainly, but a grim smile gave his words a cruel aftertaste, like sweetness laid over rot.

Phaidor—so proud a moment ago—began to doubt in earnest. She clung close to me, no longer the daughter of the Master of Life and Death, but a frightened young girl held in the hands of enemies who spoke a different dialect of power.

The building we entered was roofless. In its centre lay a long tank of water set below the floor like a natatorium pool. Near one side floated an odd black object. For an instant I wondered if it were some beast of these buried seas—or a queer raft.

When we reached the edge of the pool above it, Xodar called out in a strange tongue. Immediately a hatch cover was raised from the object’s surface and a black seaman sprang up from its depths.

Xodar addressed him. “Transmit to your officer the commands of Dator Xodar. Say to him that Dator Xodar, with officers and men, escorting two prisoners, would be transported to the gardens of Issus beside the Golden Temple.”

“Blessed be the shell of thy first ancestor, most noble Dator,” replied the man. “It shall be done even as thou sayest.” He raised both hands, palms backward, above his head in the common Barsoomian salute, and disappeared back into the craft.

A moment later an officer, resplendent in rank and ornament, appeared on deck and welcomed Xodar. We followed aboard and below.

The cabin extended across the ship with port-holes on either side below the water line. As soon as we were all down, commands were given; the hatch closed and was secured; and the vessel began to vibrate with the rhythmic purr of its machinery—a softer, more intimate sound than the airship’s roar, like a great animal breathing through its ribs.

“Where can we be going in such a tiny pool of water?” Phaidor asked, voice thin.

“Not up,” I replied. I had noted that though the building was roofless, it was covered by a strong metal grating. “Then where?” she insisted.

“From the look of the craft,” I said, “we are going down.”

Phaidor shuddered. The old seas of Barsoom had been a tradition for so long that even a girl born within sight of the planet’s only remaining open water carried the common Martian terror of deep water—a fear inherited like a story told so often the body remembers it as truth.

Soon the sensation of sinking became unmistakable. We dropped swiftly. We could hear the water rushing past the port-holes, and in the dim light filtering through, the swirling eddies were visible, twisting like pale smoke in a pot.

Phaidor’s hand clenched on my arm. “Save me,” she whispered. “Save me and your every wish shall be granted. Anything within the power of the Holy Therns to give will be yours. Phaidor—” Her voice stumbled, caught on its own name, and then came in a very low breath, as though she feared the words themselves. “Phaidor already is yours.”

I felt a sudden, genuine pity—pity for a child raised on a language that taught her she was divine, only to find herself as helpless as any other when the grammar of power shifted. I placed my hand over hers where it lay on my arm, intending comfort.

She misunderstood.

With a swift glance about the cabin to assure herself that we were alone, she threw both arms about my neck and dragged my face down to hers.

Her confession—wrenched from her by fear as the vessel sank—struck me with a double edge: pity, and a sharp, private shame. I had given her some careless spark to nurse into flame, and now it burned her. I have never been skilled at the soft arts. Give me a blade, a broken door to hold, a plan to force—those are languages I speak without stumbling. But the language of a young woman’s hope, offered bare-handed in the dark? I found myself clumsy as a man wearing another’s boots.

So I did the only honest thing left, and did it badly.

I loosened her fingers from my neck, and while I still held her hands—warm, trembling, too alive for this steel room and its growing pressure—I told her of Dejah Thoris. I spoke her name as one speaks the name of bread after famine, of water after thirst: not to boast, not to wound, but because it was the one truth in me that would not bend. Of all the women of two worlds I had known, admired, fought beside, or served, she alone had taken my heart entire.

The tale did not soothe Phaidor.

She jerked from me as though my words had stung her skin. In an instant the frightened girl was gone and something fierce stood in her place—beautiful as a drawn sword, and as dangerous.

“Dog,” she breathed, and the word was not merely insult but verdict, flung with the cold precision of a knife into meat. “Dog of a blasphemer! Think you Phaidor, daughter of Matai Shang, supplicates? She commands. What is your puny outer-world passion for the vile creature you chose in your other life?

“I have glorified you with my love, and you have spurned me. Ten thousand deaths—unthinkably atrocious—could not atone for this affront. The thing you call Dejah Thoris shall die the most horrible of them all. You have sealed her warrant.

“And you—you shall be the meanest slave in the service of the goddess you have attempted to humiliate. Tortures and ignominies shall be heaped upon you until you grovel at my feet, begging for the boon of death.

“And then, in my gracious generosity, I shall grant it. From the high balcony of the Golden Cliffs I shall watch the great white apes tear you asunder.”

She spoke it as if reciting a household order—this first, then that, the course of a day’s work laid out from dawn to dusk. Only the work was agony, and the day ended in dismemberment. It was astonishing, how vengeance could be arranged with the neatness of a menu.

But she had forgotten one ingredient: where we were.

I pointed, without malice and without triumph, to the nearest port-hole. The water beyond it was not merely dark; it was alive with a slow, swirling blackness that pressed like a hand against glass. The reminder broke through her rage as suddenly as cold through thin cloth. She looked once—and crumpled, as if the bones in her had been removed. She fell to a low bench, buried her face in her arms, and sobbed—not as a goddess who had proclaimed doom, but as a very unhappy child who has realized that threats cannot stop a flood.

Down we went. Down, down, until the heavy glass warmed under the heat of the water without, and the air seemed thicker in my lungs. Then the descent ceased. The propellers took up their churn at the stern, and we were driven forward at speed through a darkness that the small port-lights could only bruise.

A powerful beam from the submarine’s nose cut the black, and in its cone I saw rock walls close on either side—a narrow, tube-like passage, raw stone smoothed by ancient currents. The sound of our passage became its own strange weather: the muffled throb of engines, the whispering scrape of water, the deep, bone-conducted vibration that made the teeth feel as though they remembered thunder.

Then the propellers stopped. We hung, still, as though the world were holding its breath. And at once we began to rise swiftly toward light.

We surfaced in a small subterranean vault. In the center lay the pool where the submarine floated as we had first seen her, only her black back showing, sleek as an animal’s. Around the pool ran a level platform, and above it the cave walls rose straight for a few feet before arching toward a low roof. The ledge was pierced by a number of entrances into dimly lit passageways, each mouth of tunnel exhaling its own damp, mineral breath.

Xodar entered with his men and gestured curtly. “Come.”

We were taken along one corridor and halted before a steel cage at the bottom of a shaft that rose above us so far the eye could not follow it. The cage was a common Barsoomian elevator car, operated by immense magnets suspended at the top. The thing had no ropes, no visible means of lift—only the unseen grip of controlled force. When the door clanged shut behind us and the current took, my stomach seemed to lag a heartbeat behind my body. In the low gravity of Mars there was little to resist the pull; the car shot upward with a sickening speed.

Almost as soon as we began we were slowing, so rapid had been our ascent, and then we emerged into a small structure that housed the upper terminus.

And stepped out into beauty so sudden it felt like being struck.

I have no words fit for it, and the languages of Earth men offer me none. Yet my senses remember, and that is a kind of vocabulary. The ground beneath my feet was a scarlet sward, soft as new-milled grain between the fingers. Trees rose on ivory stems and bore purple blossoms that seemed to drink light and give it back altered—richer, stranger. Walks wound through the gardens, paved not with humble stone but with crushed rubies, emerald, turquoise, and—yes—diamonds, as though someone had taken a jeweler’s tray and scattered it underfoot as casually as a cook strews salt.

There was a temple of burnished gold, hand-wrought with designs so delicate they seemed less hammered than grown. And the colors—there were rays there my eyes could register yet my mind could not name, hues without Earthly cousins, scintillations as if the air itself had been powdered with unknown gems. Even I, long accustomed to the barbaric splendors of Martian courts, stood amazed.

Phaidor’s eyes widened, her lips parting on a whisper. “The Temple of Issus.”

Xodar watched us with his grim smile, half amusement, half malicious gloating.

The gardens swarmed with brilliantly trapped black men and women—the First Born—moving as if the place belonged to them by the oldest right. Among them glided red and white females, serving, carrying, attending, their slavery made to look like ornamentation. It was as though whole cities and sanctuaries had been plundered not for gold but for living beauty, to be set here like captive birds in gilded cages.

Through this spectacle we were driven toward the temple.

At the main entrance a cordon of armed guards halted us. Xodar spoke to an officer who came forward; together they went within the golden doors and were gone long enough that the waiting grew heavy. When they returned, the officer announced that Issus desired to look upon the daughter of Matai Shang, and upon the strange creature from another world who had once been a Prince of Helium.

We were led through corridors that did not merely gleam but seemed to sing with reflected light—endless halls, apartments fit for kings, floors that muffled the footfall as if the very stones had been taught reverence. Somewhere, too, beneath the splendor, there was a constant sound: the distant hiss of fountains, the soft thunder of water moving through hidden channels. It should have been soothing; instead it felt like an engineered hush, a curated soundscape meant to smother thought. Even here, where beauty was weaponized, silence was part of the rule.

At last we were halted in a spacious chamber at the temple’s center. An officer went to a great door and gave some signal. It opened, and a richly trapped courtier emerged.

Then came the humiliation.

We were directed to get down on our hands and knees with our backs toward the room we were to enter. The doors swung wide. Under penalty of instant death we were warned not to turn our heads, and commanded to back into the Presence of Issus.

There are degradations that sting more than wounds. My hands pressed to the cold floor; my knees scraped; I moved like an animal trained to fear a whip. Only my love for Dejah Thoris—and the stubborn hope that I might yet see her again—kept me from rising, turning, and dying as a man should: facing my foe.

We crawled in that disgusting fashion for what seemed a couple of hundred feet before our escort halted us.

“Let them rise,” said a voice behind us—thin, wavering, yet shaped by long habit into command.

“Rise,” said the escort, “but do not face toward Issus.”

“The woman pleases me,” the voice continued after a pause. “She shall serve me the allotted time. The man you may return to the Isle of Shador, which lies against the northern shore of the Sea of Omean. Let the woman turn and look upon Issus, knowing that those of the lower orders who gaze upon the holy vision of her radiant face survive the blinding glory but a single year.”

From the corner of my eye I watched Phaidor. The blood left her face until she looked carved from pale stone. Slowly—very slowly—she turned, as though drawn by a force that was not physical but absolute. She stood close enough that her bare arm brushed mine, and when her eyes met what she was allowed to see, a shudder ran through her so hard I felt it tremble through that touch.

“It must be dazzling loveliness indeed,” I thought bitterly, “to strike such awe into one as radiant as Phaidor.”

“Let the woman remain,” said the voice. “Remove the man. Go.”

A heavy hand fell upon my shoulder. I dropped again to my hands and knees and crawled out of that room like a beaten cur. It had been my first audience with a deity, and I confess that what impressed me most was the ridiculous figure I cut on my marrow bones.

Once the doors closed behind us, I was bid to rise. Xodar joined me, and together we retraced our steps toward the gardens.

“You spared my life when you easily might have taken it,” he said at last, and his tone held an odd strain, as though pride and gratitude fought for space in his throat. “I would aid you if I might. I can help make your life here more bearable. But your fate is inevitable. You may never hope to return to the outer world.”

“What will be my fate?” I asked.

“That depends upon Issus. So long as she does not send for you and reveal her face to you, you may live for years—in bondage, yes, but in a mild form, as mild as I can arrange.”

“Why should she send for me?”

“The men of the lower orders she often uses for purposes of amusement. Such a fighter as you would render fine sport in the monthly rites of the temple. Men are pitted against men, and against beasts, for the edification of Issus and the replenishment of her larder.”

“She eats human flesh?” I asked.

Not in horror—my recent knowledge of the Holy Therns had cauterized surprise. Fanaticism and power had made these people regard other Barsoomians as we regard cattle. The difference was only in the costumes and the words they used to excuse appetite.

“She eats only the flesh of the best bred of the Holy Therns and the red Barsoomians,” Xodar said. “The flesh of the others goes to our boards. The animals are eaten by the slaves. She also eats other dainties.”

I did not understand, then, the weight he put behind those last words. I believed the abyss already had a bottom.

We had nearly reached the last of the many chambers and corridors that led back to the gardens when an officer overtook us at a brisk pace.

“Issus would look again upon this man,” he said. “The girl has told her he is of wondrous beauty and such prowess that alone he slew seven of the First Born, and with his bare hands took Xodar captive, binding him with his own harness.”

Xodar’s jaw tightened. He did not relish that his defeat had become court gossip in the ears of his goddess.

Without a word he turned. We followed the officer back to the closed doors of the audience chamber.

Again the ceremony. Again the crawling backward. Again the command to rise.

Silence lay over the room like a lid. I felt—rather than heard—the attention of something ancient measuring me, weighing me like meat.

Then the thin wavering voice spoke, droning words that had, for countless ages, sealed the doom of victims:

“Let the man turn and look upon Issus, knowing that those of the lower orders who gaze upon the holy vision of her radiant face survive the blinding glory but a single year.”

I turned, expecting—if not splendor—at least the sort of crafted illusion with which rulers dazzle their subjects.

What I saw was a solid phalanx of armed men between myself and a dais. Upon the dais stood a great bench of carved sorapus wood. And on that bench squatted a female black.

She was very old. Bald, her skull wrinkled like dried fruit left too long in sun. She was toothless save for two yellow fangs that jutted like obscene ornaments. Her eyes burned from the depths of horribly sunken sockets, and on either side of her thin, hawk-like nose the skin folded in deep-cut furrows as if her face had been carved by cruelty rather than time. Her body was as wrinkled as her face, emaciated limbs attached to a torso that seemed mostly distorted abdomen.

This was the “holy vision.” This was Issus, Goddess of Life Eternal.

Around her hovered female slaves. Among them was Phaidor, white and trembling, her beauty suddenly made tragic by the nearness of that ruinous thing upon the throne.

“This is the man who slew seven of the First Born and, bare-handed, bound Dator Xodar with his own harness?” Issus asked.

“Most glorious vision of divine loveliness, it is,” replied the officer at my side, voice thick with practiced lies.

“Produce Dator Xodar,” she commanded.

Xodar was brought from an adjoining room. Issus glared at him, and something like a squeal—thin, vicious—escaped her.

“And such as you are a Dator of the First Born?” she cried. “For the disgrace you have brought upon the Immortal Race you shall be degraded to a rank below the lowest. No longer be you a Dator, but forevermore a slave of slaves—to fetch and carry for the lower orders that serve in the gardens of Issus. Remove his harness. Cowards and slaves wear no trappings.”

Xodar stood rigid, head high. Not a muscle twitched as a guard stripped his gorgeous harness from him with rough hands. If he felt humiliation, he swallowed it whole.

“Begone!” Issus screamed. “Begone! But instead of the light of the gardens of Issus, let you serve as a slave of this slave who conquered you in the prison on the Isle of Shador in the Sea of Omean. Take him away out of the sight of my divine eyes.”

Slowly, proudly, Xodar turned and stalked from the chamber.

Issus rose, turning to leave by another exit, her retinue gathering like shadows behind her. Before she passed through, she looked—if that burning stare could be called looking—toward me.

“You shall be returned to Shador for the present,” she said. “Later Issus will see the manner of your fighting. Go.”

Then she was gone.

Only Phaidor lagged behind, as if the room itself held her by the hem. As I started to follow my guard toward the gardens, she broke from the line of slaves and came running after me.

“Oh, do not leave me in this terrible place,” she begged. Her words spilled out too fast, tumbling over one another like grain poured from a ruptured sack. “Forgive the things I said—I did not mean them. Only take me away with you. Let me share your imprisonment on Shador.

“You did not understand the honor I did you. Among the therns there is no marriage, no giving in marriage, as among the lower orders. We might have lived together forever in love and happiness. We have both looked upon Issus and in a year we die. Let us live that year at least together—in what joy remains for the doomed.”

How does one explain, in the few heartbeats allowed by guards and fear, that the same words can be different food to different mouths? That what she called honor sat in me like ash, not because I despised her, but because the meanings of our lives had been seasoned with entirely different salts?

“If it is difficult for me to understand you, Phaidor,” I said gently, “can you not understand that it may be equally difficult for you to understand the motives, the customs, the social laws that guide me? I do not wish to hurt you, nor to undervalue the honor you believe you have done me. But the thing you desire may not be.

“Regardless of the foolish belief of the peoples of the outer world—or of Holy Thern—or ebon First Born—I am not dead. While I live, my heart beats for but one woman: Dejah Thoris, Princess of Helium. When death overtakes me my heart shall have ceased to beat. What comes after, I know not. In that, I am no wiser than Matai Shang, Master of Life and Death upon Barsoom—or Issus, Goddess of Life Eternal.”

She stared at me a long moment. No anger now—only a hopeless sorrow, as if the very language between us had failed and left her hungry, unable to swallow what I had offered.

“I do not understand,” she said at last.

And then she turned and walked slowly toward the door through which Issus and her retinue had passed, her steps soft on the polished floor, swallowed by the temple’s manufactured hush.

A moment later she was gone from my sight.

In the outer gardens where the guard herded me, I came upon Xodar. He stood as if he had been poured from basalt—upright, silent—while a knot of noble blacks swarmed him like wasps around a fallen fruit.

They made a feast of humiliation.

Men cuffed his cheeks with open palms that cracked in the still air. Women spat—thin, bright strings that struck his ornaments and ran in ugly trails. Their voices rose and fell in harsh, metallic bursts, the way a rack of poorly tuned drums can turn a courtyard sour. The garden itself was calm enough—stone paths powdered with ruby dust, rigid shrubs trained into obedient shapes—yet the sound of it all felt like grit between the teeth.

When they saw me, their cruelty pivoted as neatly as a cook turning a skewer.

“Ah!” cried one. “So this is the creature who overcame great Xodar bare-handed. Let us see how it was done.”

“Let him bind Thurid,” a beautiful woman suggested, laughing as though cruelty were a clever spice. “Thurid is a noble Dator. Let Thurid show the dog what it means to face a real man.”

“Yes, Thurid! Thurid!” rose a dozen voices, their chant beating against the garden walls.

“Here he is now,” another exclaimed.

I followed their pointing hands and saw him advancing—huge, black, heavy with resplendent ornaments and arms, bearing himself with the careless grace of a man long accustomed to having others step aside. His jewelry chimed softly as he walked, little bright clinks like coins tossed on stone.

“What now?” he demanded. “What would you of Thurid?”

They poured their explanation over him in eager, overlapping phrases.

Thurid’s gaze cut to Xodar, narrowing to two cruel slits.

“Calot!” he hissed. “Ever did I think you carried the heart of a sorak in your putrid breast. Often have you bested me in the secret councils of Issus, but now—now in the field of war where men are truly gauged—your scabby heart hath revealed its sores to all the world. Calot, I spurn you with my foot.”

And he turned, lifting his leg for the kick—an easy, contemptuous motion, as though he meant to nudge aside refuse with the edge of a boot.

My temper, already simmering at the cowardice of a pack turning on a fallen comrade, boiled over. I had no love for Xodar; but I have never been able to watch injustice served up with laughter and not feel something in me rise like blood to the eyes.

Xodar, for his part, did not flinch. He stood ready to take their insults like a man swallowing sand because he had been told sand was holy.

Thurid’s foot swung.

So did mine.

I caught him hard on the shin bone—a flat, sharp blow that rang through him and checked the kick mid-arc. The sound of impact made a clean, ugly note, and the garden fell into sudden silence, as if every throat had been pinched closed at once.

Then Thurid roared and sprang for my throat, the same blind rush Xodar had tried upon the cruiser’s deck.

The results were the same.

I dipped beneath his outstretched arms; as he lunged past I drove my right fist into the side of his jaw. The punch landed with a satisfying solidity, like striking a ripe gourd at exactly the right angle. Thurid spun half around, his knees went soft, and the proud Dator crumpled into the ruby dust at my feet, ornaments clattering.

The blacks stared—first at Thurid sprawled ignominiously, then at me as though their eyes could not persuade their minds to accept what they had seen.

“You asked me to bind Thurid,” I cried. “Behold!”

I stooped, tore the harness from his body, and bound his arms and legs securely with the very straps meant to proclaim his rank. The leather bit into his dark skin; the knots held. It was work that needed no flourish—just firm, practiced hands and the refusal to be hurried by an audience.

“As you have done to Xodar,” I said, “now do you likewise to Thurid. Take him before Issus, bound in his own harness, that she may see with her own eyes that there be one among you now who is greater than the First Born.”

“Who are you?” whispered the woman who had first proposed the sport, her laughter gone thin.

“I am a citizen of two worlds,” I said. “Captain John Carter of Virginia, Prince of the House of Tardos Mors, Jeddak of Helium. Take this man to your goddess as I have said—and tell her, too, that as I have done to Xodar and Thurid, so also can I do to the mightiest of her Dators. With naked hands, with long-sword or with short-sword, I challenge the flower of her fighting-men to combat.”

The officer guarding me stepped forward then, his face changed. He still held power over me, but not contempt—not after seeing Thurid fall.

“Come,” he said. “My orders are imperative; there is to be no delay. Xodar, come you also.”

There was little disrespect in his tone now. During the rest of the way he kept behind me with a drawn short-sword, not as one strolling beside a slave, but as a man who has learned caution in the presence of something he does not understand.

We returned to the Sea of Omean as we had come—through the awful shaft in the car, dropping down into that buried world where daylight is only a rumor and distance is measured by echoes. We entered the submarine, took the long dive into the tunnel far beneath the upper crust, traveled the passageway, rose again into the pool that had first introduced us to this strange artery between Omean and the Temple of Issus.

From the submarine island we were carried on a small cruiser to the distant Isle of Shador.

The journey had the dull inevitability of a chain being pulled link by link—no sudden turns, no mercy, only motion.

Shador proved to be a small stone prison with a guard of half a dozen blacks. No rites, no speeches. A key as large as a man’s forearm grated in a lock. A door swung inward. We stepped through. The door closed. The lock rasped shut.

At that sound, hopelessness came over me again—swift and cold—as it had in the Chamber of Mystery beneath the Golden Cliffs. Then Tars Tarkas had been with me. Now, of all Barsoom, I had no friendly companionship at my side.

I found myself thinking of the great Thark, and of Thuvia, the girl. Even if by some miracle they had escaped and found refuge among a friendly nation, how could they ever guess my whereabouts? No one on Barsoom dreamed of such a place as this buried sea. And even if they knew, what force could batter through the navy of the First Born and the labyrinth that guarded this vault?

No. If I allowed myself to take the measure of it, my case was hopeless.

So I refused to measure it.

I rose, shook off the brooding despair like a man shaking off a cloak that has grown too heavy, and set about learning my cage.

The building was roofless, its walls rising some thirty feet, their stone polished so smooth it caught the constant phosphorescent light and returned it in a dull sheen. Halfway up, two small windows were barred thickly enough to mock any notion of slipping through. Partitions about twenty feet high divided the interior into several rooms. In ours stood only a low stone bench.

Xodar sat on it with bowed head. Since Issus had degraded him, he had not spoken.

Two doors in our room stood open into adjoining chambers. I moved through them one by one, my boots whispering on stone. Several rooms were empty—bare benches, bare floors, the air holding the faint mineral smell of stone that has never known sun or rain.

In the last chamber I found a young red Martian boy asleep on the bench.

Something in his face snagged my attention—a familiarity I could not name. His features were regular, delicately cut, his limbs graceful even in the slackness of sleep. He was lighter in color than most red men I had known, but in all else a typical specimen of that handsome race.

I did not wake him. Sleep in prison is a ration more precious than food, and I have seen men become animals when robbed of it.

I returned to our cell. Xodar had not moved.

“Man,” I said, “it will profit you nothing to mope thus. It were no disgrace to be bested by John Carter. You have seen that in the ease with which I accounted for Thurid. You knew it before, on the cruiser’s deck, when I slew three of your comrades.”

“I would that you had dispatched me at the same time,” he said dully.

“Come, come!” I snapped, because pity and impatience are often neighbors. “There is hope yet. Neither of us is dead. We are great fighters. Why not win to freedom?”

He looked up as though I had suggested he grow wings.

“You know not of what you speak,” he replied. “Issus is omnipotent. Issus is omniscient. She hears now the words you speak. She knows the thoughts you think. It is sacrilege even to dream of breaking her commands.”

“Rot, Xodar,” I said.

He sprang to his feet, horror in every line of him.

“The curse of Issus will fall upon you!” he cried. “In another instant you will be smitten down, writhing to your death in horrible agony.”

“Do you believe that, Xodar?” I asked.

“Of course; who would dare doubt?”

“I doubt,” I said. “Yes, and further, I deny. You tell me she knows my thoughts. The red men have had that power for ages. And another: they can shut their minds so none may read them. I learned the first secret years ago; the other I never had to learn, since upon all Barsoom is none who can read what passes in the secret chambers of my brain.

“Your goddess cannot read my thoughts—nor yours when you are out of her sight, unless you will it. Had she been able to read mine, I fear her pride would have suffered a rather severe shock when I turned at her command to gaze upon the holy vision of her radiant face.”

“What do you mean?” he whispered, so low I had to lean in to catch it. Even the air seemed to listen.

“I mean,” I said, “that I thought her the most repulsive and vilely hideous creature my eyes ever had rested upon.”

For a heartbeat he only stared, as though the words had struck him across the mouth.

Then he cried, “Blasphemer!” and sprang upon me.

I had no wish to strike him, and none to hurt him. He was unarmed; to me he was dangerous only in the way a panicked man is dangerous to himself.

As he came I caught his left wrist with my left hand, swung my right arm over his left shoulder, and hooked him beneath the chin with my elbow. The motion rolled him back across my thigh. He hung there helpless, face twisted with rage, eyes glaring up at me.

“Xodar,” I said, “let us be friends. For a year, possibly, we may be forced to live together in the narrow confines of this tiny room. I am sorry to have offended you, but I could not dream that one who has suffered from the cruel injustice of Issus still could believe her divine.

“I will say a few more words, not to wound you further, but that you may think on this: while we live, we are still more the arbiters of our own fate than is any god.

“Issus has not struck me dead. Nor is she rescuing her faithful Xodar from the clutches of the unbeliever who defamed her beauty. No, Xodar—your Issus is a mortal old woman. Once out of her clutches she cannot harm you.

“With your knowledge of this strange land and my knowledge of the outer world, two such fighting-men as you and I should be able to win our way to freedom. Even though we died in the attempt, would not our memories be fairer than if we remained in servile fear to be butchered by a cruel and unjust tyrant—call her goddess or mortal, as you will?”

I set him on his feet and released him.

He did not attack again. He did not answer. He walked back to the bench, sank down upon it, and sat in silence—thinking so hard it seemed to draw the sound out of the room, leaving only the faint, constant hush of this underground world pressing against the walls like the sea presses against a hull.

Hours passed. The phosphorescent glow never changed.

At last I heard a soft sound at the doorway to one of the other chambers—a careful shift of weight, a breath caught and held. I looked up and saw the red Martian youth standing there, gazing at us with intense curiosity.

“Kaor,” I said.

“Kaor,” he replied, stepping nearer.

“What do you here?” I asked.

“I await my death, I presume,” I said with a wry twist of the mouth.

He smiled back—a brave, winning smile that made the stone cell seem less airless.

“I also,” he said. “Mine will come soon. I looked upon the radiant beauty of Issus nearly a year since. It has always been a source of keen wonder to me that I did not drop dead at the first sight of that hideous countenance. And her belly! By my first ancestor, but never was there so grotesque a figure in all the universe. That they should call such a one Goddess of Life Eternal, Goddess of Death, Mother of the Nearer Moon, and fifty other equally impossible titles, is quite beyond me.”

“How came you here?” I asked.

“It is very simple.” He spoke lightly, but there was steel beneath it. “I was flying a one-man air scout far to the south when the brilliant idea occurred to me that I should like to search for the Lost Sea of Korus, which tradition places near the south pole. I must have inherited from my father a wild lust for adventure, as well as a hollow where my bump of reverence should be.

“I had reached the area of eternal ice when my port propeller jammed, and I dropped to the ground to make repairs. Before I knew it the air was black with fliers, and a hundred of these First Born devils were leaping to the ground all about me.

“With drawn swords they made for me, but before I went down beneath them they had tasted of the steel of my father’s sword, and I had given such an account of myself as I know would have pleased my sire had he lived to witness it.”

“Your father is dead?” I asked.

“He died before the shell broke to let me step out into a world that has been very good to me. But for the sorrow that I had never the honor to know my father, I have been very happy. My only sorrow now is that my mother must mourn me as she has for ten long years mourned my father.”

“Who was your father?” I asked.

He was drawing breath to answer when the outer door of the prison clanged open. A burly guard entered, his boots loud on stone, the blunt noise of authority. He ordered the youth back to his own quarters for the night and locked the inner door after him as the boy passed into the further chamber.

Then the guard returned to our cell.

“It is Issus’ wish that you two be confined in the same room,” he said. “This cowardly slave of a slave is to serve you well,” he added, indicating Xodar with a contemptuous wave. “If he does not, you are to beat him into submission. It is Issus’ wish that you heap upon him every indignity and degradation of which you can conceive.”

With that, he left. The outer lock grated again, the sound lingering like an aftertaste.

Xodar sat with his face buried in his hands.

I crossed to him and put my hand on his shoulder.

“Xodar,” I said, “you have heard the commands of Issus, but you need not fear that I shall attempt to put them into execution. You are a brave man. It is your own affair if you wish to be persecuted and humiliated; but were I you I should assert my manhood and defy my enemies.”

He lifted his head slowly. When he spoke, his voice had changed: less broken, more deliberate, as if he had been chewing bitter roots until he could name their flavor.

“I have been thinking very hard, John Carter,” he said, “of all the new ideas you gave me a few hours since. Little by little I have been piecing together the things you said which sounded blasphemous to me then with the things I have seen in my past life and dared not even think about for fear of bringing down upon me the wrath of Issus.

“I believe now that she is a fraud; no more divine than you or I. More I am willing to concede—that the First Born are no holier than the Holy Therns, nor the Holy Therns more holy than the red men.

“The whole fabric of our religion is based on superstitious belief in lies that have been foisted upon us for ages by those directly above us, to whose personal profit and aggrandizement it was to have us continue to believe as they wished us to believe.

“I am ready to cast off the ties that have bound me. I am ready to defy Issus herself; but what will it avail us? Be the First Born gods or mortals, they are a powerful race, and we are as fast in their clutches as though we were already dead. There is no escape.”

“I have escaped from bad plights in the past, my friend,” I said. “Nor while life is in me shall I despair of escaping from the Isle of Shador and the Sea of Omean.”

“But we cannot escape even from the four walls of our prison,” he urged, striking the rock with his fist. The sound was a dull thud that went nowhere. “Test this flint-like surface. And look upon this polished face—none could cling to it to reach the top.”

I smiled, because the wall was the sort of problem a man can put his hands on, and therefore the sort of problem a man can solve.

“That is the least of our troubles, Xodar,” I said. “I will guarantee to scale the wall and take you with me, if you will help with your knowledge of the customs here to appoint the best time for the attempt, and guide me to the shaft that lets from the dome of this abysmal sea to the light of God’s pure air above.”

“Night time is the best and offers the only slender chance,” he said, “for then men sleep, and only a dozing watch nods in the tops of the battleships. No watch is kept upon the cruisers and smaller craft. The watchers upon the larger vessels see to all about them. It is night now.”

“But,” I said, glancing at the steady, eerie glow on stone, “it is not dark. How can it be night, then?”

He gave a small, humorless smile.

“You forget,” he said, “that we are far below ground. The light of the sun never penetrates here. There are no moons and no stars reflected in the bosom of Omean. The phosphorescent light you see pervading this great subterranean vault emanates from the rocks that form its dome; it is always thus upon Omean—just as the billows are always as you see them, rolling, ever rolling over a windless sea.

“At the appointed hour of night upon the world above, the men whose duties hold them here sleep—but the light is ever the same.”

“It will make escape more difficult,” I said. Then I shrugged; an easy task has never fed my appetite for life. “Let us sleep on it to-night.”

“A plan may come with our awakening,” Xodar said.

So we threw ourselves upon the hard stone floor and slept the sleep of tired men—while beyond our walls the windless sea rolled on, forever sounding like a distant, restless breath.

Morning came to Shador with a thin, cold light that smelled of wet stone and brine, and with it the soft, steady hush of Korus beyond the walls—a living sound, like a giant kettle kept just under simmer. Even in a cell, you could tell when the sea was calm; its breath laid itself along the corridors and slipped beneath doors the way steam finds a crack.

Xodar and I put our minds to escape the way old women put their hands to kneading: with patience, with repetition, with the certainty that rough material can be made workable. I had him scratch a map of the south polar regions across the cell’s stone floor. We had no stylus—only the buckle from my harness and the keen edge of the strange gem I had taken from Sator Throg. The gem bit the stone cleanly, leaving pale grooves that looked like chalk lines until you brushed them and felt the grit under your fingertips.

He drew the curve of the Golden Cliffs; he marked the general lay of the ice and the southward wastes; he indicated the hidden opening that led into Omean. While he worked, I listened—not only to his voice but to the place itself: the shuffle of distant guards, the metal tick of harness fittings, the far-off clank that came from Shador’s docks. Sound carried here as if the air were a drumskin. Even the smallest noise announced a life, a duty, a threat.

From his crude cartography I computed direction and distance—Helium out there somewhere beyond the frozen desolation, as real and unreachable as a remembered taste. Then I had him draw Omean itself, the bowl of the buried sea, and the prison’s position within it; he marked plainly the opening in the dome that led to the outer world, and where Shador sat by the edge like a scabbed wound on the shore.

I studied the lines until they no longer lay in the floor but in my mind. If the body must move in darkness, the memory must be a lamp.

From Xodar I learned the guard customs. During the sleep hours, only one man patrolled at a time, pacing a slow circuit around the prison at a distance of about a hundred feet. It took him close to ten minutes to complete a round—so that for nearly five minutes any given side lay unwatched while his snail’s pace carried him to the opposite face.

“This information you ask,” Xodar said at last, voice low with the sour caution of a man who has watched too many hopes spoiled, “will be valuable after we are out. But nothing you have asked touches the first matter: how do we pass these walls?”

“We will get out,” I told him, and could not keep the laugh from my throat. The sea’s hush steadied me, and the rhythm of the guard’s distant step set a pace to my thoughts. “Leave that to me.”

“When?” he asked.

“The first night a small craft lies moored near Shador,” I said.

“But how will you know?” He jerked his chin toward the high windows, far above reach. “The openings are beyond us.”

“Not beyond,” I answered, and sprang.

Earth-bred muscles on Barsoom are a sort of sorcery; I caught the iron bars, climbed as lightly as if the grating were a ladder, and lifted myself to glance out.

Below, in the pale morning, several small craft and two large battleships lay within a hundred yards of the prison.

To-night, I thought—had the word on my tongue—when the door opened without warning and a guard stepped in.

If he saw me at the window, he would not merely scold. He would put me in irons, and once iron has a man, it makes him a theory instead of a threat.

The black entered and stood facing the center of the cell. His back was toward me. Between my boots and the floor was the whole weight of his suspicion. Five feet above me ran the top of the partition wall dividing our cell from the next.

There was no graceful way down. If I dropped, I would strike him. If he turned, I was done.

“Where is the white man?” he bellowed at Xodar. “Issus commands his presence.” He began to turn his head, just enough for one eye to search.

I did not descend. I sprang.

My foot found the sill for the briefest heartbeat; I let go and launched for the partition top. Metal rasped on stone as I slid over—an ugly, betraying scrape.

“What was that?” the guard snarled, the sound of his voice deep as a drum.

I dropped into the adjacent cell and landed soft, knees bent, breath held. From the other side came his renewed roar.

“Where is the white slave?”

“I know not,” Xodar answered, calm as a man stirring embers. “He was here even as you entered. I am not his keeper—go find him.”

The black muttered something I could not catch; then came the click and grind of keys, a door opening in the corridor beyond, then another. He was searching cells. I listened to the pattern of his movements until his steps passed into the next compartment and the door shut behind him.

Then I was over the partition again and down into my own cell beside Xodar, who stared as though he had seen a conjurer pull smoke from a fist.

“Do you see now how we will escape?” I whispered.

“I see how you may,” he replied, bitterness and wonder tangled together. “But I am no wiser how I am to pass these walls. Certain it is I cannot bounce over them as you do.”

We heard the guard returning, keys jangling. He came in at last, and when his eyes fell on me they bulged with a mixture of rage and relief, as though his mind could not decide whether he had been tricked or simply shamed.

“By the shell of my first ancestor!” he roared. “Where have you been?”

“In prison,” I said blandly. “Since you put me here yesterday. I was in this room when you entered. You had better look to your eyesight.”

He glared a moment longer—anger searching for purchase—and then the order overcame the uncertainty.

“Come. Issus commands your presence.”

He took me out, leaving Xodar behind. Outside the prison other guards waited, and with them the red Martian youth from another cell. The sight of him tightened something in me like a knot drawn suddenly. His face had haunted my thoughts. There was a familiarity in the set of his mouth, in the way his shoulders carried their strength, in the way his gaze moved—alert, appraising, full of a contained heat that was more than youth.

I knew too well that I had never seen him before. And yet my memory kept insisting I had—like a word you cannot translate but recognize in your bones because it belonged once to your mother’s tongue.

We made again the journey I had taken the day before toward the Temple of Issus. The guards kept the youth and me apart, so we could not finish the conversation that had been cut short the previous night. The world around us wore its false beauty like spiced sweetness laid over spoiled meat: exquisite gardens, the air rich with perfume, water murmuring in hidden channels. But beneath it all I heard the harsher soundtrack of Omean—chains, shouted orders, the slap of sandals on stone, the sharp clack of harness buckles. This place tried to sell holiness the way cities sell entertainment, with spectacle and noise; yet the sea beyond still kept its own truth, steady and unbought.

When we reached the gardens of Issus we were led away from the temple rather than toward it. The path wound through parks that felt staged—trees too perfectly spaced, flowers too lavishly arranged, the air heavy with scent as if to drown out the stench of fear. We came at last to a mighty wall a hundred feet high, with massive gates that opened upon a small plain rimmed by the same gorgeous forests I had seen below the Golden Cliffs.

Crowds of blacks strolled toward the same destination. Among them moved plant men and great white apes, as casually as a man might walk dogs. The blacks shoved the beasts aside or struck them with the flat of a sword; the apes slunk away, cowed by habit. It was not courage that ruled here, but ownership.

Half a mile beyond the garden wall stood a great amphitheatre at the edge of the plain. Through a massive arched gateway the blacks poured in to take their seats while our guards led us to a smaller entrance. We passed into an enclosure beneath the stands where other prisoners were herded, some in irons, most simply held by the knowledge that every exit was guarded.

Here, in the caged gloom, the guards’ attention softened. I slipped to the youth’s side.

“What is the object of this assembly?” I asked. “Are we to fight for their amusement, or something worse?”

“It is a part of the monthly rites of Issus,” he said. His voice carried the flatness of repeated horror, as though he had learned to speak of it the way a butcher speaks of slaughter—without letting the taste of blood rise into the mouth. “Black men wash sins from their souls in the blood of men from the outer world. If the black is killed, it proves his disloyalty to Issus—the unpardonable sin. If he lives, he is held acquitted of the charge that forced the rites upon him.

“The forms vary. Sometimes we fight in groups against equal numbers, or twice our number. Sometimes singly against beasts, or some famed black warrior.”

“And if we win?” I asked. “Freedom?”

He laughed—short, sharp.

“Freedom. The only freedom is death. None who enters the domains of the First Born ever leave. If we fight well, we are permitted to fight often. If we fight poorly—” He lifted a shoulder and let it fall. “Sooner or later we die in the arena.”

“And you?” I asked. “You have fought often.”

“Very often,” he replied, and for the first time something like pleasure warmed his eyes. “It is my only pleasure. Some hundred black devils have I accounted for during nearly a year of the rites. My mother would be proud could she know how well I have maintained the traditions of my father’s prowess.”

“Your father must have been a mighty warrior,” I said. “I have known most of Barsoom’s great fighters. Doubtless I knew him. Who was he?”

“My father was—”

“Come, calots!” a guard barked. “To the slaughter with you.”

We were hustled down the steep incline to the chambers that let out upon the arena. The amphitheatre, like others on Barsoom, had been built into an excavation so that only the uppermost seats formed a low wall above ground. The arena lay far below, a pit of sand and blood.

Just beneath the lowest tier of seats ran a series of barred cages level with the arena floor. Into these we were herded. The youth—my strange familiar—was placed elsewhere, not in my cage.

Directly opposite stood the throne of Issus. The creature squatted upon it, surrounded by a hundred slave maidens glittering with jewels. Brilliant cloths of many hues covered the dais like a feast of color meant to distract from the hunger that sat at its center.

Below the throne, in rigid ranks, stood heavily armed soldiers. In front of them lounged the dignitaries—gleaming blacks weighted with precious stones, their foreheads marked with circles of gold that announced rank the way a chef’s knife announces his station: by what it can cut.

On both sides of the throne the seats were packed—women and men in elaborate harness, each attended by one to three slaves drawn from therns and from the outer world. Among the First Born there was no peasantry; even the lowest soldier was “noble” and kept slaves as casually as one keeps cups.

They did no work. The men fought as a sacred privilege and duty; the women did nothing at all. Slaves washed them, dressed them, fed them. Some had slaves who spoke for them. I saw one woman sit with eyes closed while a slave narrated the arena’s events into her ear, turning slaughter into a purchased story.

The first event was called the Tribute to Issus. It ended the year-long service of ten young women—beauties taken from courts of jeddaks and from the temples of the Holy Therns. For a year they had attended Issus. To-day they were to pay for the “honor” with their lives. Tomorrow they would grace the tables of court functionaries.

A huge black entered the arena with them. He inspected them as a buyer inspects carcasses: pressing flesh, prodding ribs, testing limbs as though judging tenderness. At last he chose one, led her before the throne, spoke words I could not hear. Issus nodded. The black raised his hands in salute, took the girl by the wrist, and dragged her through a small doorway below the throne.

“Issus will dine well to-night,” said a prisoner beside me, his tone the exhausted cynicism of a man who has been forced to learn the vocabulary of cannibals.

“What do you mean?” I asked, though the smell of the place—perfume over sweat, incense over fear—made the answer sour on my tongue already.

“That was her dinner Thabis is taking to the kitchens. Did you not note how carefully he selected the plumpest and tenderest of the lot?”

The words struck like a fist. In my mind I saw hands at a chopping block, but not hands that cook with reverence; hands that butcher to display power. The First Born had turned hunger into doctrine and called it divinity.

I growled my curses on the monster squatting opposite.

“Fume not,” my companion said. “You will see far worse if you live even a month among them.”

Then a gate near by was thrown open and three monstrous white apes sprang into the arena. The girls shrank together at the center like a clutch of doves under hawk shadow. One fell to her knees and stretched imploring hands toward Issus; the goddess leaned forward in anticipation, as though the screams were music and the rush of the beasts a drumroll.

The apes spied the huddled knot and charged with shrieks that tore through the amphitheatre. The sound hit the ear not as mere noise but as weapon—industrial in its brutality, like machines designed to grind nerve and hope into silence. The crowd’s roar rose in waves, a manufactured ecstasy, the commercialization of agony dressed as rite.

A blood-red haze swam before my eyes.

The guard lounged at my cage gate, unbarred. Why bar it, indeed? Where would any victim flee, when the world itself had been built as their pen?

One blow dropped him unconscious. I tore his long-sword from his grasp and sprang into the arena.

For an instant the amphitheatre went silent—the sudden hush that comes when a kitchen sees a knife lifted against the master. Then a wild shout rose from the cages of the doomed.

My blade cut the air with a clean, singing arc. One ape fell headless at the feet of the fainting girls. The other two whirled on me.

From the corner of my eye I saw guards rushing across the sand, and then a figure broke from a cage behind them. It was the red youth. He stood a moment with sword raised high.

“Come, men of the outer world!” he shouted. His voice carried clear over the arena, cutting through the din the way a bell cuts through market clamor. “Let us make our deaths worth while! At the back of this unknown warrior, turn this day’s Tribute into revenge that will echo through the ages—so black skins blanch at every telling of the rites of Issus! Come! The racks without your cages are filled with blades!”

He did not wait for an answer. He bounded toward me.

From every cage that held red men a thunderous response rose. Guards went down beneath howling mobs. Cages vomited forth their inmates, hot with the lust to kill. Swords were ripped from racks—blades meant to stage controlled contests now taken for uncontrolled revolt.

While the charging guards were still distant, my sword put the great apes down. They towered fifteen feet, but flesh is flesh; and the will behind my arm that day was not merely anger but refusal—refusal to let cruelty become routine, refusal to let the cries of girls be treated as background noise for a crowd’s pleasure.

I stayed with the maidens behind me. I had sprung for them, and if I died it would be in that service.

The youth raced after the guards with a speed I had never seen in any Martian. His leaps and bounds were nearly akin to my own, and the sight of him stirred in me that same untranslatable certainty: that I knew him, that some part of my life was speaking through him in a dialect I could not name aloud.

He fell upon the guards from behind with such ferocity that they turned as though a dozen men attacked them. I rushed them from the front.

Then there was only fighting—metal purring and snarling, sand turning dark, breath burning the throat. Now and again I glimpsed the youth beside me: a lightly springing figure of sinewy steel, a grim smile on his handsome face, taunting his foes as he cut them down. There were movements in him that echoed my own—an old habit of stance, a familiar timing of step—and it filled me with a yearning so sharp it was almost pain, and with a pride that had no explanation.

We fought as men do when the world has stripped them of everything but the right to choose how to die.

For half an hour it was as though all hell had broken loose. Within the arena’s walls we became an inextricable mass—howling, cursing, blood-streaked demons. Slowly, by shouted command and sheer will, I drew the prisoners into a rough formation, a circle in whose center we kept the doomed maids. Many fell on both sides, but more guards died than prisoners. Messengers ran through the audience; noble blacks unsheathed their swords and leaped down. They meant to crush us with numbers.

I saw Issus leaning far forward, her face twisted with hate and rage—and beneath it, fear. That fear was the crack in her godhood, the proof she was not a deity but an appetite wearing jewels.

It inspired what followed.

I ordered fifty prisoners to fall back and form a tighter ring around the girls.

“Remain and protect them until I return,” I commanded.

Then I turned to those holding the outer line.

“Down with Issus!” I cried. “Follow me to the throne. We will reap vengeance where vengeance is deserved.”

The youth at my side took up the cry first, his voice ringing like struck metal: “Down with Issus!”

From behind and all around rose the hoarse answer: “To the throne! To the throne!”

We moved as one, a fighting mass, over dead and dying toward the dais. The strongest warriors of the First Born poured down to stop us. We cut through them as though they were paper men. The sound in the amphitheatre became a storm—steel on steel, screams, the crowd’s roar breaking into panicked shrieks as the spectacle turned on its spectators.

“To the seats, some of you!” I shouted as we neared the barrier wall. “Ten of us can take the throne!” For I saw most of Issus’ guards had already joined the arena fight.

Prisoners broke left and right, vaulting the low wall with dripping swords and rushing into the stands. In another moment the amphitheatre was filled with the shrieks of the dying and the wounded, with triumphant shouts, with the hideous chorus of a world overturned.

Side by side the youth and I, with a dozen others, fought our way to the foot of the throne. Remaining guards, reinforced by dignitaries and nobles, closed between us and Issus. She leaned forward on her carved sorapus bench, screaming high-pitched commands, hurling curses as though words alone could restore the hierarchy.

The slave girls around her trembled, wide-eyed, not knowing whether to pray for our victory or our defeat. Some—proud daughters of great warriors, I had no doubt—snatched swords from fallen hands and struck at Issus’ guards. They fell quickly, martyrs to a hope that had only seconds to live, but in those seconds they had reclaimed their own language of action.

The men with us fought well, but soon they were gone. One by one they fell until only the youth and I remained pressing toward the bench. Never, not even beside Tars Tarkas against Warhoon, had I seen two men fight to such good purpose and unconquerable ferocity as we did there before the throne of Issus.

Man by man those who stood between us and the sorapus wood went down. Others swarmed in to fill the gap, and still we advanced—inch by inch, foot by foot.

Then a new cry rose from the stands, at first in one section, then rolling outward in waves like sound across water:

“Rise slaves! Rise slaves!”

For an instant the fighting slackened as men turned to understand this new note in the storm. It took only a heartbeat to see: everywhere the female slaves were falling upon their masters with whatever came to hand. Daggers snatched from harnesses, swords pulled from corpses, heavy ornaments swung as clubs. Those who found nothing else used fingers and teeth. It was both shuddering and glorious—a long, pent vengeance finally given voice, a chorus rising against the dominant lexicon that had named them “property” until even they nearly believed it.

“Rise slaves! Rise slaves!” echoed again, but already the sound began to thin under the answering screams. Even revolt has its acoustics: first the clean bell-tone of hope, then the ragged tearing as it is cut down.

We returned to our own struggle. Only a thin rank now stood between us and Issus. Her face had gone blue with terror; foam flecked her lips. She looked less like a goddess than like a frightened animal trapped on a table.

A black Dator swung a long-sword in a nasty cut meant to split my skull. I would have died—my own blade wedged in a breastbone at the worst moment—had not a hand from behind my adversary shot out and clutched his elbow as he struck. The blow faltered. The youth sprang to my side and ran his sword through the man before he could recover.

My sword came free as the Dator fell. I snatched the dying man’s weapon and looked, over his prostrate body, into the eyes of the one whose quick hand had saved me.

Phaidor—daughter of Matai Shang.

“Fly, my Prince!” she cried, breathless with terror and urgency. “It is useless to fight longer. All within the arena are dead. All who charged the throne are dead but you and this youth. Only among the seats are there any of your fighting-men, and they and the slave women are fast being cut down. Listen—you can scarce hear the battle-cry of the women now for nearly all are dead. For each one of you there are ten thousand blacks within the domains of the First Born. Break for the open and the sea of Korus. With your mighty sword arm you may yet win to the Golden Cliffs and the templed gardens of the Holy Therns. There tell your story to Matai Shang, my father. He will keep you, and together you may find a way to rescue me. Fly while there is yet a bare chance for flight.”

But that was not my mission. Nor, in the bitter knowledge of what these hidden heavens did to flesh and memory, could I see much to be preferred in the cruel hospitality of the Holy Therns over that of the First Born.

For a heartbeat I stood where the sand still held the imprint of my heel, staring at the ring of faces closing in—black-skinned First Born, their metal ornaments catching the arena’s glare like knives laid out for carving. The next instant the press of them drove me back. My foot searched for the reassuring hardness of floor—and found air.

The world dropped away beneath me.

I had backed into the very pit that had swallowed Issus when she teetered at the edge of defeat. I pitched backward, the boy locked against my chest as if I could keep him from falling by sheer will, and we vanished into a mouth of darkness that smelled not of clean stone but of long-closed places—dry dust, ancient minerals, and the faint, sour note of something sealed too long.

We struck a polished chute and shot downward as though flung through the throat of a machine. Above, the opening snapped shut with the same impossible neatness with which it had opened, as if the temple itself were a cook’s hand closing a lid to smother flame. We slid, unharmed, into a dim apartment far below the arena.

I found my feet, and the first thing I saw—before I could even take the boy’s weight fully to my arms—was Issus.

She stood behind a grated door at one side of the chamber, her face a mask of malignant triumph, eyes gleaming through the bars as if she meant to roast my soul and savor the smoke.

“Rash mortal!” she shrilled, and her voice did not simply strike the ear—it clawed at it. The chamber’s stone made her words ring thin and metallic, the way cheap vessels sing when struck. “You shall pay the awful penalty for your blasphemy in this secret cell. Here you shall lie alone and in darkness with the carcass of your accomplice festering in its rottenness by your side, until crazed by loneliness and hunger you feed upon the crawling maggots that were once a man.”

There are curses meant to blister the skin; hers was meant to poison appetite itself, to make the very idea of eating turn to nausea, and yet to promise hunger so sharp you would take anything between your teeth.

That was all. She was gone before I could answer. And the dim light that had filled the cell thinned, guttered, and died—like a flame pinched out between damp fingers—until we stood in a blackness so complete it felt poured, heavy as oil.

“Pleasant old lady,” said a voice at my side, dry with humor.

“Who speaks?” I demanded, turning toward the sound that was now the only geography I had.

“’Tis I—your companion—who has had the honour this day of fighting shoulder to shoulder with the greatest warrior that ever wore metal upon Barsoom.”

Relief came like the first swallow after thirst. “I thank God that you are not dead,” I said. “I feared for that nasty cut upon your head.”

“It but stunned me,” he replied. “A mere scratch.”

“Maybe it were as well had it been final,” I muttered, because the pit’s air already seemed intent on making prophets of despair. “We appear to have a splendid chance of dying of starvation and thirst.”

“Where are we?”

“Beneath the arena. We tumbled down the shaft that swallowed Issus as she was almost at our mercy.”

He gave a low laugh—soft, controlled—yet full of the relief of a man who has found, in the pantry of disaster, an unexpected door.

Then, moving like a shadow in a place without light, he reached for me. Fingers found my shoulder, and he drew my ear close to his mouth as if the darkness itself had ears and a tongue.

“Nothing could be better,” he whispered. “There are secrets within the secrets of Issus of which Issus herself does not dream.”

“What do you mean?”

“I laboured with the other slaves a year since in the remodelling of these subterranean galleries. We found below these an ancient system of corridors and chambers sealed up for ages. The blacks in charge explored them, taking several of us along for the work. I know the system perfectly.

“There are miles of corridors honeycombing the ground beneath the gardens and the temple itself. One passage leads down and connects with the lower regions that open on the water shaft that gives passage to Omean.

“If we can reach the submarine undetected, we may yet make the sea—there are many islands where the blacks never go. There we may live for a time, and who knows what may transpire to aid us to escape?”

He spoke as though laying out ingredients for a dish that might save us: corridor, passage, shaft, boat, sea, islands—each word placed carefully, not for poetry but for survival. Even here, where Issus meant us to rot, he was measuring distances by memory the way an elder measures grain by the feel of it in the hand.

I kept my voice as low as his. “Lead back to Shador, my friend. Xodar—the black—is there. We were to attempt our escape together. I cannot desert him.”

“No,” said the boy at once. In the dark I could hear his head turn, could almost hear the set of his jaw in the sound of his breath. “One cannot desert a friend. It were better to be recaptured ourselves than that.”

He began to grope about the floor, hands sweeping the stone with a care that reminded me of searching a hearth for a dropped seed—quiet, patient, knowing the smallest find might matter. At last he gave a low, sibilant summons—“S-s-t”—and I crept toward the sound.

He knelt at the brink of an opening in the floor. “There is a drop here of about ten feet,” he whispered. “Hang by your hands and you will alight safely on a level floor of soft sand.”

Very quietly, I lowered myself from the inky cell above into the inky pit below. The darkness was so absolute it stole even the comfort of one’s own hands. I have been in night, in fog, in the smoke of battle; none of it compared to this—this deliberate absence, as if light had been forbidden by law.

For a moment I hung, arms straining, feet searching for a world that was not there. When the feet find only emptiness and the distance below is hidden, the body invents horrors. Though he had said ten feet, the mind insisted on infinity. The muscles begged not to let go.

I let go.

I dropped—four feet—and struck a soft cushion of sand that yielded like flour sifted onto a board. The boy followed, his landing quick and controlled.

“Raise me to your shoulders,” he whispered, “and I will replace the trap.”

I bent, he climbed, and his fingers worked above my head. I could not see, but I could hear the faint scrape of stone upon stone and then the subtle change in the air as the opening sealed. Even without sight, I felt the temple’s craft: a cellar that could swallow you and leave no mouth behind.

He took my hand. We moved slowly, his pace cautious, with frequent halts—his palm tightening on mine when a corridor forked, loosening when he recognized a turn by the feel of wall and floor. He navigated as some food-historian might move through a vanished kitchen by memory—where the salt was kept, where the oven door opened, where the smoke used to cling. Here, instead, it was angles and slopes and the faintest drafts.

Presently we began the descent of a very steep incline.

“It will not be long,” he said, “before we shall have light. At the lower levels we meet the same stratum of phosphorescent rock that illuminates Omean.”

Never shall I forget that passage through the pits of Issus. Nothing leapt at us; no guards sprang from niches; no sudden blade sang. And yet every step was an incident, because the corridors themselves were old beyond reckoning—old enough that their silence had a texture.

In that silence, sound became fate. Our breaths were loud. The soft drag of sand beneath our sandals was louder. Somewhere far above, an echo of the arena’s roar—distant now, muffled as though heard through water—came and went, like a storm remembered after it has passed. There were no birds here, no wind in leaves, no ordinary world-sound to soothe the mind. It was the kind of acoustic emptiness that can drive men to invent voices, and I understood why Issus promised madness as a seasoning for our hunger. A place can starve you not only of food, but of the gentle noises by which a human heart keeps time.

In the black, my imagination cooked up its own feast of the past. I saw ancient peoples moving through these tunnels with lamps and burdens, plotting, praying, fearing—setting their last defenses against a dying planet’s slow famine. I thought of Barsoom’s three principal races—the blacks, the whites, and that vanished yellow people spoken of only in scraps of story, their names lost the way a tongue dies when no child learns it. As waters dried and seas receded, all sustenance dwindled: not merely food, but the very vocabulary of belonging. Cities were abandoned. Communities broke into smaller wandering groups. The fierce green men preyed upon them. Blended blood made the splendid red men of today.

I had believed the old races gone utterly, like forgotten recipes nobody can now translate because the dialect of spices is dead. Yet within these past days I had found whites and blacks in multitudes. Could some remnant of the yellow race still exist in some far corner of the world, keeping their words alive in whispers, seasoning their meals with names no outsider could pronounce?

A low exclamation from the boy broke my reverie.

“At last,” he breathed, and there was triumph in it, as if he had smelled bread baking after days of dust. “The lighted way.”

Looking up, I saw it far ahead: a dim radiance. As we advanced, the light increased until we emerged into passageways lit by softly glowing rock. It cast no harsh glare—only a steady, cool gleam, like moonlight caught in stone. The corridors here seemed less oppressive, not because they were newer but because the eye could finally sip something other than darkness.

From then on we moved quickly. The boy’s confidence returned in the surety of his stride, and soon we came to the end of a corridor that opened directly upon the ledge surrounding the pool of the submarine.

The craft lay at her moorings. The hatch was uncovered.

The boy raised a finger to his lips, then tapped his sword in a gesture that needed no translation. He crept forward without a sound. I followed, keeping my breathing shallow. The air here was damp and tasted faintly of metal and old water, like a knife left too long in a sink.

Silently we dropped to the deserted deck. On hands and knees we crawled toward the hatchway. I stole a glance below.

No guard. No crew. Only shadowed bunks, silent instruments, the stale smell of enclosed machinery.

With the quickness and soundlessness of cats, we dropped together into the main cabin. Still no sign of life. We covered and secured the hatch, and the small, decisive noises of metal locking into place sounded enormous in the hush.

The boy stepped into the pilot house, touched a button, and the boat sank. Water swirled outside the ports as we descended the shaft. Even then, there was none of the scurry we had expected—no boots running, no shouted orders. The acoustic landscape remained strangely clean, as if all the world’s noise had been pulled away to leave only our own pulse and the faint working of the vessel.

While he guided the craft, I moved from cabin to cabin, searching for any member of the crew.

The submarine was entirely deserted.

It felt like finding a pantry stocked and unlocked in a famine—too good, too sudden. When I returned to the pilot house, the boy held out a paper.

“This may explain the absence of the crew,” he said.

It was a radio-aerial message to the commander of the submarine:

“The slaves have risen. Come with what men you have and those that you can gather on the way. Too late to get aid from Omean. They are massacring all within the amphitheatre. Issus is threatened. Haste.

“ZITHAD”

“Zithad is Dator of the guards of Issus,” explained the youth. “We gave them a bad scare—one that they will not soon forget.”

“Let us hope that it is but the beginning of the end of Issus,” I said.

“Only our first ancestor knows,” he replied, using the old phrase as naturally as one might invoke the origin of a recipe—acknowledging that some knowledge is inherited yet never fully owned.

We reached the submarine pool in Omean without incident. There we debated sinking the craft before leaving her, but decided it would add nothing to our chances. There were plenty of blacks on Omean to thwart us if we were taken; a few more arriving from Issus’s gardens would not worsen odds already sharp as broken glass.

We stood for a moment with the damp air around us and the faint lap of water below, considering how to pass the guards patrolling the island about the pool. The voices of the sentries carried—crisp, harsh, made harsher still by stone that returned every syllable like an accusation. It struck me then how power speaks not only through weapons, but through sound: who gets to shout, who must whisper, whose language fills the corridors until it seems the only language that exists.

At last a plan came to me—simple, audacious, almost domestic in its reliance on the everyday machinery of authority: the written order, the signature, the assumption that ink is truth.

“What is the name or title of the officer in charge of these guards?” I asked.

“A fellow named Torith was on duty when we entered this morning,” he replied.

“Good. And what is the name of the commander of the submarine?”

“Yersted.”

I found a dispatch blank in the cabin and wrote, with as firm a hand as the light allowed:

“Dator Torith: Return these two slaves at once to Shador.

“YERSTED”

“That will be the simpler way to return,” I said, smiling as I handed the forged order to the boy. “Come. We shall see now how well it works.”

“But our swords!” he protested. “What shall we say to explain them?”

“Since we cannot explain them, we shall have to leave them behind,” I replied.

He hesitated. “Is it not the extreme of rashness to thus put ourselves again, unarmed, in the power of the First Born?”

“It is the only way,” I said. “You may trust me to find a way out of the prison of Shador. And once out, we shall find no great difficulty in arming ourselves in a country that abounds so plentifully in armed men.”

“As you say,” he answered, with a shrug and a quick smile that held more courage than youth ought to have. “I could not follow another leader who inspired greater confidence than you. Come—let us put your ruse to the test.”

We left our swords behind us, the weight of that choice settling into my shoulders as tangibly as any chain. Then, boldly, we emerged from the hatchway and strode to the main exit that led to the sentry’s post and the office of the Dator of the guard.

At sight of us, the guards sprang forward in surprise. Rifles levelled; a sharp chorus of commands cut the air. It was the kind of sound that makes the stomach tighten, as if the body remembers, in its oldest instincts, the snap of predators.

I held out the message to one of them. He took it, glanced at the name, and handed it to Torith, who was emerging from his office to learn the cause of the commotion.

Torith read the order. For a moment his eyes moved from the paper to our faces and back again, suspicion working behind them like a knife testing the grain of meat.

“Where is Dator Yersted?” he asked.

My heart sank, heavy as a stone dropped into deep water. I cursed myself in silence for a stupid fool in not sinking the submarine—to make my lie unchallengeable.

“His orders were to return immediately to the temple landing,” I replied, keeping my tone flat, as though I were repeating an uninteresting detail from a duty roster.

Torith took a half step toward the entrance to the pool as if he meant to corroborate my story. In that instant, everything hung in the balance. Had he gone and seen the empty submarine still lying at her wharf, the whole thin crust of my concoction would have broken beneath us.

But he did not go. Either the authority of the signature soothed him, or the sheer improbability of two slaves walking willingly into custody made the message seem self-proving. Boldness, like a strong spice, can disguise a poor cut—at least for a time.

“Were you connected with the rising of the slaves?” he demanded. “We have just had meagre reports of some such event.”
