“All were involved,” I said. “But it amounted to little. The guards quickly overcame and killed the majority of us.”

He seemed satisfied; perhaps the promise of order restored was the sweetest thing he could taste.

“Take them to Shador,” he ordered, turning to one of his subordinates.

We entered a small boat lying beside the island. The water slapped softly against the hull, a sound almost gentle—too gentle for the business at hand. In a few minutes we were disembarking upon Shador.

Here, the routine of captivity resumed with the efficiency of a well-run kitchen—doors opened and shut, keys turned, chains counted, bodies placed where they belonged. We were returned to our cells: I with Xodar, the boy by himself. And behind locked doors, with the echoes of the guards’ voices fading into the stone, we were again prisoners of the First Born.

Xodar sat with his long legs folded beneath him, the pale sheen of the phosphorescent dome washing his sharp features in an underwater light that made him look carved from old bone. While I told him what had passed in the arena at the rites—Issus upon her throne, the ceremonial blades, my own point at her throat—he listened as though I were recounting some impossible recipe: a man seasoning the gods and finding they tasted of mere flesh.

When I finished he let out a breath that was half laugh, half sob.

“It is the final proof,” he said. “No more is needed. She is no goddess—only a wicked old woman. She has fattened herself on lies, and kept Barsoom hungry for truth.”

“She is still all-powerful here,” I answered. “A cook may know the king is mortal and still be hanged for serving him a bitter dish. We leave at the first moment that offers itself.”

He tipped his head, listening, as though even in the stone cell he could hear the broad breath of the Lost Sea beyond the walls. “A propitious moment,” he repeated, amused. “In all my life I have never seen one in which a prisoner of the First Born might escape.”

“To-night will do as well as any,” I said.

His eyes narrowed with the practical hunger of a man who has decided. “It will soon be night. How may I aid?”

“Can you swim?” I asked.

“No slimy silian that haunts the depths of Korus is more at home in water than is Xodar.”

“Good. The red one likely cannot. There is scarce water enough in his world to teach it to him. One of us will have to bear him through. I had thought we might keep the whole distance below the surface, but he will not do it.”

“The red one is to go with us?” Xodar’s tone held both surprise and calculation.

“Yes.”

“It is well. Three blades are better than two.” His gaze flicked to me, then away, as if he were tasting an old idea and finding it changed. “I have watched him in the arena at the rites of Issus many times. Never, until I saw you fight, had I seen one so unconquerable when odds were thick. When you two move, one might swear you master and pupil—or father and son. There is the same grim smile, the same contempt that maddens an enemy. Even your faces—there is a resemblance.”

“Be that as it may,” I said, “he is a great fighter. With him we will be hard to overcome. And if my friend Tars Tarkas, Jeddak of Thark, were but here—” I let the thought hang. There are names that are like old household spices; say them, and the mouth remembers feasts and firesides that are far away.

Xodar’s laugh came short. “It will be the whole world against you when they learn from whence you came. That, too, is one of Issus’ superstitions—another taste she has trained tongues to crave. She works through the Holy Therns, and those white fools are as ignorant of her true self as the outer world. Her decrees come to them written in blood upon strange parchment. They think a goddess’ hand lays them on guarded altars—altars no one may touch without being seen.”

His voice fell into the cadence of confession, and as he spoke I heard beneath his words the distant, ceaseless lap of Omean against stone—water worrying rock with patient insistence, the oldest kind of argument.

“I myself have borne these messages for years. There is a tunnel from Issus’ temple to Matai Shang’s principal temple, dug ages ago by slaves in such secrecy no thern guessed its existence. The therns, for their part, have temples dotted across the civilized world. Priests the people never see teach of the Mysterious River Iss, the Valley Dor, the Lost Sea of Korus—so that the deluded will take the voluntary pilgrimage. It swells thern wealth and adds to their slaves.

“Thus the therns gather riches and labor, and the First Born take what they need. Sometimes we raid the outer world ourselves—capturing females of royal houses, the newest battleships, and the artisans who build them, that we may copy what we cannot create.

“We are a non-productive race,” he added with bitter pride, as if repeating a proverb he had once been forced to honor. “We boast of it. A First Born does not labor or invent. Lower orders exist to feed our idleness. Fighting is all that counts. Were it not for that, there would be more of us than Barsoom could support—for none of us, so far as I know, dies a natural death. Our females would live forever but that we tire of them and remove them to make room for others. Issus alone is protected against death. She has lived for countless ages.”

“Would not the other Barsoomians live forever,” I asked, “but for the doctrine that drives them down Iss at or before their thousandth year?”

“I feel now,” he said slowly, “there is no doubt they are the same species as we. I hope I shall live to fight for them—in atonement for what I have done through ignorance, as generations were taught.”

A weird call rang across the waters then—thin and carrying, as a conch note carries over a shore. I had heard it the evening before and knew it marked the ending of the day, when the men of Omean spread their silks upon battleship and cruiser and surrender to that dreamless Martian sleep.

Our guard came to inspect us one last time. His sandals whispered; the metal of his accoutrements made the small, habitual sounds of routine, the complacent clink of a system that has never been challenged. He looked, grunted, went. The heavy door closed, sealing us again in stone.

We waited until he had time to return to his quarters. Then I sprang to the grated window and surveyed the near waters. A monster battleship lay perhaps a quarter mile from the island, hulking and dark against the glow. Between her and the shore floated smaller cruisers and one-man scouts. Only the battleship carried a watch. I saw him plainly, a solitary figure in the upper works, and watched him spread his sleeping silks upon his tiny platform. He lay down like a man who trusts the world to stay as it is.

Lax discipline, but why not? No enemy guessed that such a fleet existed—nor even the First Born, nor the Sea of Omean. Why keep ears tuned for a sound that has never been heard?

I dropped to the floor again and described the craft to Xodar.

“There is one there,” he said, and for an instant the old pride warmed his voice, “my personal property, built to carry five men. The swiftest of the swift. If we can board her, we can at least make a memorable run for liberty.” He spoke of her engines and fittings with a lover’s attention, naming each feature as a cook names his tools—the right blade for a clean cut, the right fire for a quick sear.

In his explanation I recognized a trick of gearing Kantos Kan had shown me once, when we sailed under false names in the navy of Zodanga beneath Sab Than. Only Helium gears thus. So the First Born had stolen it; they were thieves even in their vaunted idleness. And I knew Xodar spoke truth of her speed. Nothing that cleaves the thin air of Mars matches the ships of Helium.

We agreed to wait an hour, until stragglers had sought their silks. Meanwhile I was to fetch the red youth to our cell, so we would be ready to move together.

I sprang to the top of the partition wall and pulled myself up. Along the flat surface, scarce a foot wide, I walked to the neighboring cell. The boy sat upon his bench, leaning back and gazing up at the glowing dome as though it were a hearth. When he saw me balancing above him his eyes widened, then a grin broke across his face—quick, bright, full of that reckless appreciation a fighter has for any bold stroke.

As I stooped to drop to the floor beside him he motioned me to wait and whispered up, “Catch my hand. I can almost leap to the top of that wall myself. I have tried it many times. Each day I come a little closer. Some day I should have made it.”

I lay flat across the wall and reached down. With a short run from the center of his cell he sprang. His fingers met mine; I locked my grip and hauled, and he came up beside me, breathing quietly through his teeth—controlled, practiced.

“You are the first jumper I ever saw among the red men,” I told him.

He smiled. “It is not strange. I will tell you why when we have more time.”

Together we returned to Xodar. We descended and talked in low voices until the hour had passed, setting each step of our plan like measures of grain—this much risk, this much hope, nothing wasted. We bound ourselves by solemn oath to fight to the death for one another against whatever enemies should confront us. We knew that even if we slipped the First Born, the outer world might still rise against us—the power of superstition is like a dominant tongue: it commands mouths that never chose it, and punishes those who speak otherwise.

It was agreed that I should navigate once we reached the craft, and that if we made the outer world we should attempt to reach Helium without a stop.

“Why Helium?” the boy asked.

“I am a prince of Helium,” I said.

He gave me a peculiar look—measuring, intent, as though my words carried a flavor he recognized but could not name in his own speech. He said nothing more, and in the press of the moment I let it go.

“Come,” I said at last. “Now is as good a time as any. Let us go.”

In a moment I was atop the partition wall again with the boy beside me. I unbuckled my harness, snapped it into a single long strap, and lowered it to Xodar. He grasped it and climbed swiftly, sitting beside us with a soft laugh.

“How simple,” he murmured.

“The balance should be even simpler,” I replied.

I raised myself to the top of the outer wall so I could peer over and find the passing sentry. For five minutes I waited, muscles quiet, hearing my own blood and the distant water, and then he came on his slow beat around the structure.

I watched him make the turn at the end of the building, carrying him out of sight of the side that would witness our dash. The instant he disappeared I drew Xodar up, placed the strap in his hands, and lowered him quickly to the ground. The boy slid down after him.

As arranged, they did not wait for me. They walked slowly toward the water—perhaps a hundred yards—directly past the guard-house where sleeping soldiers lay.

When they had taken a dozen steps I dropped too and followed at a leisurely pace. Passing the guard-house I felt the pull of the blades within. Swords are more than metal here; they are language, status, survival—the lexicon of force the First Born have made everyone speak. If ever men would need that vocabulary, it was we three on the path we’d chosen.

I glanced toward the shore. Xodar and the boy had slipped over the dock’s edge into the water. They clung to metal rings set into the concrete-like substance at water level, with only mouths and nostrils above the surface, as planned.

The lure of the weapons was strong. I hesitated—just a breath, just the blink between impulse and wisdom—and in that blink I betrayed us. Another moment found me creeping toward the guard-house door.

Gently I pressed it open a crack. Inside, a dozen blacks lay stretched upon their silks, sunk in profound slumber. At the far side stood a rack holding swords and firearms. I pushed the door wider, easing my body through. A hinge gave out a resentful groan—old metal complaining like a harsh instrument in a quiet room. One man stirred. My heart stopped. The world narrowed to the sound of his breath.

With a noiseless spring I lit beside him, hands poised near his throat, waiting for the opening of his eyes. Time dragged, thick as cold paste. Then he turned, sighing, and resumed the even respiration of deep sleep.

I stepped between and over the sprawled bodies until I reached the rack. All were quiet. Their breathing rose and fell in a rhythm so steady it was almost musical—soothing, domestic, the sound of men who have never had to keep watch over their own power.

I drew a long-sword. The scabbard scraped its holder with a sound to my strained nerves like iron being rasped. I waited for the eruption of alarm. None came.

The second sword slid free without sound. The third clanked with a frightful din. I braced to charge for the doorway, to take my chance in open fight. But again, to my intense surprise, not a black moved. Either they slept like the dead, or fear had made every sound inside my skull larger than it truly was.

I turned to go. My eye fell upon the revolvers. I could not carry more than one; I was already laden. As I lifted a revolver from its pin, I noticed an open window beside the rack—an easy exit onto the dock, scarcely twenty feet from the water. Relief surged.

Then, from the opposite door, a figure stepped in. The officer of the guard stood looking straight at me. He took in the scene at a glance—my arms full of swords, the revolver in my hand—and his face hardened with the quick comprehension of a man whose world is about to be shamed.

Our revolvers rose together. The two reports were so nearly one that they sounded as a single clap. I felt the wind of his bullet brush past my ear. At the same instant he crumpled. Where I hit him I did not know—nor whether I killed him—for even as he fell I was through the window.

Water swallowed me. The sea of Omean closed above my head, and the three of us struck out for the little flier a hundred yards away.

Xodar bore the boy. I carried three long-swords, and the revolver I had dropped. The weight made my arms feel bound in wet cloth; though we were strong swimmers our progress seemed slow, as if the sea itself wished to keep its secret. I swam entirely beneath the surface; Xodar had to rise often so the youth could breathe. It was a wonder we were not discovered.

We reached the boat and clambered aboard before the watch on the battleship, roused by the shots, detected us. Then an alarm gun bellowed—deep, brutal—and its boom reverberated under the rocky dome until the air itself seemed to vibrate like a struck kettle.

Instantly the sleeping thousands were awake. Decks filled with fighting-men. An alarm on Omean was rare; it tasted of panic.

We cast away before the first report died. A heartbeat later we were rising from the sea. I lay full length along the deck at the controls. Xodar and the boy stretched behind me, prone to offer as little resistance to the air as possible.

“Rise high,” Xodar whispered close to my ear. “They dare not fire their heavy guns toward the dome—the fragments would rain among their own craft. If we are high enough our keel plates will protect us from rifle fire.”

I lifted her as he bade. Below, men leaped into the water by hundreds, striking out for small cruisers and scouts moored about the big ships. Larger craft were getting under way but stayed on the surface, their propellers raising a constant purr and churn that, mingled with shouted orders and cracking rifles, became a kind of poisoned storm-song. In that clamor I heard what all tyrannies breed: not music, but noise—the harsh ecology of domination, where every sound is command or fear and none is peace.

“A little to your right!” Xodar cried—there are no true compass points in Omean, where every direction feels due north.

I dared not throw the speed lever to its utmost for fear of overrunning the mouth of the shaft to the world above, but even so we were moving at a clip few craft could match on this windless sea.

Smaller fliers began to rise toward us. Then Xodar shouted, sharp as a snapped cord: “The shaft! Dead ahead!”

I saw it: a black, yawning opening in the glowing dome, like a wound in the underworld.

A ten-man cruiser rose directly before us to cut off our escape. It was the only vessel that truly barred the way, but at her angle and speed she would be between us and the shaft in time. She climbed at about forty-five degrees, intent on skimming above our deck and combing us with grapples.

There was one forlorn hope, and I took it.

To try to pass over her would let her force us against the rocks above—we were already too near. To dive beneath would put us precisely where she wanted us, helpless under her bulk. On either side other craft converged. The alternative I chose was all risk with a thin thread of chance.

As we neared I rose as though to pass above, tempting her to do what she did: climb steeper to drive me higher. At the last instant I yelled to my companions to hold tight, shoved the speed lever to its final notch, and deflected our bows until we ran horizontal at terrific velocity—straight for the cruiser’s keel.

Her commander may have understood then; if so, he had only time to taste fear before it swallowed him. Almost at impact I turned our bows upward. With a shattering jolt we struck. What I had gambled on occurred: already tilted, the cruiser was carried completely backward. Her crew fell twisting and screaming through the air to the water far below, while the cruiser, propellers still madly churning, dived headfirst after them to the bottom of the Sea of Omean.

Our own bows were crushed. The collision nearly hurled us from the deck. We landed in a wildly clutching heap at the very extremity of the flier. Xodar and I caught the hand-rail. The boy would have plunged over had I not snatched his ankle as it swung out into emptiness.

Unguided, our vessel careened in its mad flight, rising ever nearer the rock above. For an instant the ceiling of stone filled my vision, close enough to make the skin crawl. Then I regained the levers. With barely fifty feet to spare I leveled her again and drove toward the black mouth of the shaft.

The collision had slowed us. Now a hundred swift scouts were close. Xodar had warned me: if we ascended the shaft by repulsive rays alone, with propellers idle, craft with greater buoyancy would overtake us easily. The fastest boats rarely carry large buoyancy tanks; they trade lift for speed.

As many vessels were already near, it seemed inevitable we would be overhauled in the shaft—captured or killed in short order.

But there is always another way. When you cannot go over, under, or around, there remains the oldest, hardest choice: through.

“Reverse?” Xodar screamed behind me. “For the love of your first ancestor, reverse. We are at the shaft!”

“Hold tight!” I shouted. “Grasp the boy—hold tight. We are going straight up the shaft.”

The words were hardly out before we swept beneath the pitch-black opening. I threw the bow hard up, dragged the speed lever to its last notch, and with one hand on a stanchion and the other on the steering-wheel, hung on like a man who has put his soul into a single violent motion.

I heard Xodar’s sharp intake of breath—then a grim laugh. The boy laughed too and said something I could not catch for the shriek of wind as speed tore sound into a single continuous blade.

I looked above, hoping for stars to guide me, to keep the hurtling craft centered. To touch the shaft’s side at this speed would mean instant death. But not a star showed—only utter, impenetrable darkness.

Then I glanced down and saw a circle of light diminishing rapidly: the mouth of the opening above the phosphorescent radiance of Omean. By that bright ring I steered, keeping it as perfect as I could. It was a slender cord holding us from destruction, and I think I steered that night less by skill than by the blind instinct that has saved me too often to doubt.

We were not long within. Perhaps speed itself saved us; we had entered true and passed through before there was time to drift. Omean lies perhaps two miles below the surface crust of Mars. We must have been traveling near two hundred miles an hour—Martian fliers are swift—so at most we were in the shaft under forty seconds.

We had been out for some moments before I realized we had done the impossible.

Black darkness wrapped us. There were neither moons nor stars. Never before had I seen such a sky on Mars, and for an instant I was wholly at sea, robbed of the familiar celestial landmarks. Then understanding came: it was summer at the south pole. The ice cap was melting, and those meteoric phenomena—clouds, unknown upon most of Barsoom—had rolled over this portion of the planet, shutting out the light of heaven.

My son.

For an instant the words were only sound—vibration in the thin Martian air, a note struck somewhere between the bones of my ear and the deeper chambers of memory. Then I rose, slowly, as though gravity itself had changed its mind, and looked full upon the youth who had stepped from shadow into the scant light.

Now that my eyes had leisure to read him, I understood the pull he had had upon me from the first. In the clean planes of his cheeks and the proud set of his head there lived the old, unforgotten beauty of his mother—yet tempered, hardened into a masculine mould; and in the grey eyes that met mine there was no mistaking the mirror. Their glance held the same quick fire, the same stubborn refusal to bow to the universe, that I had known all my life.

He stood very still, as if fear of disappointment were a physical weight upon his shoulders—half hope, half uncertainty.

“Tell me of your mother,” I said, and my voice did not sound like my own. “Tell me all you can of the years that a relentless fate has stolen from me.”

At that he broke. With a cry that was joy and ache together he sprang forward and flung his arms about my neck. I held him—my boy—and in that brief, fierce clasp I felt tears gather, hot and foolish, threatening to choke me. Let them. A man may be iron in battle and yet melt where women and children are concerned; it is no weakness, only proof that he has something worth fighting for.

“Your stature, your manner,” he said against my shoulder, “the terrible ferocity of your swordsmanship—my mother has told me of them a thousand times. Yet even so, I could scarce believe. Do you know what convinced me more than all else?”

“What, my boy?” I asked.

“Your first words to me. They were of her. No other man—no other—would have thought first of my mother unless he loved her as she has always said my father did.”

“For long years,” I said, and it hurt to say it aloud, “there has not been a moment when the radiant vision of her face was not before me. Tell me of her.”

“Those who have known her longest say she has not changed—unless to grow more beautiful, were that possible. Only… when she thinks I do not watch, her face grows very sad and wistful. She thinks ever of you. All Helium mourns with her and for her. Her grandfather’s people love her; they loved you also, and worship your memory as the saviour of Barsoom.

“Each year the anniversary is held—the day you raced across a near-dead world to unlock the secret of that awful portal that guarded the power of life for millions. They feast and give thanks; but there are tears in the celebration, because the giver of the gift is not there to share it. Upon all Barsoom there is no greater name than John Carter.”

“And by what name has your mother called you?” I asked, clinging to particulars as a starving man clings to crumbs.

“The people of Helium wished me named for my father,” he said, “but my mother would not. She said that you and she had chosen a name together, and that your wish must stand before all others. So she called me Carthoris—hers and yours joined.”

It was then that Xodar, who had remained at the wheel while my world remade itself, called out with the clipped urgency of a man who hears disaster in the ship’s own voice.

“She is dropping badly by the head, John Carter,” he said. “While we rose it was not to be seen, but on a level course—look. The wound in her bow has opened one of the forward ray tanks.”

I went forward and examined the rent. It was worse than I had believed. Not only did the forced angle at which we must hold the bow to keep a horizontal flight drag against our speed, but the forward repulsive rays were bleeding away as surely as blood from an opened vein. Within an hour—perhaps less—we would hang stern-up and helpless in the sky like a butchered bird on a hook.

I took the helm again and drove the engine wide open. The little craft answered bravely; she shrieked through the thin air northward at a pace that made the dead world beneath blur into ochre and shadow. Behind me, in the cramped bow, Carthoris and Xodar crouched with tools, their hands busy, their brows drawn—patching, propping, attempting the hopeless: to stem the invisible tide of escaping force.

Night still lay on Barsoom when we passed the northern boundary of the ice cap and slid beneath the last of the cloud banks. Below us unrolled a typical Martian vista—rolling sea bottoms of ancient, long-vanished oceans, low hills like the ribs of a sleeping beast, and here and there the hulks of dead cities: grim architectures piled in silence, tenanted only by memories and by the great white apes that haunt old grandeur with old ferocity.

The bow sagged lower, lower. It became impossible to keep her level without turning our flight into a murderous dive. At last I shut down the engine rather than risk driving us nose-first to destruction.

With sunrise came the final failure. As the new light swept the dark away, our craft gave a spasmodic plunge, rolled half upon her side, and began to swing in a slow, drunken circle—deck tilting, bow dropping, stern rising with every heartbeat.

We clung to hand-rails and stanchions until there was no longer any “down” that could be trusted; then we snapped our harness buckles to the rings along the sides. A moment later the deck reared to a full ninety degrees, and we hung in our leather, feet dangling a thousand yards above the ground, the wind tugging at us like a hungry thing.

The controlling levers were close enough to my swing that I could reach them. I stretched, fingers straining, and found the lever that directed the rays of repulsion. The boat responded, as though grateful for instruction, and very gently we began to descend.

It took fully half an hour to touch ground.

North of us rose a range of hills, not mountains, but lofty enough to break sight-lines and shelter us from any aerial scouts that might hunt this way. We set off toward them, and within an hour we were in their time-rounded gullies, among the hardy flowering plants that somehow make beauty in Barsoom’s waste places.

There, too, grew the huge milk-giving shrubs—those strange, swollen-stemmed plants that are food and drink to the roaming green hordes. To men who had lived on danger and haste, it was a blessing more immediate than any prayer. We broke the thick skins and drew out the pale, nourishing milk. It had a faintly mineral sweetness and a coolness that slid down the throat like mercy. Even the sound of it—soft gurgle into our improvised cups, a steady, sane domestic noise—seemed to quiet the nervous ringing in my skull after days of engines, battle cries, and the dead hush of Dor.

Beneath a clustered growth that hid us perfectly from above, we lay down to sleep. For me it was the first true sleep in many hours—the beginning of my fifth day upon Barsoom since I had been snatched from my cottage on the Hudson and flung into Dor: the valley beautiful, the valley hideous. In all that time I had slept but twice, and once only by the ticking mercy of a clock within the thern storehouse.

It was mid-afternoon when a hand seized mine—warm fingers, urgent—and covered it with kisses. I started awake to find Thuvia bending over me, her beautiful face lit as though the sun had risen inside her.

“My Prince! My Prince!” she cried. “It is you. I mourned you as dead. My ancestors have been good to me; I have not lived in vain.”

Her voice woke Xodar and Carthoris. My son stared at her, astonishment plain upon him; but Thuvia saw no one but me. She would have flung her arms about my neck, smothering me with gratitude and relief, had I not disengaged myself gently but firmly.

“Come, come, Thuvia,” I said, striving to steady her. “You are overwrought by danger and hardship. You forget yourself—and you forget I am the husband of the Princess of Helium.”

“I forget nothing, my Prince,” she replied, and if she spoke calmly now it was only because she had willed her heart into obedience. “You have spoken no word of love to me, nor do I expect you ever shall. But nothing can prevent me from loving you. I would not take the place of Dejah Thoris. My greatest ambition is to serve you forever—as your slave. No greater boon could I ask.”

Her words made me uneasy in a way no sword-point ever had. I knew the Martian custom—how high honour guards every woman in a man’s household even when she bears the name of slave. Yet I had never chosen women as my body servants; I had never wished to complicate the clean lines of duty with the tender entanglements of the heart.

“And if ever I return to Helium, Thuvia,” I said, “you shall go with me—but as an honoured equal, not as a slave. In Helium you will find handsome young nobles enough who would face Issus herself for a smile from you; and we shall see you married quickly to one of the best. Forget this gratitude-born infatuation. I value your friendship more.”

“You are my master,” she said simply. “It shall be as you say.” Yet there was sadness in the cadence, as though a song had lost a note that could not be replaced.

“How came you here?” I asked. “And where is Tars Tarkas?”

At the name, her face changed.

“The great Thark,” she said slowly, “I fear is dead. He fought like a god, but a multitude of green warriors—of a horde not his own—overwhelmed him. The last I saw they bore him wounded and bleeding to the deserted city from which they had sallied to attack us.”

“You are not sure he is dead, then?” I demanded. “Where is this city?”

“Just beyond this range of hills,” she answered. “The vessel in which you resigned your place—so nobly—defied our skill in navigation. We drifted aimlessly for two days. Then we abandoned it and attempted to go on foot to the nearest waterway. Yesterday we crossed these hills and came upon the dead city beyond.

“We had passed within its streets, walking toward the central portion, when at an intersecting avenue we saw green warriors approaching. Tars Tarkas was in advance. They saw him, but they did not see me. He sprang back and forced me into a doorway and told me to remain hidden until I could escape, making my way to Helium if possible.

“‘There will be no escape for me now,’ he said. ‘These are the Warhoon of the South. When they have seen my metal, it will be to the death.’

“Then he stepped out to meet them.” Her voice trembled despite her effort. “Ah, my Prince—such fighting. For an hour they swarmed about him until the Warhoon dead formed a hill where he had stood. At last they overwhelmed him—those behind pushing the foremost upon him until there was no room to swing his great sword. Then he stumbled. He went down. They rolled over him like a wave. When they carried him away toward the heart of the city, I think he was dead, for I did not see him move.”

“Before we go farther we must be sure,” I said. “I cannot leave Tars Tarkas alive among the Warhoons. To-night I shall enter the city and make certain.”

“And I shall go with you,” Carthoris said at once, stepping forward as though his blood had leapt to answer mine.

“And I,” said Xodar.

“Neither of you,” I replied. “This is work for stealth and strategy, not force. One man alone may succeed where more would invite disaster. I shall go alone. If I need help, I will return.”

They did not like it, but both were soldiers; and it had been agreed that command rested with me. The sun already leaned low, and Barsoom’s night falls suddenly, like a curtain dropped. We waited only long enough for the last light to drain from the sky.

I gave them parting instructions in case I did not return, and then I left them—Thuvia’s eyes following me like a hand I could feel between my shoulders—and trotted toward the city at a rapid dogtrot.

When I emerged from the hills, the nearer moon was winging its wild flight overhead, throwing bright beams that turned the barbaric splendour of the ancient metropolis to burnished silver. The city had been built upon gentle foothills that, in some dim past, had sloped down to meet a living sea. Because of that I could enter from the hillside streets without being seen, while the Warhoons—when they come and go—usually cross the dead sea bottoms that face the city.

Within the streets I kept to dense wall-shadows. At each intersection I halted, listening not only with my ears but with my skin, feeling for the slightest change in the city’s breathing. Then I sprang across to the opposite shadow, and so advanced, block by block, without detection.

As I neared the inhabited portion, I knew it before I saw it. The sonic world changed. There came the squealing and grunting of thoats and zitidars corralled in hollow courtyards, the occasional clank of metal, the low, clipped monosyllables of green men—sounds as characteristic of green Martian life as the scent of their rancid oils and the dust of their streets. Those animal voices, crude as they were, struck something in me like the remembered crackle of a hearth: a domestic noise, a lived-in noise, as opposed to the dead, commercial hush of temples and false gods. I had first courted Dejah Thoris to such accompaniment, in the marble halls of Korad, while beasts complained softly in courtyards below and the city itself listened.

At the far corner of the first square occupied by the horde, I saw warriors emerging from several buildings. All moved in one direction, toward a great structure in the centre of the plaza. That, by custom, could only be the quarters of their principal chieftain or the audience chamber where the Jeddak met his jeds and lesser chiefs. In either case something was afoot—something that might concern the capture of Tars Tarkas.

To reach that building I must traverse the full length of a square, cross a broad avenue, and cover a portion of the plaza itself. From the animal noise rising from every courtyard, I knew there were many people in the surrounding piles—likely several communities of the Warhoons of the South.

I stood at the corner where the avenue I had entered met the first intersecting avenue south of the plaza. The buildings along the south side of the square appeared uninhabited—no lights, no movement—so I chose one and slipped in to gain an inner courtyard, hoping to move unseen along the rear.

Nothing interrupted me. I passed through a deserted pile and came into the court, where a great herd of thoats and zitidars moved restlessly about, cropping the moss-like ochre growth that overcovers uncultivated Mars. A light breeze came from the northwest; it would not carry my scent to them. Had it done so, their grunting and squealing would have risen to a storm that would draw warriors like flies to blood.

Close to the east wall, beneath overhanging second-floor balconies, I crept in dense shadow along the full length of the court to the north buildings. Here there were lights—about three floors up. Above that, darkness.

To pass through lighted rooms was impossible; they swarmed with green Martian men and women. My only path lay through the upper floors. To gain them I must scale the wall.

The balcony of the second floor was easy—an agile leap, hands grasping stone rail, a swift pull, and I was up. Through open windows I saw green folk squatting upon sleeping silks and furs, grunting an occasional monosyllable—speech made sparse because their minds carry so much unspoken between them. Their language, pared to bone, always struck me as both powerful and perilous; so much can be commanded without being said, so much can be erased without a word to witness the loss.

A warrior entered from the hall.

“Come, Tan Gama,” he cried. “We are to take the Thark before Kab Kadja. Bring another with you.”

The one addressed rose. He beckoned to a fellow near him. The three left the apartment together.

If I could follow them, I might find Tars Tarkas at once—perhaps free him. At the least I would learn where they held him.

To my right was a door from the balcony into an unlighted hall. On impulse I stepped within. The corridor was broad, running straight to the front of the building, with doorways opening on either side.

I had barely entered when I saw the three warriors at the far end. Then they turned right and vanished. I hurried after them, reckless now, because Fate had tossed me a thin chance and I could not afford to let it fall.

At the corridor’s end I found a spiral stair leading both above and below. They had taken it. From my knowledge of these buildings and of Warhoon habit, I was sure they had gone down.

I had once been a prisoner of the northern Warhoons; I remembered their underground pits, remembered the smell of stone that never sees sun, remembered the way sound is swallowed there so that a man begins to doubt his own breathing. It seemed certain that Tars Tarkas lay in such darkness beneath some nearby structure.

I descended.

At the lower landing the shaft continued into deeper pits, and a flickering torch below revealed the three I hunted. Down they went, and behind them I followed, far enough back to keep beyond the outer rim of their light.

The way led through a maze of corridors, unlighted except for that wavering flame. After perhaps a hundred yards they turned abruptly through a doorway on the right. I followed as quickly as I dared, and at the doorway I saw into the chamber.

There they were, removing chains that secured a massive figure to the wall—Tars Tarkas, the great Thark. Even in that poor light his bulk was unmistakable; even in captivity his posture held contempt for it.

They hustled him roughly out, so quickly that I came within a breath of discovery. I sprang down the corridor in the direction I had been moving, far enough to slip outside their torch’s radius. I assumed they would return the way they came, carrying him away from me.

To my chagrin, they turned directly toward me.

There was nothing for it but to keep ahead of them, always skirting the edge of the light, never letting its thin blade touch me. I dared not duck into a side passage at random; I did not know this underworld well enough, and chance might throw me directly into their path.

I ran through blackness that felt thick as cloth. The faint torch-glow behind gave just enough radiance to show the passage directly before me, to keep me from smashing into walls at turns. Yet every step carried the knowledge that beneath these ancient cities are pits, wells, breaks in the stone—and things that live where sound and light do not belong.

At last I came to a place where five corridors diverged from a common point. I chose one and hurried along it for some distance when, suddenly, the faint light behind me vanished.

I stopped, listening. Silence lay down like a hand across my mouth. The green men’s naked feet gave no echo.

They had taken another corridor.

Relief came sharp and immediate; it is safer to trail than to be trailed. I turned back, but now the darkness was complete, and I could not run. I had to feel my way with one hand on the wall, inching along, counting turns by touch. Time stretched until it seemed I had been underground all my life.

At last I found the meeting point again—recognized it by groping across openings until I counted five. Yet in none of them did I see even a thread of light.

I listened. Very faintly—so faint I doubted it at first—I thought I heard the clank of side arms, far away, from the middle corridor. I took it, moving quickly now, stopping now and again to listen for repetition. But only darkness and silence answered me, and soon I had to admit I had followed a blind lead.

I retraced my steps—only to find myself abruptly at the mouth of three diverging corridors, where there should have been one. In my haste after the false clue I had crossed some branching without knowing it.

Here, indeed, was a pretty fix. If I could regain the place of the five ways, I might wait there and catch the warriors upon their return; my knowledge of their customs suggested they had taken Tars Tarkas to the audience chamber for sentence, saving him for the Great Games. But if I could not find my way back, I might wander these black passages for days until thirst and hunger ended it—or until something else did.

A faint shuffling sounded behind me.

I glanced over my shoulder, and my blood froze—not only from present danger, but from the memory it dragged up: a corpse in a Warhoon dungeon, my mind teetering toward madness, and then those eyes—blazing eyes—coming out of darkness to claim their feast, scraping their prize over stone.

Now I saw them again.

Two fiery points burned in the void behind me, unblinking, revealing nothing of the creature that bore them. That is their most terrible trait: they are not seen. Only the eyes exist, floating in blackness like hatred made visible.

I gripped my long-sword and backed slowly away. As I retreated, the eyes advanced. No sound came—no breath, no pad of feet—only an occasional soft shuffle, as of something dragging a dead limb.

On and on I backed, the corridor seeming endless. Then, from an intersecting passage to my right, came that same faint shuffle, and another pair of eyes appeared, approaching.

I started to renew my retreat—heard the shuffling behind again—and before I could turn I heard it to my left as well.

Darkness snapped back into light as if someone had lifted a lid from my eyes. I could not have been insensible more than a handful of heartbeats, yet the certainty of that blank interval lay in the next thing I knew: the staring eyes that had held me were gone, and a widening radiance now washed the corridor stones with torch-flare yellow.

My forehead throbbed where it had kissed the flagging in my fall. I sprang up, tasting dust, and turned toward the light.

Four green warriors were coming fast, the foremost carrying the torch high so its smoke smeared the air with the sharp, resinous scent of burning fat and dried reed. They had not yet seen me. I slid into the first intersecting passage like a shadow slipping behind a curtain, but this time I did not risk losing the spine of the place. I kept near the main artery of the pits, close enough that their footfalls and the faint clank of metal could be read like a familiar dialect.

They swept past the opening where I crouched, their gait steady, their talk low. Relief came and went in a single breath, for I recognized them at once: Tars Tarkas and his three guards—the same party I had followed down into the darkness.

I fell in behind them when the last had passed, silent as the air itself, and soon we stood before the cell where the great Thark had been chained.

Two warriors remained without while the one with the keys entered with the Thark to fasten his irons again. The others, bored and confident, began to stroll toward the spiral runway that led upward. In a moment they vanished beyond a bend.

The torch was set into an iron socket beside the door, and its wavering light illuminated the corridor and the cell at once. The flame hissed softly each time it licked at the fat-soaked wrapping; the sound reminded me bitterly of how even a small noise can rule a place—how the pits were built not merely of stone but of echoes and the obedience those echoes enforced.

When the two guards were out of sight I moved toward the doorway, a plan already hardened in me. I had no love for what I meant to do, but I saw no other path if Tars Tarkas and I were to stand again in the open air together—if we were to find my little camp in the hills and the others depending upon me.

Keeping to the wall, I came close to the cell entrance and raised my longsword over my head, both hands on the grip, so that one swift blow would end it the instant the jailer stepped out.

I will not dress what followed in any fine words. There are moments a man carries like a stone under his tongue—always there, never spoken. It is enough that within another minute Tars Tarkas, now wearing the metal of a Warhoon chief, was hurrying down the corridor toward the spiral runway with the torch in his hand to light the way, and a dozen paces behind him followed John Carter, Prince of Helium.

The two companions of the man who lay now beside the cell door had just started to ascend when the Thark came into view.

“Why so long, Tan Gama?” called one.

“I had trouble with a lock,” replied Tars Tarkas without hesitation, the borrowed voice rough but steady. “And now I find that I have left my short-sword in the Thark’s cell. Go you on; I’ll return and fetch it.”

“As you will, Tan Gama,” the other answered. “We shall see you above directly.”

“Yes,” said Tars Tarkas, and turned as if to go back. But he waited only until they had disappeared above.

Then I joined him. We extinguished the torch—pinching the life from it so the corridor’s smell of smoke died into cold stone—and together we crept toward the spiral incline.

At the first floor we found the hall ran only half the building’s length, forcing anyone who wished to cross to the inner courtyard to pass through a rear room crowded with green folk. There was no thread of safety through that knot. We climbed to the second floor instead, where I had earlier traversed the long hallway.

Cautiously we ascended. Voices drifted down, laughter and argument, the layered noise of a people who believed themselves secure. Yet the hall above was unlit, and when we reached the top of the runway no one was in sight. We threaded the long corridor and reached the balcony overlooking the courtyard without being detected.

To our right was a window admitting to the room where I had seen Tan Gama and his companions at the beginning of the night. They had returned, and their talk slid out into the hall like steam from a pot.

“What can be detaining Tan Gama?” asked one.

“He certainly could not be all this time fetching his short-sword from the Thark’s cell,” said another.

“His short-sword?” a woman’s voice cut in. “What mean you?”

“Tan Gama left his short-sword in the Thark’s cell,” the first explained, “and left us at the runway to return and get it.”

“Tan Gama wore no short-sword this night,” the woman replied. “It was broken in to-day’s battle with the Thark, and Tan Gama gave it to me to repair. See, I have it here.”

There was the soft rasp of metal against fabric, the unmistakable whisper of a blade drawn from beneath silks and furs.

The warriors sprang to their feet; we could hear it in the scrape of their ornaments, the sudden sharpness of their breathing.

“There is something amiss here,” cried one.

“’Tis even what I myself thought when Tan Gama left us at the runway,” said another. “Methought then that his voice sounded strangely.”

“Come! Let us hasten to the pits.”

We waited to hear no more. I slung my harness into a long single strap and lowered Tars Tarkas to the courtyard beneath; an instant later I dropped to his side.

We had spoken scarcely a dozen words since I had felled Tan Gama at the cell door and seen, by torch-light, the bewilderment on the great Thark’s face.

“By this time,” he had said then, “I should have learned to wonder at nothing which John Carter accomplishes.”

That was all. He did not need to speak gratitude; in him it would have been a speech too large for the mouth. Yet I knew. This fierce warrior had been the first to greet me on that day—now twenty years gone—when I first stood upon Mars. He had met me with leveled spear and hatred as he charged down upon me beside the incubator of his horde on the dead sea bottom beyond Korad. Now, among two worlds, I counted none a better friend than Tars Tarkas, Jeddak of the Tharks.

In the shadows beneath the balcony we halted a moment to shape our next moves.

“There be five now in the party, Tars Tarkas,” I whispered. “Thuvia, Xodar, Carthoris, and ourselves. We shall need five thoats to bear us.”

“Carthoris!” he exclaimed softly. “Your son?”

“Yes. I found him in the prison of Shador, on the Sea of Omean, in the land of the First Born.”

“I know not any of these places, John Carter. Be they upon Barsoom?”

“Upon and below, my friend; but wait until we have made good our escape, and you shall hear the strangest narrative that ever a Barsoomian of the outer world gave ear to. Now we must steal our thoats and be well away to the north before these fellows discover how we have tricked them.”

We reached the great gates at the far end of the courtyard, where the mounts were kept. Five thoats. Five tempers. Five savage stomachs and the feral pride of beasts that were broken not by kindness but by force.

Even before we touched them they lifted their heads and tested us. They sniffed our unfamiliar scent and squealed—a tearing, rusty sound that set my teeth on edge—and circled, necks upreared so their gaping mouths hung high above us, the heat of their breath carrying the sour tang of ochre moss and old blood. Their hides were sleek and hairless, dark slate along back and sides, shading down eight legs to vivid yellow at the huge padded, nailless feet; their bellies were white as salt. A broad flat tail, heavier at the tip than the root, slapped and twitched with impatience. Ten feet at the shoulder, all muscle and malice—fit war-steeds for warlike men.

There were no reins or bridle; the thoat is guided by thought alone, by that strange telepathic communion Barsoom has never lost the way it has lost so much else. But communion requires recognition, and we were strangers to these brutes. Our task was to seize two that would accept our unspoken commands long enough for us to herd the rest.

As they charged about us we kept them from combining, turning aside sudden lunges, dodging the snap of jaws. Their squealing rose and fell in ugly waves that promised to carry straight to any listening warrior’s ears. The courtyard’s soundscape, usually a hollow bowl of echoes, became a beacon.

At last I managed to reach the flank of one great brute. Before he could twist away I was astride his glossy back, knees clamped, mind pressing hard against his will. A heartbeat later Tars Tarkas had mounted another. Between us we drove three more toward the gates.

Tars Tarkas rode ahead, leaned down, and flung the barriers open while I held the loose thoats from bolting back into the herd. Then we rode through into the avenue, mounts stolen, and did not waste the time to close the gates. We hurried toward the southern boundary of the dead city.

Thus far our escape had been little short of marvelous—and fortune did not desert us. We passed the outer purlieus of the silent city and came to our camp without hearing even the faintest sound of pursuit.

A low whistle—the signal agreed upon—slipped out into the thin air. The others answered at once, and we were met by Thuvia, Xodar, and Carthoris with a joy that needed no words.

There was no leisure for full telling. Tars Tarkas and Carthoris exchanged the formal greetings common upon Barsoom, yet I could read in the Thark’s eyes that he loved my boy, and in Carthoris’s carriage that the feeling was returned. Xodar and the green Jeddak were presented to one another with due ceremony. Then we set to the simple domestic labor that underlies all flights and all wars: getting bodies and beasts arranged, burdens balanced, tempers soothed, the living made ready to move.

Thuvia was lifted to the least fractious thoat. Xodar and Carthoris mounted two others. Tars Tarkas and I took the remaining pair, and we set out at a rapid pace toward the east. At the far extremity of the city we circled toward the north, and under the glorious rays of the two moons we sped noiselessly across the dead sea bottom—away from Warhoons and First Born, but toward what new dangers we could not know.

Toward noon the following day we halted to rest ourselves and the thoats. We hobbled the beasts so they might move slowly about, cropping the ochre, moss-like vegetation that serves them as both food and drink on the march. The dry stalks crackled faintly under their padded feet, a small, steady sound—almost comforting in its natural honesty after the metallic clamor of the pits.

Thuvia volunteered to stand watch while the rest of us slept an hour.

It seemed to me I had scarcely closed my eyes when I felt her hand upon my shoulder.

“Arise, O Prince,” she whispered. “There be that behind us which has the appearance of a great body of pursuers.”

She pointed back along our trail. On the far horizon a thin dark line lay like a seam in the world.

I roused the others. Tars Tarkas, towering above us all, stared longest.

“It is a great body of mounted men,” he said at last, “and they are travelling at high speed.”

There was no time to measure fear. We sprang to the hobbled thoats, freed them, mounted, and turned north again. We took flight at the highest speed of our slowest beast.

For the balance of that day and all the following night we raced across the ochre wilderness with the pursuers at our backs, ever gaining. Slowly but surely they lessened the distance. Just before dark they were near enough for us to distinguish their color—green—and all through the long night we heard behind us the clanking of their accoutrements, a hard, industrial music that invaded the open world and made even the stars seem farther away.

When the sun rose on the second day of our flight it showed the pursuing horde not a half-mile in our rear. A fiendish shout of triumph rose from their ranks.

Ahead lay a range of hills—the farther shore of the dead sea. Could we but reach those slopes our chances would improve. But Thuvia’s mount, though carrying the lightest burden, already showed exhaustion: his gait uneven, breath coming in harsh pulls that carried the stale odor of strain.

I rode beside her when her thoat staggered and lurched against mine. I saw he was going down. Before he fell I snatched Thuvia from his back and swung her behind me. She clung with her arms about my waist, her breath warm through my harness.

The double burden soon proved too much for my already overtaxed beast. Our speed faltered, and the others would proceed no faster than we could go. In that small party there was not one who would desert another—though we were of different countries, different colors, different races, different religions, and one of us of a different world.

We were close to the hills, yet the Warhoons gained so rapidly that hope thinned to a thread.

Thuvia and I were in the rear now, our thoat lagging more with every hundred yards. Suddenly I felt the girl’s warm lips press a kiss upon my shoulder.

“For thy sake, O my Prince,” she murmured.

Then her arms slipped from about me, and she was gone.

I wheeled and saw she had deliberately dropped to the ground in the very path of the demons behind us, thinking that by lightening my mount she might save me. Poor child—she did not know John Carter as she thought she did.

I turned my thoat after her at once, urging him back, hoping to reach her side and lift her again into the saddle. Carthoris must have looked behind him at the same instant, for by the time I reached her he was there too. He sprang from his mount, caught Thuvia, flung her across the beast’s back, and, turning its head toward the hills, struck it sharply with the flat of his sword to send it on.

Then he tried to do the same with mine.

The boy’s chivalrous self-sacrifice filled me with pride. Nor did I begrudge that it tore away our last frail chance for escape. The Warhoons were close now. Tars Tarkas and Xodar, seeing our absence, wheeled and charged back toward us.

Everything pointed toward the end of my second journey to Barsoom.

I hated to go out without having held my divine Princess once more, yet if Fate had written otherwise, then I would take what was coming to me. In these last moments I could at least leave behind such an account of myself that the Warhoons of the South would have food for discourse for twenty generations.

Carthoris stood unmounted beside me. I slipped from my thoat and took my place at his side. A moment later Tars Tarkas and Xodar ranged themselves on either hand, turning their mounts loose so that we might all meet the charge on equal footing.

The Warhoons were perhaps a hundred yards away when a loud explosion sounded from above and behind us. Almost at the same instant a shell burst among their advancing ranks.

Confusion tore through them like fire through dry moss. A hundred warriors toppled. Riderless thoats plunged among the dead and dying. Dismounted men were trampled in the stampede. Order dissolved into panic. As they stared upward, trying to trace the origin of the attack, retreat became a wild flight.

In another moment they were racing away from us as madly as they had been charging down.

We turned toward the sound, and there, just clearing the nearer hilltops, swung a great battleship through the air, majestic as a moving citadel. Her bow gun spoke again even as we looked, and another shell burst among the fleeing Warhoons.

As she drew nearer I could not repress a cry of elation, for upon her bows I saw the device of Helium.

Carthoris, Xodar, Tars Tarkas, and I stood where the low hills broke the wind, watching the great gray battleship slide above us with the tranquil assurance of a gull riding warm air. Its hull caught the thin Barsoomian sun and returned it in hard, metallic glints, yet what struck me more keenly than the light was the sound: the disciplined hush of well-tuned engines, the faint, steady purr of lifting fans—nothing like the rasping industrial shriek of lesser craft that scour men’s nerves raw. This was Helium’s voice in the sky, measured and confident, a music the streets below had not heard in too long a time.

A second vessel topped the hills, then a third, ghosting after their sister. From the nearer ship, one-man scouts sprang in a score from the upper decks, diving in long, swift arcs that made the air itself seem to bow before them. Their approach stitched new notes into the soundscape—whistling descents, the soft cough of jets correcting angle, the crisp clap of vanes biting into thinner air—until, in the space of a few breaths, armed sailors ringed us.

An officer stepped forward to speak, but the moment his eyes fell on Carthoris his whole body answered before his tongue could. Surprise broke into joy; he strode in and gripped the boy by the shoulders as though to make certain the flesh was real.

“Carthoris, my Prince!” he cried. “Kaor! Kaor! Hor Vastus greets the son of Dejah Thoris, Princess of Helium, and of her husband, John Carter. Where have you been, O my Prince? All Helium has been plunged in sorrow. Terrible have been the calamities that have befallen your great-grandsire’s mighty nation since the fatal day that saw you leave our midst.”

“Grieve not, my good Hor Vastus,” Carthoris answered, and though he spoke with a prince’s poise I heard the tremor beneath it—the same tremor a young cook gets when he carries the first dish out to an unforgiving hall. “Since I bring not back myself alone to cheer my mother’s heart and the hearts of my beloved people, but also one whom all Barsoom loved best—her greatest warrior and her saviour—John Carter, Prince of Helium!”

Hor Vastus turned to me, and for an instant the wind seemed to pause with him. His face lost its color, as though he had bitten into something too bitter to swallow.

“John Carter!” he breathed. Then trouble shadowed his eyes. “My Prince,” he began, “where hast thou—” He stopped, the question dying behind his teeth. He would not be the one to force the confession from me, not here, not in front of men whose ears were already tuned to the old doctrine of Dor as surely as a temple chorus is trained to certain notes.

“Ah, my Prince,” he continued, as if no fracture had opened in his greeting, “that you are back is sufficient, and let Hor Vastus’ sword have the high honour of being first at thy feet.”

He unbuckled his scabbard and cast his blade upon the ground before me.

There are oaths that require grand words, and there are oaths that need only the weight of metal on stone. In that clean clink of steel I heard every vow Helium makes to its own memory: the allegiance not to a crown alone, but to a way of being, to names spoken in old dialects that carry meanings no conqueror’s tongue can fully steal. It was surrender and trust together—“My sword, my life, my soul.” A single act, yet it filled the air like the aroma of a familiar hearth bread after years of exile.

I stooped, lifted the sword, raised the hilt to my lips, and stepped close enough that Hor Vastus could see the truth in my eyes if he wished.

“Hor Vastus,” I said, my hand upon his shoulder, “you know best the promptings of your own heart. That I shall need your sword I have little doubt; but accept from John Carter upon his sacred honour the assurance that he will never call upon you to draw this sword other than in the cause of truth, justice, and righteousness.”

“That I knew, my Prince,” he replied, “ere ever I threw my beloved blade at thy feet.”

While we spoke, other craft came and went between ground and battleship like bees between hive and flowering scarlet fields. Presently a larger boat dropped from above—room for a dozen, perhaps—and settled near us with scarcely more sound than a lid set gently upon a pot so as not to lose the steam. An officer sprang down and saluted Hor Vastus.

“Kantos Kan desires that this party whom we have rescued be brought immediately to the deck of the Xavarian,” he said.

As we moved toward the boat, I looked about for those who had stood with us in the dust and danger, and for the first time my eyes understood what my mind had missed: Thuvia was not there. I asked; answers came too quickly and all the same—none had seen her since Carthoris had sent her thoat galloping madly toward the hills, hoping speed and distance would be her shield.

Hor Vastus’ face tightened. Without flourish he snapped orders, and a dozen air scouts sprang away in as many directions, their engines whispering through the air with the searching insistence of hounds. It could not be possible she had gone far. Yet absence is sometimes louder than any engine: it rang in my skull.

We stepped aboard. A moment later we were upon the Xavarian.

The first to greet me was Kantos Kan.

Age and office had set new lines around his eyes, but they were the same eyes that had watched me in a Warhoon dungeon, that had endured the horrors of the Great Games, that had followed me through Zodanga when I was a stranger to Barsoom and he a simple padwar. Now he commanded Helium’s terrors of the skies. Now I bore the title of Prince in the House of Tardos Mors. Yet when his hands gripped mine, I felt not rank but history—like the taste of an old family dish prepared the same way each generation, a continuity that survives the ruin of cities.

He did not ask where I had been. His silence had the careful courtesy of one who knows a man cannot be forced to taste grief before he is ready, and that some bitterness, once on the tongue, will not leave.

He greeted Carthoris and Tars Tarkas with open delight; he could scarcely keep his hands off my son.

“You do not know, John Carter,” he said to me, “how we of Helium love this son of yours. It is as though all the great love we bore his noble father and his poor mother had been centred in him. When it became known that he was lost, ten million people wept.”

“What mean you,” I whispered, my throat suddenly dry, “by ‘his poor mother’?”

Kantos Kan drew me aside, away from the ears of sailors and the softer, treacherous ears of superstition. The ship around us murmured with contained power; below, the city’s distant hush pressed up like a held breath.

“For a year,” he said, “ever since Carthoris disappeared, Dejah Thoris has grieved and mourned for her lost boy. The blow of years ago, when you did not return from the atmosphere plant, was lessened to some extent by the duties of motherhood, for your son broke his white shell that very night.

“That she suffered terribly then, all Helium knew, for did not all Helium suffer with her the loss of her lord! But with the boy gone there was nothing left, and after expedition upon expedition returned with the same hopeless tale of no clue as to his whereabouts, our beloved Princess drooped lower and lower, until all who saw her felt that it could be but a matter of days ere she went to join her loved ones within the precincts of the Valley Dor.

“As a last resort, Mors Kajak, her father, and Tardos Mors, her grandfather, took command of two mighty expeditions, and a month ago sailed away to explore every inch of ground in the northern hemisphere of Barsoom. For two weeks no word has come back from them, but rumours were rife that they had met with a terrible disaster and that all were dead.

“About this time Zat Arras renewed his importunities for her hand in marriage. He has been for ever after her since you disappeared. She hated him and feared him, but with both her father and grandfather gone, Zat Arras was very powerful, for he is still Jed of Zodanga, to which position, you will remember, Tardos Mors appointed him after you had refused the honour.

“He had a secret audience with her six days ago. What took place none knows, but the next day Dejah Thoris had disappeared, and with her had gone a dozen of her household guard and body servants, including Sola the green woman—Tars Tarkas’ daughter, you recall. No word left they of their intentions, but it is always thus with those who go upon the voluntary pilgrimage from which none returns. We cannot think aught than that Dejah Thoris has sought the icy bosom of Iss, and that her devoted servants have chosen to accompany her.

“Zat Arras was at Helium when she disappeared. He commands this fleet which has been searching for her since. No trace of her have we found, and I fear that it be a futile quest.”

His words fell one upon another like stones into a pot already boiling over. I tasted metal at the back of my mouth. The names—Iss, Dor—were not merely places; they were the old lexicon’s weapons, terms loaded with centuries of fear and obedience. To speak them was to invoke a doctrine that had eaten the language of truth until men could no longer tell hunger from holiness.

While I stood there, the scouts Hor Vastus had dispatched returned to the Xavarian, one by one. Not one brought word of Thuvia. Each arrival was a brief surge of engine-song, each report another silence.

The weight of Dejah Thoris’ disappearance pressed me toward the deck. And beside it, the new, sharp worry for Thuvia—whom I believed the daughter of some proud Barsoomian house, and whom I had meant to return with honor to her people—added itself like a second blade laid across the first.

I was about to ask Kantos Kan to continue the search when a flier from the flagship arrived, bearing an officer with a message from Arras. Kantos Kan read the dispatch. His jaw tightened.

“Zat Arras commands me to bring our ‘prisoners’ before him,” he said. “There is naught else to do. He is supreme in Helium, yet it would be far more in keeping with chivalry and good taste were he to come hither and greet the saviour of Barsoom with the honours that are his due.”

“You know full well,” I said, and I heard my own voice find a thin edge of irony as if it were something I could chew to keep from choking, “that Zat Arras has good cause to hate me. Nothing would please him better than to humiliate me and then to kill me. Now that he has so excellent an excuse, let us go and see if he has the courage to take advantage of it.”

Summoning Carthoris, Tars Tarkas, and Xodar, we entered the small flier with Kantos Kan and Arras’ officer. The craft lifted; the sound of its engines, sharp and insistent compared to the battleship’s disciplined hum, set my teeth on edge. We crossed to the flagship and stepped upon its deck.

Zat Arras awaited us.

No greeting softened his face. Not even to Carthoris did he give a kind word. He stood cold and haughty, his silence a calculated noise—like a gong struck once, meant to command a hall.

“Kaor, Zat Arras,” I said.

He did not respond. His gaze slid over us as if measuring not men but liabilities.

“Why were these prisoners not disarmed?” he demanded of Kantos Kan.

“They are not prisoners, Zat Arras,” Kantos Kan replied, and there was steel in him now beneath the courtesy. “Two of them are of Helium’s noblest family. Tars Tarkas, Jeddak of Thark, is Tardos Mors’ best beloved ally. The other is a friend and companion of the Prince of Helium—that is enough for me to know.”

“It is not enough for me, however,” Zat Arras snapped. “More must I hear from those who have taken the pilgrimage than their names. Where have you been, John Carter?”

I did not lower my eyes. “I have just come from the Valley Dor and the Land of the First Born, Zat Arras,” I said.

“Ah!” He allowed pleasure to show itself, quick as a knife-flash. “You do not deny it, then? You have returned from the bosom of Iss?”

“I have come back from a land of false hope,” I answered, “from a valley of torture and death; with my companions I have escaped from the hideous clutches of lying fiends. I have come back to the Barsoom that I saved from a painless death to again save her, but this time from death in its most frightful form.”

“Cease, blasphemer!” Zat Arras cried. “Hope not to save thy cowardly carcass by inventing horrid lies to—”

He did not finish.

Some words are thrown as easily as scraps to a hungry animal. “Coward.” “Liar.” He spoke them as though the old creed gave him license, as though the dominant lexicon could rewrite a man’s life by naming it.

Before a hand could rise to stop me, I was at his side. My fingers closed on his throat.

“Come I from heaven or hell, Zat Arras,” I said, and my voice was very quiet, the way a man speaks when he has put all unnecessary sound away, “you will find me still the same John Carter that I have always been; nor did ever man call me such names and live—without apologizing.”

I bent him back across my knee and tightened my grip.

“Seize him!” he choked out.

A dozen officers sprang forward. The air around us filled with the scuffle of sandals on deck plates, the strained squeak of leather, the sudden raggedness of men breathing too fast—noise that tore at the order of the ship like a careless hand tearing cloth.

Kantos Kan came close, his lips to my ear. “Desist,” he whispered. “I beg of you. It will but involve us all, for I cannot see these men lay hands upon you without aiding you. My officers and men will join me and we shall have a mutiny then that may lead to revolution. For the sake of Tardos Mors and Helium, desist.”

For Helium. For the fragile continuity that held a nation together when its old words were being used as chains. I released Zat Arras.

He staggered, white and trembling amid his officers, his dignity hanging by a thread he pretended was iron. I turned my back upon him and walked toward the ship’s rail.

“Come, Kantos Kan,” I said. “The Prince of Helium would return to the Xavarian.”

No one interfered. Some faces watched Zat Arras with scorn; some shifted toward me as though pulled by an older allegiance than fear. One man—long in the service and confidence of Tardos Mors—spoke in a low voice as I passed.

“You may count my metal among your fighting-men, John Carter,” he said.

I thanked him and went on.

In silence we embarked, and shortly after stepped once more upon the deck of the Xavarian. Fifteen minutes later orders came from the flagship to proceed toward Helium.

The journey was uneventful in event, but not in mind. Carthoris and I moved through the hours as men move through a room where a pot has boiled dry: everything smells faintly scorched, and the smallest sound seems like accusation. Kantos Kan grew sombre, considering what further calamity Zat Arras might invite by clinging to the age-old precedent that demanded a terrible death for fugitives from the Valley Dor. Tars Tarkas grieved for Sola, his daughter, and the grief sat on him like a cloak too heavy for the season. Xodar alone wore a kind of lightness—a fugitive and outlaw, he could be no worse off in Helium than elsewhere.

“Let us hope that we may at least go out with good red blood upon our blades,” he said, as though discussing the only honest seasoning left in a tasteless world.

Among the officers of the Xavarian I felt division begin to form, quiet but unmistakable—like two dialects emerging in the same household, one spoken openly at the table, the other whispered in back rooms. There were those who gathered about Carthoris and myself whenever they could, their loyalty a warmth they tried to hide beneath formality; and about an equal number held aloof, courteous yet distant, their eyes carrying the old fear of Dor and Iss and Korus. I could not blame them. A creed, however ridiculous to the outsider, can root itself in a people’s daily habits the way a staple grain roots itself in soil; it becomes sustenance and identity, and to doubt it is to threaten starvation of the spirit.

By returning from Dor we had committed sacrilege. By speaking of what we had seen we had, in their minds, tried to murder the religion of their fathers. We were blasphemers—lying heretics.

Even those who still clung to us out of love and loyalty did so, I think, with an ache behind their eyes—the ache of being asked to translate experience that will not translate, to accept a truth that has no words in the language they were given. It is hard enough to exchange one faith for another; harder still to be told the old is false and offered nothing familiar in its place.

Kantos Kan would not listen to my account.

“It is enough,” he said, “that I jeopardize my life here and hereafter by countenancing you at all—do not ask me to add still further to my sins by listening to what I have always been taught was the rankest heresy.”

Sooner or later, I knew, friends and enemies would be forced to declare themselves openly. In Helium there must be an accounting. If Tardos Mors had not returned, Zat Arras’ enmity might weigh heavy against us, for he represented the government; to oppose him was treason. The majority of troops would follow their officers, and I knew many powerful men of the land and air forces would cleave to John Carter in the face of god, man, or devil. Yet the majority of the populace, steeped in the old words, might demand our blood to cleanse the sacrilege.

The outlook was dark from whatever angle I turned it. But my mind was torn open by the thought of Dejah Thoris, and in that pain all other calculations tasted thin. Day and night I saw a nightmare of what she might even then be enduring—plant men, white apes, the machinery of terror disguised as sanctity. At times I covered my face with my hands as though I could shut out images by closing a door.

It was in the forenoon that we arrived above the mile-high scarlet tower that marks greater Helium from her twin city. As we descended in great circles toward the navy docks, a mighty multitude surged in the streets beneath. Helium had been notified by radio-aerogram of our approach, and the city’s sound rose to meet us—not a roar, but a broad, breathing murmur like wind moving through a dry harvest. From the deck of the Xavarian we four—Carthoris, Tars Tarkas, Xodar, and I—were transferred to a lesser flier for transport to quarters within the Temple of Reward.

It is there that Martian justice is meted to benefactor and malefactor alike. There the hero is decorated. There the felon is condemned. The place itself is a kind of public memory, a stone repository of praise and punishment, deciding what names will be sung in the old tongue and what names will be spat out as warning.

We were taken in from the landing stage upon the roof, so we did not pass among the people at all, as is customary. Always before, prisoners of note or wanderers returned from distant ventures had been paraded from the Gate of Jeddaks to the Temple of Reward up the broad Avenue of Ancestors through dense crowds—jeering or cheering, blessing or cursing. Now we were kept from them.

Zat Arras dared not trust the people near to us. He feared their love for Carthoris and myself might flare into a demonstration strong enough to burn away, even for a moment, their superstitious horror at the charge he meant to lay upon us. Only his most trusted servitors accompanied us to the temple.

We were lodged in a room on the south side overlooking the Avenue of Ancestors. From there we could see the full length down to the Gate of Jeddaks, five miles away. The temple plaza and the streets for a mile were packed so tight with bodies that the city looked paved with living color.

And yet there was no disorder. Neither scoffs nor plaudits rose—only a vast, contained quiet, broken here and there by sobbing. When they saw us at the window above them, many buried their faces in their arms and wept. That sound—human, untrained, uncommercialized—cut deeper than any shouted hatred could have. It was the sound of a people whose old words had left them only one way to mourn.

Late in the afternoon a messenger arrived from Zat Arras. He informed us that we would be tried by an impartial body of nobles in the great hall of the temple at the first zode on the following day.

The next morning, a little before the hour they had chosen for us, the corridor outside our quarters filled with the metallic hush of men under orders. Zat Arras’ officers arrived in a tight file, their harness fittings clicking like insects against stone. They had oiled their swords; the sharp, green scent of the lubricant cut through the colder smell of the temple—polished ersite and old incense that had soaked the walls for centuries. It was a place built to make a man feel small, and even the air seemed trained to obey.

They marched us in pairs.

Down the broad passage they call the Aisle of Hope we went, with guards before and behind, and three solid ranks of Zodangan soldiers lining either side—helmets forward, eyes fixed, hands placed at identical distances along spear-shafts. The sound of our steps was swallowed by the hall’s great dome and returned thinly, as if the building itself rationed out every noise. Ten thousand people sat in that circular coliseum, packed from the lowest benches to the highest tiers; yet as we entered, the low hum of their talk died away until there was nothing but the faint rasp of leather and the soft ring of metal.

I had faced armies; I had faced beasts; but there is a particular cruelty in a silence made by many throats at once.

The platform at the center—the Throne of Righteousness—rose like an island. Around it, seated in a circle, were the thirty-one judges, nobles chosen “by lot,” as custom said when nobles were tried. I looked for a single familiar face, some old comrade of Helium, some man who remembered what I had done for their city.

I found none.

They were Zodangans, nearly to a man.
