Zodanga had not forgotten who broke her pride in the days of green hordes and red fire—who watched her broad avenues run with panic, who saw her towers blackened, her standards torn down. And with Zat Arras set above them in his golden chair, Helium’s justice tasted already of another man’s hand: bitter, foreign, and ground fine.

They seated us with our backs toward a smaller dais at the exact center of the larger platform, so that we faced both judges and crowd. A place where a man could feel the weight of every eye, the bite of every whispered word, even when no one dared whisper.

Zat Arras rose. His harness was splendid, his face handsome in the cold, precise way of those born to command. He called my name, and it struck the hall like a knife-tip on crystal.

“John Carter,” he cried, “take your place upon the Pedestal of Truth to be judged impartially according to your acts and here to know the reward you have earned thereby.”

Impartially. The word rang false in that Zodangan circle.

I stepped onto the Pedestal of Truth. It was smooth under my boots, worn by centuries of condemned men, and it felt as if it held a faint chill that belonged to every sentence ever spoken there.

Zat Arras turned, performing for the coliseum, and began to recite my “acts”—each phrase set in the ritual cadence the priests loved, the cadence that made men stop listening to meaning and begin listening to authority.

“Know you, O judges and people of Helium,” he proclaimed, “that John Carter, one time Prince of Helium, has returned by his own statement from the Valley Dor and even from the Temple of Issus itself. That, in the presence of many men of Helium he has blasphemed against the Sacred Iss, and against the Valley Dor, and the Lost Sea of Korus, and the Holy Therns themselves, and even against Issus, Goddess of Death, and of Life Eternal.

“And know you further by witness of thine own eyes that see him here now upon the Pedestal of Truth that he has indeed returned from these sacred precincts in the face of our ancient customs, and in violation of the sanctity of our ancient religion.

“He who be once dead may not live again. He who attempts it must be made dead for ever. Judges, your duty lies plain before you—here can be no testimony in contravention of truth. What reward shall be meted to John Carter in accordance with the acts he has committed?”

A judge sprang up as if pulled by a string.

“Death!” he shouted.

The word dropped into the hall and sat there, heavy as a stone.

Then, from the benches, a man rose and flung his hand high, the gesture sharp enough to cut through silence.

“Justice! Justice! Justice!”

Kantos Kan.

Before the Zodangan ranks could remember they were meant to be a wall, he was over them—moving with the clean, economical speed of a Helium naval man who has spent his life amid rigging and narrow decks. He landed on the platform, his eyes bright with anger that had been disciplined into precision.

“What manner of justice be this?” he demanded of Zat Arras. “The defendant has not been heard, nor has he had an opportunity to call others in his behalf. In the name of the people of Helium I demand fair and impartial treatment for the Prince of Helium.”

For a heartbeat the hall held its breath. Then the audience answered him like surf answering a wind.

“Justice! Justice! Justice!”

The sound changed the air. It was no longer a temple hush; it was a living thing, rough-throated, stubborn. Zat Arras’ nostrils flared. He dared not refuse the city when it spoke in one voice, yet his consent came like a snarl.

“Speak, then,” he said, turning to me, “but blaspheme not against the things that are sacred upon Barsoom.”

I did not look at the judges when I began. I looked past them, over them, to the faces packed in their tiers—faces of men and women, of elders with dry, weathered skin and youths whose mouths had not yet learned to hide their thoughts. Helium’s people had always been a city of ceremony, but also a city that could remember a taste, a scent, a song. I needed them to remember.

“Men of Helium,” I cried, “how can John Carter expect justice from the men of Zodanga? He cannot nor does he ask it. It is to the men of Helium that he states his case; nor does he appeal for mercy to any.

“It is not in his own cause that he speaks now—it is in thine. In the cause of your wives and daughters, and of wives and daughters yet unborn.”

The words were plain, but the pictures behind them were not. Dor was a place of wet stone and pale terror, and the memory of it came with smells that would not leave me: the sour green reek of plant-men, the animal musk of the great white apes, the faint perfumed rot of Thern chambers where false sanctity tried to sweeten cruelty.

“It is to save them,” I said, forcing each sentence into the light, “from the unthinkably atrocious indignities that I have seen heaped upon the fair women of Barsoom in the place men call the Temple of Issus. It is to save them from the sucking embrace of the plant men, from the fangs of the great white apes of Dor, from the cruel lust of the Holy Therns, from all that the cold, dead Iss carries them to from homes of love and life and happiness.”

A murmur rippled through the tiers—half disbelief, half something darker. I pressed on before it could turn into the easy dismissal of an old myth.

“Sits there no man here who does not know the history of John Carter. How he came among you from another world and rose from a prisoner among the green men, through torture and persecution, to a place high among the highest of Barsoom. Nor ever did you know John Carter to lie in his own behalf, or to say aught that might harm the people of Barsoom, or to speak lightly of the strange religion which he respected without understanding.”

I could feel Zat Arras’ stare like heat between my shoulder blades. I ignored it.

“There be no man here, or elsewhere upon Barsoom to-day who does not owe his life directly to a single act of mine, in which I sacrificed myself and the happiness of my Princess that you might live.”

The word Princess struck me. It always did. Dejah Thoris was not a title to me; she was color and warmth, the scent of her hair after bathing, the bright snap of her laughter in this very palace. Her absence made the air taste like metal.

“And so, men of Helium,” I continued, “I think that I have the right to demand that I be heard, that I be believed, and that you let me serve you and save you from the false hereafter of Dor and Issus as I saved you from the real death that other day.

“It is to you of Helium that I speak now. When I am done let the men of Zodanga have their will with me. Zat Arras has taken my sword from me, so the men of Zodanga no longer fear me. Will you listen?”

A great noble in the audience stood.

“Speak, John Carter, Prince of Helium,” he shouted.

The answer rolled through the hall until the dome seemed to shake with it. Even the Zodangan soldiers shifted uneasily, hearing how sound itself could become a weapon when a city agreed on a word.

So I spoke.

For two hours I told Helium what I had seen—told it with the plainness of a man describing a burned meal to a hungry household: what it looked like, what it smelled like, what it did to those who tried to swallow it. I spoke of the lies that had been handed down like heirlooms, polished bright through repetition until no one remembered the crude hands that made them. I spoke of how words could be used like chains—how “sacred” could become a gag, how “blasphemy” could become a knife, how a people could be trained to distrust their own senses because some priest had taught them a certain sound meant “truth.”

When I finished, the hall was not silent. It was full of small sounds—the scrape of a foot, the faint clink of jewelry, the quick breathing of men who had been forced to imagine their own daughters in the dark of Dor.

Zat Arras rose at once, not allowing the moment to ripen into rebellion.

“My nobles,” he said, turning to the judges, his voice lowered as if he spoke only to them, “you have heard John Carter’s plea; every opportunity has been given him to prove his innocence if he be not guilty; but instead he has but utilized the time in further blasphemy. What, gentlemen, is your verdict?”

One sprang up again, eager as a hound.

“Death to the blasphemer!” he cried.

In an instant all thirty-one were on their feet with upraised swords—the old Barsoomian sign of unanimous judgment. Metal flashed in a ring around me like a crown forged for execution.

The people heard that, if they heard nothing else. A sullen sound rose from the benches—a growl gathering depth.

Kantos Kan, who had not left my side, lifted his hand. His calm was more frightening than rage; it said he had already decided what kind of man he would be remembered as.

“You have heard the fate that the men of Zodanga would mete to Helium’s noblest hero,” he said evenly. “It may be the duty of the men of Helium to accept the verdict as final. Let each man act according to his own heart. Here is the answer of Kantos Kan, head of the navy of Helium, to Zat Arras and his judges.”

He unbuckled his scabbard and flung his sword at my feet.

It struck the stone and rang.

The sound was contagious. Men surged past the Zodangan lines—citizens, officers, nobles—and pressed onto the platform. Blades clattered down around me, a shower of steel, each one a choice made in public. A hundred swords lay at my feet like offerings on an altar.

Zat Arras’ face tightened with fury, but he was helpless; the moment had slipped from his hands into the hands of the crowd.

One by one, I lifted the swords, pressed each hilt briefly to my lips in the old salute, and buckled it back on its owner. It was not my place to take their weapons; it was my place to honor what they risked by laying them down.

“Come,” said Kantos Kan. “We will escort John Carter and his party to his own palace.”

They formed around us and began toward the stairway that led down to the Aisle of Hope.

“Stop!” Zat Arras shouted, his voice cracking sharp across stone. “Soldiers of Helium, let no prisoner leave the Throne of Righteousness.”

But the only organized body of Heliumetic troops inside the temple was Zodangan. He thought that would be enough.

He did not understand what had changed in the air.

As the Zodangan soldiers advanced, swords flashed in every quarter of the coliseum. Men poured into the aisles, not in ordered ranks but in the terrible, organic motion of a people deciding they would no longer be managed like livestock. Somewhere a voice cried out, high and wild:

“Tardos Mors is dead—a thousand years to John Carter, Jeddak of Helium!”

That cry was a spark above a powder magazine.

I saw it instantly: one thrust, one scream, one fall, and Helium would split—brother against brother, father against son—until no palace feast or hearth-song could ever stitch it back together. The temple was already too loud, the sound too raw, the echo too eager. The city’s acoustic world—the very texture of peace—was being shredded into war.

I sprang back onto the Pedestal of Truth.

“Hold!” I shouted. My voice cut through the tumult, not because it was loudest, but because they were listening for a reason not to kill. “Let no man move till I am done. A single sword thrust here to-day may plunge Helium into a bitter and bloody war the results of which none can foresee. It will turn brother against brother and father against son. No man’s life is worth that sacrifice.

“Rather would I submit to the biased judgment of Zat Arras than be the cause of civil strife in Helium.”

The words tasted like ash, but I forced them down.

“Let us each give in a point to the other, and let this entire matter rest until Tardos Mors returns, or Mors Kajak, his son. If neither be back at the end of a year a second trial may be held—the thing has a precedent.”

Then I leaned toward Zat Arras and spoke low, so only he could hear what I offered him: a way out that looked like mercy.

“Unless you be a bigger fool than I take you to be, you will grasp the chance I am offering you ere it is too late. Once that multitude of swords below is drawn against your soldiery no man upon Barsoom—not even Tardos Mors himself—can avert the consequences. What say you? Speak quickly.”

He hesitated—a heartbeat where he weighed pride against survival—then lifted his voice to the angry sea beneath us.

“Stay your hands, men of Helium!” he shouted, his tone trembling with rage he could not spend. “The sentence of the court is passed, but the day of retribution has not been set. I, Zat Arras, Jed of Zodanga, appreciating the royal connections of the prisoner and his past services to Helium and Barsoom, grant a respite of one year, or until the return of Mors Kajak, or Tardos Mors to Helium. Disperse quietly to your houses. Go.”

No one moved.

They stood with that tense stillness that is louder than shouting, eyes fixed upon me as though I held the city’s next breath in my hand.

Zat Arras, scowling, murmured to an officer, “Clear the temple.”

I saw the danger at once: force would make martyrs; martyrs would make war.

I stepped to the edge of the platform and pointed toward the main entrance, bidding them go. Not ordering—asking. In Helium, the difference mattered.

As one man, they turned at my request and filed out, silent and grim, past the Zodangan soldiers who stood rigid and humiliated. The sound of their leaving—ten thousand sandals, ten thousand harness rings, ten thousand controlled exhalations—was like a storm pulling away into distance, promising it could return.

Kantos Kan and the hundred who had cast their swords still stood with us.

“Come,” he said. “We will escort you to your palace, my Prince. Come, Carthoris and Xodar. Come, Tars Tarkas.”

He gave Zat Arras a haughty sneer and strode down the throne steps into the Aisle of Hope. We followed—Carthoris beside me, Xodar, Tars Tarkas towering over all—and behind us the loyal hundred, a moving knot of Helium’s own will.

No hand rose to stop us. Only eyes followed—glowering, calculating.

Outside, in the avenues, the city pressed close. People opened a path as we passed, and again and again swords were flung down at my feet—not as threats now, but as pledges. The metal struck the paving with a sound like dropped utensils in a kitchen—abrupt, honest, impossible to ignore. Each clatter said, I remember. Each ring said, I choose.

At my palace on the outskirts of Helium, my old slaves ran to meet us. They fell to their knees, kissed my hands, their faces shining with a love so simple it made my throat ache. They did not ask where I had been. In their world, return was its own miracle, and a miracle needed no explanation.

“Ah, master,” cried one, his voice breaking, “if our divine Princess were but here this would be a day indeed.”

The words struck like a blow. Tears came hot, unbidden. I turned away so they would not see them, but Carthoris did not hide his grief; he wept openly as the slaves crowded him too, touching his hands, murmuring affection, speaking sorrow for the loss that sat in our midst like an empty place at the table.

It was then—standing amid familiar walls, with familiar voices around him—that Tars Tarkas learned for the first time that his daughter, Sola, had gone with Dejah Thoris upon the last long pilgrimage.

I had not told him. I could not. Kantos Kan had told me, and I had carried that knowledge like a stone in my mouth.

The great Thark showed no sign. His face remained the same hard-carved mask of green Martian stoicism. Yet I knew him; beneath that mask he held love and friendship in a way rare among his kind, as if some older, gentler blood remembered how to ache. His grief was there, silent, as deep as a buried spring.

We gathered for the feast of welcome in the great dining hall—over a hundred of us, not counting the members of my little court, for Dejah Thoris and I had maintained a household suited to our rank. The hall smelled as it always had when the palace prepared to honor guests: warmed fruits from the thin gardens under the domes, roasted viands whose spiced vapors rose and clung to tapestries, and the clean bite of fermented liquors decanted into long-necked crystal. The servants moved with practiced grace, their sandals whispering across the floor in a rhythm that once had always been answered by Dejah Thoris’ laughter.

The board was triangular, as red Martian custom dictated—three sides for the three of our family. Carthoris and I took our places at the centers of our sides.

Midway along the third side stood Dejah Thoris’ chair: high-backed, carved, empty.

They had draped it in her gorgeous wedding trappings and jewels, as though cloth and metal might persuade absence to become presence. Behind it stood a slave in readiness, just as in the old days when her mistress sat there and could lift a hand, tilt her head, speak a command in the soft dialect of her childhood that no outsider could ever perfectly imitate.

It was the way upon Barsoom. And so I endured it—the ritual that kept memory alive even while it tore at the heart.

At my right sat Kantos Kan. To the right of Dejah Thoris’ empty place sat Tars Tarkas in the huge chair before the raised section I had had built years ago for his massive bulk. The place of honor at a Martian board is always at the hostess’s right, and Dejah Thoris had always reserved it for the great Thark when he was in Helium. On Carthoris’ side Hor Vastus sat in the seat of honor.

There was little conversation. Even the clink of service seemed subdued, as if the palace itself listened for a voice that did not come. Food was set before us—beautifully prepared, fragrant, sustaining—yet each aroma carried a sting: what is a feast without the one who made it a home?

We spoke of Tardos Mors and Mors Kajak in broken phrases, fear lurking under every careful word. If Helium had truly lost her Jeddak, what would she become in the hands of men like Zat Arras? A city could forget a truth in a generation if the wrong tongues were given power to name things. A people could be made to swallow any story if it was served with ceremony often enough.

Then, above the quiet of our hall, a sound rose—distant shouting, the blended voices of many people. At first we could not tell if it was anger or rejoicing. The tumult drew nearer, swelling, filling the avenues outside like wind funneling toward a door.

A slave rushed in, panting, to cry that a great concourse was swarming through the palace gates.

Another burst in on his heels, laughing and shrieking by turns like a madman overcome.

“Dejah Thoris is found!” he screamed. “A messenger from Dejah Thoris!”

I waited to hear no more.

The great windows of the dining hall overlooked the avenue leading to the main gates, but they were across the room from me with the triangular table between. I did not go around. I cleared it.

With a single leap I sprang over board and diners and landed on the balcony beyond. Thirty feet below lay the scarlet sward of the lawn. Beyond, the crowd clustered around a great thoat, its bulk shifting, its harness creaking. A rider on its back urged it forward toward the palace.

I vaulted down to the lawn and ran—fast, reckless, hearing only the pounding of my own blood and the roaring of the crowd’s voices, as if all Helium had become a single throat.

As I drew near, the rider’s outline sharpened. Green skin. Familiar posture. The long, sure seat of one who had ridden with the hordes.

Sola.

She slid from the thoat and ran to me, and for an instant I saw in her face not triumph but grief.

“O my Prince! My Prince!” she cried.

Hope faltered inside me like a candle struck by wind.

“Where is the Princess of Helium?” I demanded.

Sola’s voice broke, and the words came out like something torn from her.

“She is gone for ever. Even now she may be a captive upon the lesser moon. The black pirates of Barsoom have stolen her.”

Once inside the palace I drew Sola straight to the dining hall, because there was comfort in that long room’s familiar noises: the soft clink of service blades laid beside platters, the hush of sandals on polished stone, the low, domestic thunder of a hundred throats swallowing warm broths. The air carried the clean bite of carved fruit and the faint tang of fermented juices—small, ordinary scents that tried, bravely, to stand between us and catastrophe.

Sola greeted her father with the grave, ritual economy of her people. Then she began, and though she spoke of gods and black ships and the Valley Dor, she spoke as one might recite the steps of a bread one must bake to keep a household alive—measured, exact, unwilling to waste a syllable. Her voice had that husky Barsoomian cadence I had come to know, a dialect that held meanings no Heliumetic court phrase could quite translate; each time she named Iss or Dor, the word itself seemed to scrape the room’s acoustics, as though the name carried its own echo.

“Seven days ago,” she said, “after her audience with Zat Arras, Dejah Thoris tried to slip from the palace in the dead of night. I had not been told what passed between them, but I saw what it did to her—like a knife turned inside the mind. When I found her moving like a shadow through the sleeping corridors, I did not need her to name her destination.

“I woke a dozen of her most faithful guards. I told them my fear. They did not argue, not one. They took up their harness and their weapons as quickly and quietly as cooks take up their knives when the fire is already hot and there is no time left for indecision. We swore to follow our Princess wherever she went—even to the Sacred Iss and the Valley Dor.

“We overtook her not far beyond the palace. Woola was with her, and no one else. She tried to drive us back, feigning anger, ordering us home as though we were children under a stern elder’s hand. But for once we disobeyed her. When she saw we would not let her walk the last long pilgrimage alone, she broke—she wept, and then she embraced us. Together we went south into the night.

“The next day we came upon a herd of small thoats. From that hour we were mounted, and the miles fell behind us. We rode hard—so hard the wind rasped in our throats and the world became a thin, whistling line of sand and scrub and bitter, dusty air. We travelled very fast and very far. On the morning of the fifth day we sighted a great fleet of battleships sailing north.”

At that, the dining hall seemed to tighten. Even the little sounds—spoons against bowls, the quiet breathing of the guards at the doors—felt suddenly too loud, as though noise itself might summon dark sails to the windows.

“They saw us before we could find shelter,” Sola went on. “Soon we were ringed by a horde of black men. The Princess’s guard fought to the end. They died as they had lived—close to her. Only Dejah Thoris and I were spared.

“When Dejah Thoris knew she was in the hands of the black pirates, she tried to take her own life. One of them tore her dagger away. Then they bound us so tightly we could not even curl our fingers. They did it with practiced speed, the way a butcher ties a struggling carcass—not for mercy, but for convenience.

“The fleet continued north. There were about twenty large battleships, and many smaller, swift cruisers. That evening one of the cruisers returned from far ahead with another prisoner—a young red woman they had found in the hills. They boasted they had taken her under the very noses of three red battleships.

“We heard scraps of their talk. They were searching for fugitives who had escaped them days before. The commander spoke with the new captive for a long time—long and earnest. When he finished, they bound her and threw her into our compartment.”

Sola’s eyes, when she remembered it, went distant, as though she still smelled that compartment’s stale breath—the sourness of fear sealed up in metal, the rank musk of strangers, the bitter oil of machinery that never quite loses its metallic sting.

“She was very beautiful,” Sola said. “She told Dejah Thoris she had taken the voluntary pilgrimage many years ago from the court of her father, the Jeddak of Ptarth. She was Thuvia—Princess of Ptarth.

“Then she asked Dejah Thoris who she was. When she learned, she fell to her knees and kissed Dejah Thoris’ bound hands. And she said that very morning she had been with John Carter, Prince of Helium—and with Carthoris, his son.”

I felt the words like a blow and a balm at once: my name carried into that prison of air and iron by a stranger’s mouth; my son alive, standing in the same hour of sunlight as a captive Princess. How much of life is nothing but sound—names spoken, vows repeated—until we hang our survival upon them?

“Dejah Thoris could not believe her at first,” Sola continued. “But Thuvia told her everything—strange adventures since she had met you, and what you and Carthoris and Xodar had told her of your time in the Land of the First Born. Then Dejah Thoris said, ‘For who upon all Barsoom other than John Carter could have done the deeds you tell of?’”

Sola paused, and when she spoke again her voice thickened, as though the memory had its own weight.

“And when Thuvia told Dejah Thoris of her love for you—of her own heart, and of your loyalty to the Princess you had chosen—Dejah Thoris broke down. She wept. She cursed Zat Arras and the cruel fate that drove her from Helium only days before your return.

“‘I do not blame you for loving him, Thuvia,’ she said. ‘That your affection is pure I can believe, from the candour with which you confess it.’”

The hall was very still then, but it was not silence so much as listening—everyone straining to hear the next thing that would decide whether the night’s bread would rise or fall.

“The fleet continued north nearly to Helium,” Sola said. “But last night they must have learned that you had escaped them. They turned south again. Not long after, a guard came into our compartment and dragged me out onto the deck.

“‘There is no place in the Land of the First Born for a green one,’ he said. Then he shoved me—hard—over the side.”

A sharp intake moved through the room like a gust. Even the servants, who had no business in councils of war, froze with trays half-lifted, the faint rattle of utensils suspended in air.

“But fate intervened,” Sola said, and there was something grim in the way she said fate, as though it were merely another predator with its own habits. “By a miracle I lived. The ship was moving slowly, and I thought I would fall thousands of feet to my death, for all day they had sailed high above the ground. But I struck soft vegetation—no more than twenty feet below the deck. The keel must have been grazing the surface.

“I lay there all night. When the sun rose I understood: I was on the highest peak of a lofty range. In the darkness the fleet had barely skimmed the crest of the hills, and in that brief moment the guard threw me overboard, thinking to rid the ship of me and kill me in one act.”

She lifted her chin, proud in spite of bruises and exhaustion.

“A few miles west I saw a great waterway. When I reached it I learned it belonged to Helium. There I procured a thoat. The rest you know.”

For many minutes no one spoke. The ordinary sounds of the dining hall—the tame, human noises that make a palace feel like a home—had gone thin, as though the room itself were holding its breath. Dejah Thoris in the clutches of the First Born. The thought made my skin crawl, as if the word Issus were a foreign spice that burned rather than nourished.

Then the old fire rose in me, sudden and absolute. I sprang to my feet. The chair scraped the floor with a raw, protesting cry that seemed too loud in that hushed hall.

With back-thrown shoulders and sword upraised I took a solemn vow: I would reach her. I would rescue her. I would have revenge.

A hundred swords sang from a hundred scabbards—bright, clean sounds that cut through the room’s heavy air. A hundred fighting-men leaped up, some even to the table-top, pledging lives and fortunes as though they were passing bread from hand to hand: not charity, but necessity.

My plans had already formed themselves in my mind, each part fitted to the next like well-made gear. I thanked them—each loyal friend—and left Carthoris to keep them occupied while I withdrew with Kantos Kan, Tars Tarkas, Xodar, and Hor Vastus to my audience chamber.

There, long after dark, we spoke in low voices and listened hard between phrases, because a palace has its own acoustic ecology: the whisper that travels under a door; the footfall that does not belong; the faint click of a listening device or the soft intake of breath behind a curtain. In courts, the wrong sound at the wrong moment can kill more surely than a blade.

Xodar was positive Issus would choose both Dejah Thoris and Thuvia to serve her for a year.

“For that length of time at least they will be comparatively safe,” he said, “and we will know where to look.”

We spoke of equipping a fleet to enter Omean. The details were left to Kantos Kan and Xodar. Kantos Kan would take the vessels into dock as rapidly as possible; Xodar would direct their equipment with water propellers. For years the blacks had refitted captured battleships so they might navigate Omean, and Xodar knew the construction of propellers, housings, and auxiliary gearing.

Six months, we estimated—because secrecy would be as essential as steel. Let Zat Arras scent this plan too soon, and he would poison it as one spoils a pot by slipping in the wrong bitter herb at the last moment.

Kantos Kan’s face was hard as he spoke of Zat Arras. “The man’s ambitions are fully aroused,” he said. “Nothing short of the title of Jeddak of Helium will satisfy him. I doubt he would even welcome Dejah Thoris’ return, for it would place another nearer the throne than he. With you and Carthoris out of the way, there would be little to prevent him from assuming the title. So long as he is supreme here there is no safety for either of you.”

“There is a way,” Hor Vastus cried, “to thwart him effectually and for ever.”

“What?” I asked.

He smiled as though tasting the words before releasing them. “I shall whisper it here,” he said, “but some day I shall stand upon the dome of the Temple of Reward and shout it to cheering multitudes below.”

“What do you mean?” Kantos Kan demanded.

“John Carter, Jeddak of Helium,” Hor Vastus said softly.

Eyes brightened around me—grim, eager. The room’s very air seemed to sharpen with expectation. But I shook my head.

“No,” I said. “I thank you, but it cannot be. Not yet. When we know that Tardos Mors and Mors Kajak are gone to return no more—if I be here then—I shall join you to see that the people of Helium are permitted to choose fairly their next Jeddak. Whom they choose may count upon my loyalty. I will not seek the honour for myself. Until then Tardos Mors is Jeddak of Helium, and Zat Arras is his representative.”

“As you will, John Carter,” Hor Vastus said—and then his head snapped toward the window overlooking the gardens. His voice dropped to a hiss. “But—what was that?”

He pointed. In the same instant he sprang to the balcony.

“There he goes!” he cried. “The guards! Below there—the guards!”

We were close behind him. All of us saw it: the figure of a man running across a patch of sward, vanishing into shrubbery beyond. A fleeting silhouette, but the sound of his movement—too light, too practiced—was unmistakable.

“He was on the balcony when I first saw him,” Hor Vastus said. “Quick—after him!”

We ran to the gardens. We scoured the grounds with the entire guard for hours, but found nothing—not a footprint, not a broken stem, not a cloth snagged on thorn. Whoever had been there moved like a rumor, leaving only the unease of a disturbed night-bird’s cry.

“What do you make of it, Kantos Kan?” Tars Tarkas asked.

“A spy sent by Zat Arras,” Kantos Kan replied. “It was ever his way.”

“He will have something interesting to report, then,” Hor Vastus laughed, but the laughter had no warmth in it.

“I hope he heard only our references to a new Jeddak,” I said. “If he overheard our plans to rescue Dejah Thoris, it will mean civil war. He will try to thwart us—and in that I will not be thwarted. I would turn against Tardos Mors himself, were it necessary. If all Helium is thrown into bloody conflict, I shall still go on. Nothing shall stay me now short of death.”

I looked from face to face—men whose voices I knew, whose steps I could identify in any corridor by the rhythm of their stride.

“And should I die,” I said, “will you take oath to prosecute the search for her and bring her back in safety to her grandfather’s court?”

Each man swore upon the hilt of his sword.

We agreed the battleships to be remodelled should be ordered to Hastor, far to the southwest. Kantos Kan believed the docks there, alongside their regular work, could handle six battleships at a time. As commander-in-chief of the navy he could order the vessels there without raising suspicion, and keep the remodelled fleet in remote parts of the empire until we were ready to assemble it for the dash upon Omean.

Our conference broke up late. Each man left with duties clear as a recipe learned by heart: Kantos Kan and Xodar to oversee the remodelling; Tars Tarkas to communicate with Thark and learn his people’s sentiment toward his return from Dor, and if favourable to go at once and assemble a great horde of green warriors to be transported directly to the Valley Dor and the Temple of Issus while our fleet entered Omean and destroyed the vessels of the First Born; Hor Vastus to organize, quietly and delicately, a secret force of fighting-men sworn to follow me wherever I might lead. Over a million men to man a thousand great battleships and transports and convoys—an undertaking as vast as any harvest, requiring patience, timing, and hands willing to work in darkness.

After they had gone I bade Carthoris good-night. I was exhausted in a way that went beyond muscle and bone, like a man who has listened too long for the wrong sound outside his door.

In my own apartments I bathed and lay down upon sleeping silks and furs, hoping at last for the first full night’s sleep since my return to Barsoom. The fabrics held the faint scent of clean oils and the warm animal musk of the furs—small, intimate assurances of home.

Even that was denied me.

How long I slept I cannot say. I woke suddenly to the crushing weight of bodies. A gag was already in my mouth. Before my mind fully cleared, my arms and legs were bound so securely that struggle was a mockery. They worked with the swift efficiency of men who have rehearsed the task until it is nothing but muscle memory.

Not one of them spoke. The gag made speech impossible. They lifted me and bore me toward the door.

As they passed the window, the farther moon spilled brilliant beams across them, and I saw their faces were swathed in layers of silk. No features—no recognition—only eyes that avoided my own.

In the corridor they turned toward a secret panel in the wall—the one that led to the passage terminating in the pits beneath the palace. That any outside my household knew of it had seemed unlikely; yet the leader did not hesitate. He stepped directly to the concealed button. The panel swung open. His companions carried me through. He closed it behind him and followed.

Down we went through passageways that swallowed sound, the air growing cooler and closer with each turn, until the world was nothing but the scrape of sandals, the faint creak of leather, the muffled beat of my own heart trapped behind the gag.

At last we stopped. The leader rapped upon the wall with the hilt of his sword—three quick, sharp blows, a pause, then three more, another pause, then two. A code, crisp as a kitchen signal between cooks when words would carry too far.

A second later the wall swung inward.

I was pushed into a brilliantly lighted chamber. Three richly trapped men sat within. One turned toward me with a sardonic smile upon thin, cruel lips.

Zat Arras.

“Ah,” purred Zat Arras, “to what kindly circumstance am I indebted for the pleasure of this unexpected visit from the Prince of Helium?”

A guard’s fingers yanked the gag away. My jaw ached where the straps had bitten. I gave the Jed of Zodanga nothing in return—not even the courtesy of breath. I merely stood, tasting the stale air of his chamber—hot metal, oiled leather, the faint peppery tang of radium lamps—and held his gaze as one might hold a knife over a board: steady, unmoved.

The others in the room watched our silence thicken. It was like a kitchen gone wrong, when a pot boils over and everyone hears it, yet no one dares reach for it. Color climbed Zat Arras’ cheeks, slow and poisonous.

“You may go,” he snapped at those who had brought me. When they were gone and only his two companions remained, he addressed me again, each word measured as if he were shaving slivers from ice.

“John Carter,” he said, “by the edict of custom, by the law of our religion, and by the verdict of an impartial court, you are condemned to die. The people cannot save you—I alone may accomplish that. You are absolutely in my power to do with as I wish—I may kill you, or I may free you, and should I elect to kill you, none would be the wiser.

“Should you go free in Helium for a year, in accordance with the conditions of your reprieve, there is little fear that the people would ever insist upon the execution of the sentence imposed upon you.

“You may go free within two minutes, upon one condition. Tardos Mors will never return to Helium. Neither will Mors Kajak, nor Dejah Thoris. Helium must select a new Jeddak within the year. Zat Arras would be Jeddak of Helium. Say that you will espouse my cause. This is the price of your freedom. I am done.”

He offered it like a platter set down with flourish—yet the smell of it was rancid. I knew what lay under his polished phrasing: my death in darkness, Helium steered by a traitor, my Princess flung to the obscene rites of Issus. And, for a heartbeat, the arithmetic of it clawed at me. If I died, how might our friends execute the rescue? If I lived, I could search. If I refused, might I seal Dejah Thoris’ fate by my own pride?

But there are vows that cannot be seasoned into palatability. Dejah Thoris—daughter of a thousand Jeddaks—would rather die with clean hands than live fat on treason. I could do no less.

I turned my contempt upon him like a cold blade.

“There can be no alliance,” I said, “between a traitor to Helium and a prince of the House of Tardos Mors. I do not believe, Zat Arras, that the great Jeddak is dead.”

He shrugged as if my words were smoke.

“It will not be long, John Carter,” he replied, “that your opinions will be of interest even to yourself, so make the best of them while you can. Zat Arras will permit you in due time to reflect further upon the magnanimous offer he has made you. Into the silence and darkness of the pits you will enter upon your reflection this night with the knowledge that should you fail within a reasonable time to agree to the alternative which has been offered you, never shall you emerge from the darkness and the silence again. Nor shall you know at what minute the hand will reach out through the darkness and the silence with the keen dagger that shall rob you of your last chance to win again the warmth and the freedom and joyousness of the outer world.”

He clapped his hands—sharp, flat, like the slap of meat against stone. Guards returned.

“To the pits,” he said, as though ordering a dish sent away untouched.

A radium hand-light was thrust ahead of us, its glow a thin broth of greenish luminance, and we went down—ever down—through tunnels that stole the air from your lungs. Above lay Helium, with its gardens and fountains, its voices, its music of water and wind through scarlet trees. Below was only the dead acoustics of rock: our footfalls swallowed, our breath muffled, the faint clink of metal too loud because nothing else lived to counter it.

At last they halted in a chamber where the walls were studded with iron rings. Chains ran from them like black vines. At the ends of many chains were what remained of men—skeletal ankles still hugged by iron bands, ribs like broken basketry, skulls turned toward nothing. One skeleton was kicked aside with the casualness of sweeping crumbs from a floor. The lock of its ankle-chain was opened, and the same band was snapped about my own leg.

Then they took the light away.

Darkness, complete and thick, dropped on me as though poured. For a few minutes I could still hear the retreating clank of their harness and weapons, the fading rasp of sandals on stone. Then even that dissolved, until silence took over—so absolute that it became its own kind of sound, a pressure against the ears. In that silence I understood why sane men fear prisons more than blades: not for the pain, but for the way quiet can erase you, day by day, until you no longer remember the shape of your own name.

I stood a time, listening to nothing, with the bones of other men for company, and at last slid down to the hard floor. The rock was cold against my shoulder. I slept because there was nothing else to do.

When I woke, a young man stood before me. In one hand he carried a light; in the other, a receptacle of Barsoomian prison fare—a gray, gruel-like mixture with the flat odor of boiled grain and old water, the kind of sustenance that keeps flesh moving but offers no comfort, no memory, no home.

“Zat Arras sends you greetings,” he said, “and commands me to inform you that though he is fully advised of the plot to make you Jeddak of Helium, he is, however, not inclined to withdraw the offer which he has made you. To gain your freedom you have but to request me to advise Zat Arras that you accept the terms of his proposition.”

I shook my head once.

He set the food beside me, said nothing further, and took the light away as he departed—leaving me again with the soundless weight of earth above and bones around.

Twice each day he came, always with the same message, always with the same bowl of indifferent gruel. The routine took on the circular certainty of a household duty—like bread kneaded at dawn, like water hauled at dusk—except here the duty was despair, served hot and brought away cold. I tried at first to draw him into talk—of Helium, of the outside world, of anything that had texture and color—but he would not be coaxed. His silence was practiced, disciplined, like a priest’s.

Days became weeks, weeks months. In the half-light of each visit I studied him as one studies ingredients by smell and hand when sight is uncertain. He was handsome, about Carthoris’ age and size. His trappings were poor, rusted and patched, yet he carried himself with a dignity that did not match his station. There was something in his bearing—an inherited straightness—that reminded me of old agrarian elders who can walk into any market and, without raising their voice, command respect simply by the way they hold their shoulders.

All the while my mind churned on one question: what of our plans? What of Dejah Thoris? What of Carthoris and Kantos Kan and Tars Tarkas and Hor Vastus and Xodar? Were they free? Dead? In these pits as I was?

I scraped at a single link of my chain for months, patient as a cook grinding spice with a dull stone, knowing that only repetition yields powder. The metal resisted. My hands bled and healed and bled again. I measured time by pain and by the youth’s footsteps.

At last I resolved to gamble with speech.

On his next visit, when he set down the bowl, I said, “You have been very kind to me during my imprisonment here. As I feel that I have at best but a very short time to live, I wish, ere it is too late, to furnish substantial testimony of my appreciation.”

He watched me, cautious.

“Promptly you have brought my food each day, seeing that it was pure and of sufficient quantity. Never by word or deed have you attempted to take advantage of my defenceless condition to insult or torture me. You have been uniformly courteous and considerate. It is this which prompts my feeling of gratitude.”

His eyes flicked, despite himself, to my own accoutrements—still fine, though dulled by dirt and time—then down to his rusty harness.

“In the guard-room of my palace,” I went on, “are many fine trappings. Go thou there and select the harness which most pleases you—it shall be yours. All I ask is that you wear it, that I may know that my wish has been realized. Tell me that you will do it.”

Pleasure lit him, quickly banked.

“And I went to the palace of the Prince of Helium with any such demand,” he said at last, “they would laugh at me and, into the bargain, would more than likely throw me headforemost into the avenue. No, it cannot be, though I thank you for the offer. Why, if Zat Arras even dreamed that I contemplated such a thing he would have my heart cut out of me.”

“There can be no harm in it,” I urged, keeping my voice gentle. “By night you may go to my palace with a note from me to Carthoris, my son. You may read the note before you deliver it, that you may know it contains nothing harmful to Zat Arras. My son will be discreet; so none but us three need know.”

He stood in thought so long that the silence became again a third presence between us.

“And,” I added, as one adds the final fragrance that tips desire into action, “there is a jewelled short-sword which I took from the body of a northern Jeddak. When you get the harness, see that Carthoris gives you that also. With it and the harness there will be no more handsomely accoutred warrior in all Zodanga.”

He turned and left without speaking. For hours afterward I sat with my back against stone, listening to the tunnels as if they might deliver an answer on the breath of air.

When he came again, I heard his steps and the faint creak of leather. He set down my food—and beside it, writing materials.

For a moment I feigned surprise, then let comprehension dawn across my face as though it were reluctant. Inside, my heart surged so violently I feared it would betray me.

I wrote a brief order to Carthoris: deliver to Parthak a harness of his selection from the guard-room and the jewelled short-sword I described. Nothing more. But in such places, where language is rationed and watched, a few words can be a whole life—can be proof of breath, proof of loyalty, proof that the world above has not yet forgotten your name.

I laid the note open on the floor. Parthak took it and left without a word.

By my reckoning I had been in the pits three hundred days. If Dejah Thoris still lived among the First Born, her year was nearly spent. The rites of Issus were not an abstraction. I had seen too much of Barsoom’s pageantry—the way cruelty dresses itself in silk and music—to believe any mercy would intervene for her.

When footsteps came again, I strained to hear whether Parthak’s gait returned, whether fine harness might squeak, whether the small bright chime of a jewelled hilt might speak against stone.

The man who entered was not Parthak.

“What has become of Parthak?” I demanded.

He did not answer. He set down the bowl and left, soundless as a machine.

Days passed. Always the same mute jailer. Always the same thin gruel, its smell a daily insult. My hopes, which had risen like bread in warmth, collapsed again into sour heaviness. Parthak’s removal could mean only one thing: my note had been discovered, or Parthak had betrayed me—or Parthak had vanished into some deeper darkness. And if he had carried my message to Zat Arras instead of to Carthoris, then all my careful sparing of words had been merely seasoning on my own doom.

Thirty days crawled by from the time I had given him the note. Three hundred and thirty days since my chains had closed on my ankle. By my count, perhaps thirty days remained before Dejah Thoris would be ordered to the arena.

The picture rose in my mind with obscene clarity: the white apes, their huge bodies stinking of old blood and musky fur; the sand of the arena; the black nobles’ laughter; the scrape of claws on stone; and, afterward, the sacrilege of flesh made feast. Barsoom has always known hunger, and with hunger comes the darkest bargains—yet nothing in all my wanderings had prepared me for the thought of my Princess reduced to spectacle and meat.

I buried my face in my arms and fought to keep tears from burning out of me. The pits were not only dark; they were hungry for sound, eager to drink sobs as readily as silence.

Then I heard my jailer coming.

The approach of footsteps—however faint—was like a hand slapped sharply on a table: a summons back from madness. A grim clarity seized me. I would try once. One superhuman effort. I would kill the jailer by ruse, take his light, and trust blind fate to lead me through the tunnels to air and sky.

I threw myself to the floor near the wall and contorted my body as though dead in spasm. I gripped the slack of my chain in my right hand so I could swing it like a weighted club. My plan was simple: when he bent close, I would seize his throat with my left hand and strike with the chain. Quick. Final.

Nearer came the steps. He paused. I heard a muttered exclamation. Then a shift of weight as he knelt beside me.

I tightened my grip. I must open my eyes, find his throat, strike—all in one breath.

The ruse worked too well.

The instant my eyes opened I saw a face so close that it filled my world—and recognized it as that of my son, Carthoris.

The chain was already falling.

In that heartbeat between recognition and impact, my mind screamed to stop, but the body had begun what the will had ordered. Fate, malignant and precise, had set my own hand against my own blood. The blow landed—yet mercy, though late, somehow softened it. My vision blurred as if the radium light had turned to water. I felt myself pitching forward, and the world went out.

When I returned to consciousness, a cool, firm hand pressed my forehead. For a moment I kept my eyes closed, trying to gather thoughts that scattered like spilled grain. Then memory returned, sharp as a knife: the strike, the face, my son.

I did not dare look.

But at last I forced my eyes open.

Carthoris leaned above me, bruised across the forehead where the chain had struck, yet alive. No companion stood behind him—only the narrow chamber and the dead and the chains.

I reached for him and drew him into my arms with a strength that surprised even me, as though love could be a bandage pressed hard enough to stop any bleeding the universe might attempt. In that underground silence my gratitude rose like a prayer—fervent, wordless, beyond any dialect I knew.

“The brief instant in which I recognized you,” I whispered, “must have been enough to check the force. Thank the Eternal Mystery.”

He laughed softly, though pain tightened his eyes. “It was enough. I lay unconscious for a time. I do not know how long.”

“How came you here at all?” I demanded, still stunned that he had found me in this labyrinth.

“It was by your wit,” he said, “in apprising me of your existence and imprisonment through the youth, Parthak. Until he came for his harness and his sword, we had thought you dead. When I read your note, I did as you bid: I gave Parthak his choice among the harnesses in the guard-room, and later I brought him the jewelled short-sword. But the moment I had fulfilled the promise you had made him, my obligation ceased. Then I questioned him—but he would give no information. He was intensely loyal to Zat Arras.

“Finally I gave him a choice between freedom and the pits beneath the palace—the price of freedom to be full information as to where you were imprisoned and directions to reach you. Still he held to his stubborn partisanship. Despairing, I had him removed to the pits, where he still is.

“No threats of torture or death, no bribes, however fabulous, would move him. His only reply was that whenever Parthak died—were it to-morrow or a thousand years hence—no man could truly say, ‘A traitor is gone to his deserts.’

“At last Xodar—fiend for subtle craftiness—evolved a plan. I caused Hor Vastus to be harnessed in the metal of a Zodangan soldier and chained in Parthak’s cell beside him. For fifteen days Hor Vastus has languished in the darkness. But not in vain. Little by little he won Parthak’s confidence, until to-day Parthak, thinking he spoke to a countryman and a friend, revealed the exact cell where you lay.

“I found the plans of the pits among official papers. To come to you was harder. Though the pits connect beneath the city, there are single entrances between sections at the upper level. Those openings are always guarded. I reached the entrance beneath the palace Zat Arras occupies and found a Zodangan soldier on guard. I left him there when I passed—but his soul was no longer with him.

“And here I am,” he finished, “just in time to be nearly killed by you.”

As he spoke, he worked at the lock that held my fetters. The metal clicked, stubborn and then yielding. The chain fell away with a sound like a pot-lid set down too hard. I stood—stiff, sore, unsteady—yet freer than I had been in almost a year. He handed me a long-sword and a dagger; their familiar weight returned to my hands like a remembered tool.

We made our way back through the tunnels. At the point where we entered Zat Arras’ pits we found the guard Carthoris had slain. The body had not yet been discovered. To delay pursuit and confound their search, we carried it a short distance and hid it in a tiny cell off the main corridor beneath an adjoining estate—tucking it away as one hides evidence of a spoiled dish, though no concealment could change what it was.

Half an hour later we reached the pits beneath our own palace. We emerged into the audience chamber, stepping from underworld darkness into lamplight that tasted of oil and warmed stone.

Kantos Kan, Tars Tarkas, Hor Vastus, and Xodar were there, waiting with the taut impatience of men who have listened too long for a signal. The room felt suddenly alive with sound—the rustle of harness, the low creak of leather, the subtle chorus of breathing. Even that ordinary music struck me as precious; in the pits, silence had been a tyrant, here the smallest noise was proof of comradeship.

There was no time for recounting my suffering. I wanted only the state of our preparations.

“It has taken much longer than we had expected,” said Kantos Kan. “The need for utter secrecy has handicapped us terribly. Zat Arras’ spies are everywhere. Yet, to the best of my knowledge, no word of our real plans has reached the villain’s ear.

“To-night there lies about the great docks at Hastor a fleet of a thousand of the mightiest battleships that ever sailed above Barsoom—each equipped to navigate the air of Omean and the waters of Omean itself. Upon each battleship there are five ten-man cruisers, and ten five-man scouts, and a hundred one-man scouts; in all, one hundred and sixteen thousand craft fitted with both air and water propellers.

“At Thark lie the transports for the green warriors of Tars Tarkas—nine hundred large troopships, and with them their convoys. Seven days ago all was in readiness, but we waited in the hope your rescue might be accomplished in time for you to command the expedition. It is well we waited, my Prince.”

“How is it, Tars Tarkas,” I asked, “that the men of Thark take not the accustomed action against one who returns from the bosom of Iss?”

“They sent a council of fifty chieftains to talk with me here,” replied the Thark, his great voice like distant drums. “We are a just people. When I told them the entire story they agreed their action toward me would be guided by Helium’s action toward John Carter. In the meantime, at their request, I resumed my throne as Jeddak of Thark, that I might negotiate with neighboring hordes for warriors for the land forces. I have done that which I agreed.

“Two hundred and fifty thousand fighting men—from the ice cap at the north to the ice cap at the south, representing a thousand communities from a hundred wild hordes—fill the great city of Thark to-night. They are ready to sail for the Land of the First Born when I give the word and fight there until I bid them stop. All they ask is the loot they take and transportation home when the fighting and looting are over. I am done.”

“And thou, Hor Vastus,” I said, “what has been thy success?”

“A million veteran fighting-men from Helium’s thin waterways man the battleships, the transports, and the convoys,” he answered. “Each is sworn to loyalty and secrecy, nor were enough recruited from a single district to cause suspicion.”

“Good,” I said. The word tasted like clean water. “Each has done his duty. Now, Kantos Kan—may we not repair at once to Hastor and get under way before to-morrow’s sun?”

“We should lose no time, Prince,” Kantos Kan replied. “Already the people of Hastor question the purpose of so great a fleet fully manned. I wonder that word has not reached Zat Arras. A cruiser awaits above at your own dock; let us leave at—”

A fusillade of shots cut him off—sharp cracks from the palace gardens, a sudden tearing of the night’s fabric. Sound, again, as ruler: how quickly violence announces itself by noise, how quickly peace is measured by the return of natural quiet.

We rushed to the balcony in time to see a dozen of my palace guard vanish into shrub-shadow, pursuing a fleeing figure. Below, on the scarlet sward, a handful of guards bent over a still, prostrate form.

At my command they bore the body into the audience chamber and laid it at our feet. It was a red man in the prime of life. His metal was plain—the harness of a common soldier, or of one who wished his name and rank to taste like nothing at all.

“Another of Zat Arras’ spies,” said Hor Vastus.

“So it would seem,” I replied, and turned to the guard. “You may remove the body.”

“Wait,” said Xodar. His thin face sharpened with intent, as though he were about to dissect a fish for hidden bones. “If you will, Prince, ask that a cloth and a little thoat oil be brought.”

Two hours after we slipped away from my palace in Helium—near midnight by the soft blue clocks of Barsoom—Kantos Kan, Xodar, and I set down at Hastor. Carthoris and Tars Tarkas, with Hor Vastus, had gone by another cruiser straight for Thark to bring the green hordes to heel and harness.

At Hastor everything stood ready as if laid out on a household board: docks cleared, crews fed, ropes coiled with the neatness of a cook’s twine, every bulkhead lantern hooded so its glow would not spill. Kantos Kan had planned with a meticulousness that reminded me of a master of kitchens who can bring a hundred dishes to the pass at once—each timed, each seasoned, each served without waste. Within ten minutes of our arrival the first transport rose from its cradle, and then, as evenly as a cook’s knife falls in practiced chopping, ship after ship lifted—one a second—until a long, thin procession streamed southward through the night like a ribbon laid across black velvet.

In the cabin—close, warm with the smell of oiled metal and the faint sharpness of ozone from the buoyancy rays—I asked at last what date it was. Until then my mind had been too full of the taste of old darkness: the stale, dry breath of the pits of Zat Arras, where time had been nothing but thirst and heartbeat and the hiss of unseen things in stone.

When Kantos Kan told me, the number struck like grit between the teeth. Three hundred and sixty-five days. I had misreckoned. In the pit, days had not been measured by dawn wind through a ventilator or the changing chorus of the city—only by silence and pain. The thought came with a sick pang: too late. Too late for Dejah Thoris.

It would have been easy to say it aloud, to make my grief a shared ration at the common mess, but what would that have fed them? They had carried my sorrows enough in other years. I held my tongue. If rescue had slipped beyond reach, vengeance and revelation still remained; and there was a broader hunger than mine that this expedition could satisfy: the hunger of Barsoom to remember the truth.

For ages the red men had walked willingly into the maw of Dor, lured by a lexicon of holiness that was itself a trap. Words—Issus, Life Eternal, pilgrimage—had been used like knives, sharp and polished by repetition, until the very sound of them could make a man lay down his life with a smile. If we could break that power, if we could make the old phrases crack and fall and be replaced by plain speech, we might save thousands each year from the fate hidden behind the thern temples’ perfumed myths. Beyond superstition’s wall lay the Valley Dor—land that drank dew and rain without the ache of irrigation; an open sea; soil that would bear without pleading. Yet it had been kept as a private pantry for monsters and for the decayed remnants of races that fed on fear.

Toward dawn of the second day the battleships overtook the transports. The first flood of light showed hulls lined like silver fish above the ice cap, and the sky was alive with sound—not the grinding industrial roar of Earth’s wars, but the varied living noises of Barsoomian flight: the deep thrumming of the great cruisers, the lighter whirr of one-man scouts, the rhythmic creak of decking under strain. Even a fleet has an ecology of sound; and there is sanity in a noise that is purposeful and clean, in tones that speak of wind and muscle and balanced machines. We hugged the surface of the ice, keeping low to avoid the therns’ watchers. Ahead, a thin line of scouts skimmed like swallows. On either flank others ranged, and a smaller tail watched the rear twenty miles behind.

We had been moving thus for several hours when a scout from the front returned to report the cone-like summit of the entrance to Omean in sight. Almost at the same instant another flier—left flank—came tearing in, its engine singing urgency before we could even see the pilot’s face. He landed with a bounce and was up the stair as though his boots were on fire.

“A great fleet of battleships south-south-east, my Prince,” he cried. “Several thousands. Bearing down directly upon us.”

“The thern spies were not in the palace of John Carter for nothing,” Kantos Kan said low, his eyes already calculating distances, angles, the way a careful steward measures grain when famine threatens. “Your orders, Prince.”

The plan formed in me not as an abstract stratagem but as a sequence of motions—like courses in a meal that must arrive in the right order or all is spoiled.

“Send ten battleships to guard the entrance to Omean,” I said. “Let none enter or leave the shaft. Bottle the First Born.

“Form the rest into a great V, apex toward south-south-east. The transports with their convoys will follow in the wake until our point pierces the enemy’s line. Then the V opens—each leg drives him back and cuts us a lane. Through that lane the transports race at top speed to the temples and gardens. There they land and teach the Holy Therns what it is to be hunted, not worshipped.

“I would not have been diverted,” I added, tasting bitterness as if it were smoke in my throat, “but we must settle this with the therns now, or there will be no peace while we are near Dor.”

Kantos Kan saluted and turned to his aides. Orders flew, crisp as snapped parchment. The formation shifted with startling grace; ten ships peeled away toward Omean, and the troopships drew in close like herd beasts seeking shelter under a hunter’s wing.

Full speed was called. The whole fleet sprang forward like coursing greyhounds. The enemy line came into view—a ragged band stretching farther than sight in either direction, three ships deep, complacent in their belief that Dor’s terrors guarded them better than any sentry.

Our onslaught hit like a cleaver through soft fruit. We drove straight through their line before they could properly turn. Then, as planned, the V opened at the apex and a broad lane yawned in their midst. Through it the transports leaped, low and fast, toward the glittering temples that now flashed in sunlight like polished bone.

By the time the therns rallied, a hundred thousand green warriors had already spilled into their courts and gardens, trampling ornamented paths and sacred pools under naked feet, their harsh battle-songs tearing the air. A hundred and fifty thousand more leaned from low-swinging troopships, loosing their long rifles with that uncanny precision that seems less learned than inherited—the way an elder knows seed-time by smell, or the way an old dialect holds a thing’s true name while a conqueror’s language can only approximate it.

Above, the great fleets locked in. The din was tremendous, yet in it I could still discern layers: the heavy concussion of shell, the sharp crack of small arms, the rising howl of buoyancy engines straining under sudden turns. When sound is corrupted—when it becomes only a wall of brutal noise—it makes men stupid and cruel. But here, even in slaughter, the sky’s voices remained legible: warning, command, pursuit, retreat.

Kantos Kan brought Helium’s battleships into the circling formation of Barsoomian naval war. Ends joined. The circle tightened. Around and around we went, high speed, hard to hit, broadside after broadside delivered as each ship swung into place. The therns tried to rush and break us, but it was like throwing bare hands into a saw.

Slowly we shifted the circle until we hung above the gardens, where green warriors still fought among flowering vines and perfumed groves—perfumes now tainted by scorched powder and the iron scent of blood. The order went down for our men to embark. Transports rose within the circle like baskets drawn up from a well.

The therns’ fire faltered. They had had their lesson and were glad to see us go. Yet our way out was not to be easy.

To the north, far off, a great black line crested the horizon—a fleet. Whose, we could not guess until Kantos Kan’s operator caught a radio-aerogram and handed it up. Kantos Kan read it, his jaw tightening, and gave it to me.

“Kantos Kan: Surrender, in the name of the Jeddak of Helium, for you cannot escape.” Signed: “Zat Arras.”

So. The traitor’s shadow had found us in the open sky.

The therns must have intercepted the same message, for the moment they understood we were about to be harried from another side, their fire renewed with spiteful vigor. Before Zat Arras even came within range, we were again hotly engaged. When his fleet drew near he poured heavy shot into us; ship after ship reeled and staggered, engines screaming under strain. The air grew thick with the sour stink of discharged gases and the bitter tang of burned insulation.

This could not last. I ordered the transports down again into the gardens.

“Wreak your vengeance to the utmost,” I sent to the green allies. “By night there will be none left to avenge your wrongs.”

Then I saw the ten battleships I had sent to hold the shaft—returning at full speed, stern batteries flashing almost continuously. They were running. Behind them, like wolves behind wounded game, another fleet poured into sight.

For a heartbeat I refused what my eyes told me. Then the truth settled in my belly, cold and heavy: it was the fleet of the First Born—supposed to be bottled in Omean—free, and coming.

Misfortune after misfortune, as though a hand kept turning the same bitter spice into every dish I attempted to serve. My mind leaped, superstitious for an instant, to the thought of a curse, as though Issus were not a fraud of flesh and deceit but a true malignant power. I rejected it with a physical motion—shoulders back, lungs filling—like a man who will not choke on smoke.

A thern craft grappled us broadside. I ran down to the lower deck where my men struggled hand to hand. There, amid the press and the metallic stench, hope returned in its old, reckless form. Steel in my hand was always more honest than prophecy. Thern after thern went down beneath my blade, and the men, seeing me among them, took heart like a fire taking dry tinder. Within moments we had swept the board and surged onto the enemy’s deck. Their commander leaped from the bows in token of surrender and defeat.

When I returned above, Kantos Kan’s eyes were bright with a sudden idea. He gave an order. A moment later my colors broke from every point of the flagship. A cheer rolled along our decks, caught by the next ship, and the next, until the whole expedition flew the banner of the Prince of Helium as though it were a single great throat crying the same name.

Then Kantos Kan raised a signal visible to all the combatants—thern, First Born, and Heliumetic alike.

“Men of Helium for the Prince of Helium against all his enemies.”

It was a phrase meant to cut through confusion like a clear call through a forest—simple, native, unmisunderstandable. Not the ornate, commercialized cant of priesthood, not a slogan polished for sales, but a direct naming of allegiance that no true Heliumetic ear could mistake. In war, language is weapon and home both.

Presently my colors broke from one of Zat Arras’ ships. Then another. Then another. On some we could see fierce fighting between Zodangan soldiery and Heliumetic crews; but ship by ship, the banners of Helium’s Prince rose. Only Zat Arras’ flagship refused.

He had brought five thousand ships. Now the sky was black with three enormous fleets, and the fight dissolved into countless individual duels. There could be no grand maneuvering in that crowded, fire-split air.

Zat Arras’ flagship closed with ours until I could see his thin features across the gap, pale and hard as dried meat. His Zodangan gunners hammered us and we answered in kind. Closer and closer, until barely yards remained. Boarders lined the rails, grapplers ready.

As the first irons flew I forced my way through my men and was the first to spring to the deck of Zat Arras’ ship. After me poured Helium’s best—yelling, cheering, cursing—men who had eaten the same bread and spoken the same city-streets’ dialect since boyhood, and whose loyalty was deeper than any order signed by a usurper. The Zodangans broke beneath that surge. While my men cleared below, I sprang forward where Zat Arras stood.

“You are my prisoner, Zat Arras,” I cried. “Yield and you shall have quarter.”

For an instant he hesitated—whether to draw on me or surrender. Then he threw down his arms and ran to the far rail. Before I could reach him he flung himself headlong into the depths.

So ended Zat Arras, Jed of Zodanga.

The battle raged on, strange now in its pattern: therns and First Born had not combined against us. Wherever a thern ship met a black ship, they fought like hereditary foes. In that rivalry I saw our narrow chance.

Wherever we could pass messages that would not be intercepted, I ordered withdrawal—west and south, away from the main struggle. I sent a scout down to the green men in the gardens to re-embark, and orders to the transports to join us. I instructed my commanders: when engaged, draw the enemy toward his ancient foeman, force those two to clash, and slip away. It worked like a cook’s trick in a crowded kitchen—shift the heat, redirect the hands, and suddenly the path to the pantry is clear.

Just before the sun went down, what remained of my fleet gathered nearly twenty miles southwest of the still-terrific battle between blacks and whites.

Now I divided our force. I transferred Xodar to another battleship and sent him with all the transports and five thousand battleships directly overhead to the Temple of Issus. Carthoris and I, with Kantos Kan, took the remaining ships and headed for the entrance to Omean.

At dawn, we would attempt the assault together: Tars Tarkas with his green warriors and Hor Vastus with the red men, guided by Xodar, would land in the gardens of Issus or on the surrounding plains. Carthoris, Kantos Kan, and I would lead our smaller force from the Sea of Omean through the pits beneath the temple—ways Carthoris knew as intimately as a man knows the layout of his own hearth.

I learned then the cause of the ten ships’ retreat from the shaft. When they reached it, the navy of the First Born was already issuing from the mouth—twenty vessels out and more rising behind. My ten fought to stem the tide, but the odds were too great; they fled.

Under cover of darkness we approached. Several miles out I halted the fleet and sent Carthoris ahead alone in a one-man flier to reconnoiter. He returned in half an hour: no sign of patrol or enemy.

We moved forward again—swiftly, noiselessly. At the mouth of the shaft we paused only long enough for each vessel to find its appointed station. Then, with the flagship, I dropped into the black depths, and one by one the others followed.

We staked all on boldness. We left no guard at the shaft mouth; we lacked the force to hold it against the First Born if they returned in numbers. Our safety depended on the enemy’s assumption that the ships entering were their own.

It proved true. Four hundred of my five hundred were safely resting upon the bosom of Omean before the first shot was fired. The battle was short and hot; the guard was only a handful of ancient hulks left through carelessness born of complacency. They were obsolete, their crews inattentive, as though even war had become to them a ritual performed without attention—like a recipe repeated from a dead language, the meaning gone, the motions all that remained.

At Carthoris’ suggestion we landed prisoners under guard upon two larger islands. Then we towed captured First Born ships to the shaft and wedged them in the interior of the great well. The rest we set with buoyancy rays and let them rise to jam themselves against the blockage, a crude but effective cork.

Now we believed we had time—at least some—before the returning black fleet could reach the Sea of Omean. I hastened with a strong force to the island of the submarine and took it without resistance from its small guard. The craft lay in its pool like a sleek, sleeping beast. I set men to watch it and the island and remained there to await Carthoris and the others.

Among the prisoners was Yersted, commander of the submarine. He recognized me from the three grim journeys I had made aboard his craft during my captivity.

“How does it seem,” I asked him, “to be captive now—prisoner of your erstwhile captive?”

He smiled—grim, as though he savored some bitter morsel held between tongue and teeth.

“It will not be for long, John Carter,” he said. “We have been expecting you and we are prepared.”

“So it would appear,” I answered, “for you were ready enough to become my prisoners with scarce a blow.”

“The fleet must have missed you,” he said. “But it will return to Omean, and then that will be a very different matter—for John Carter.”

“I do not know that the fleet has missed me as yet,” I said, but he only frowned, not grasping my meaning.

“Many prisoners travel to Issus in your grim craft, Yersted?” I asked.

“Very many,” he replied.

“Might you remember one whom men called Dejah Thoris?”

“Well, indeed,” he said, and his voice changed slightly, as though even his harsh mind could not deny beauty when it sat before him. “For her great beauty—and for the fact that she was wife to the first mortal who ever escaped from Issus through all the countless ages of her godhood. And Issus remembers her best as the wife of one and the mother of another who raised their hands against the Goddess of Life Eternal.”

A cold shiver ran through me at the thought of what petty revenge Issus might have taken upon the innocent for the acts of husband and son.

“And where is Dejah Thoris now?” I asked, though dread already salted my tongue.

“Yesterday the monthly rites of Issus were held,” Yersted said, “and I saw her then, sitting in her accustomed place at the foot of Issus.”

“What!” I cried. “She is not dead, then?”

“Why, no,” he answered. “It has been no year since she gazed upon the divine glory of the radiant face of—”

“No year?” I broke in.

“Why, no,” he insisted. “It cannot have been upward of three hundred and seventy or eighty days.”

Light burst in me, sudden and almost painful. I had been stupid—Earth-stupid—measuring Barsoom with Earth’s calendar as though time itself spoke only one tongue. Martian days are longer; Martian years are six hundred and eighty-seven days. The ten Earth years I had lived here were but five years and ninety-six days by Barsoomian reckoning.

I am in time. I am in time.

The words surged through my mind until they must have escaped aloud, for Yersted shook his head slowly.

“In time to save your Princess?” he said. He did not wait for my answer. “No, John Carter. Issus will not give up her own. She knows you are coming. Ere ever a vandal foot is set within the precincts of the Temple of Issus—if such a calamity should befall—Dejah Thoris will be put away forever from the last faint hope of rescue.”

“You mean she will be killed merely to thwart me?” I demanded.

“Not that—other than as a last resort,” he said. “Hast ever heard of the Temple of the Sun? It is there they will put her. It lies far within the inner court of the Temple of Issus—a little temple whose thin spire rises above the surrounding spires and minarets. Beneath it, in the ground, lies the main body: six hundred and eighty-seven circular chambers, one below another. To each chamber a single corridor leads through solid rock from the pits of Issus.

“As the entire Temple of the Sun revolves once with each revolution of Barsoom about the sun, but once each year does the entrance to each separate chamber come opposite the mouth of the corridor which forms its only link to the world without.”

Yersted’s whispered knowledge left no room for the luxuries of debate or the slow rituals of council. Dawn would bring Tars Tarkas and the full, furious weight of an assault; I had to be inside the Temple of Issus before that first pale spill of light, as quietly as a knife sliding between ribs. From within, I told myself, I could take my Princess from those perfumed walls—take her and run—because at my back, at last, stood the fighting strength of Helium in a single gathered body.

When Carthoris came to me with the others, we began at once—trip after trip—ferrying our men through the submerged passage, moving them like grain in careful measures through a narrow mill. The water swallowed sound in a thick, muffling way; only the small, intimate noises remained—the scrape of harness, the softened click of metal against stone, the controlled breathing of thousands trying to become one silent creature. Five thousand we brought through, seasoned warriors all, the hard red men who could read a battlefield the way an elder reads a field’s soil by rubbing it between finger and thumb.

Carthoris alone knew the hidden ways. We could not split and strike the temple at multiple points, though every instinct urged it; instead, we would be a single thrust, aimed at the heart. He was about to lead us from the pool chamber into the corridor when an officer touched my arm and pointed down at the black surface where the submarine floated.

At first I mistook the agitation for pursuit—a great body rising beneath us, another boat coming up like a predatory fish. But the motion had a different character: the water itself was lifting, steady as dough swelling in a warm bowl. It rose without drama, without hurry, yet with terrible certainty. It kissed the edge of stone, crept higher, and the pool began to lose its boundaries.

For a breath I did not comprehend. Then Carthoris’s face changed, as if he’d tasted something rancid.
